#10597. Material 3 Expressive adds scrolling time picker, AI Studio upgrades Android app creation

Google has given the time picker component a Material 3 Expressive redesign, while adding a new scrolling variant. Meanwhile, Google AI Studio is boosting its Android app development experience.

Compared to Material 3, the updated M3 Expressive input and dial time pickers drop the “Select time” hint in the top-right corner, while the AM and PM buttons now benefit from expressive shape, color, and behavior. The hour and minute fields, as well as the dial selector, feature higher contrast.

The big addition today is the scroll time picker. Instead of using the keyboard or analog clock to pick the time, you can just vertically scroll when setting an alarm or scheduling an event.

Nooo, the scrolling variant is what I don't like about iOS — it takes forever to dial anything in it! Why would Google copy it?

The Material Design team says this “compact way to select time” is “best placed inline with other content.” A keyboard icon lets you switch to the input time picker.

Use scroll time pickers instead of dial pickers when you want people to see surrounding content.

Google today also introduced scroll fields to select different units of measurement, like distance, weight, or temperature. In a health app, they can be used for water, calories, and more.

#10596. Silicon Valley’s reaction to the Nvidia drama is rife with blind spots

Hello again, and welcome back to Fast Company’s Plugged In.

Jensen Huang was scheduled to speak at this week’s Semafor Silicon Valley & The World conference, which he cochaired. For reasons too obvious to require explanation, he didn’t make it to the event. And yet he dominated the on- and off-stage conversation so resoundingly that it might as well have been titled “Silicon Valley, The World & Jensen Huang.”

As tech notables sat for interviews—among them Sam Altman, Clay Bavor, Jeff Bezos, Andy Jassy, Yann LeCun, Fei-Fei Li, and David Sacks—moderators kept requesting their take on Huang’s shocking ouster from Nvidia. Most lavished praise on his entrepreneurship, but begged off saying much more on the grounds that the circumstances remained murky. Bezos’s comments were typical: “I don’t know enough of the details and I think probably most of us don’t know. . . . What I can tell you for sure is that Jensen is a remarkable guy who founded and built a remarkable company.”

Speakers at Semafor’s event approached the subject more gingerly than many in the scrum on X where Huang declared on Tuesday that “if the Board of Directors decides that my return is in the best interest of our shareholders, our employees, the company, and America, I would be honored to answer these calls and return as CEO.” Skimming the 58,000 replies to his post, I didn’t see much discussion of the specifics of the case: Huang’s confessing to an extramarital affair with an Nvidia business partner who was allegedly also a spy for China, the questions that raised relating to Nvidia chips possibly being smuggled into China, and reports that he had two additional affairs with Nvidia employees.

Instead, Huang’s X followers mostly had his back. Tech industry celebs such as Aaron Levie, CEO of Box; Palmer Luckey, cofounder of Anduril; and Linda Yaccarino, former CEO of X, shared a few words (or emojis) of support. Others were civilians who expressed their enthusiasm for Huang’s theoretical rehiring in the form of truly weird AI slop: Jensen as Jesus at the Last Supper, Jensen popping out of a casket, Jensen lip-syncing Michael Jordan’s comments upon his return to the Chicago Bulls.

Despite reports of disagreement among Nvidia’s board members, the company continues to rule out Huang’s return. Given the unfolding legal and geopolitical elements of the story, a speedy Sam Altman-style reinstatement would appear to be fantasy in even the rosiest of scenarios.

The revelations thus far have already prompted the Trump administration to tighten restrictions on chip exports to China. Huang has agreed to testify before the Senate Banking Committee on December 1. Democrats, who will regain control of the Senate and House of Representatives in January, are salivating at the opportunity to dig into questions such as whether Commerce Secretary Howard Lutnick facilitated the diversion of Nvidia chips to China. And California’s attorney general has subpoenaed Nvidia in search of any evidence that Huang’s affair led to chips or confidential information making their way to China.

Bottom line: Everyone who says we don’t know enough to come to a firm conclusion about the precise nature of what happened and its appropriate repercussions is on far firmer ground than those who think Huang was unfairly forced out and should be welcomed back.

But for all the miasma of uncertainty clouding this drama, it’s already told us plenty about Silicon Valley. Its most admired, envied, seemingly essential CEO being shown the door, even as the company he cofounded is still booming, is a deeply unsettling black swan event, and I haven’t seen many in the industry unblinkingly confront its implications. It’s surely not a coincidence that Gina Raimondo, the former U.S. commerce secretary—who is a technocrat, but not a creature of the Valley—had the bluntest assessment of the situation I heard at the Semafor conference: “Any kind of corruption, politics, dealing with spies, [there should be] no tolerance for it.”

Paul Graham, cofounder of Y Combinator, coined the term “founder mode” in 2024. It celebrates startup founders who refuse to cede control to professional managers, regardless of how big their brainchildren become. Decades before the concept had a name, Huang exemplified it. Instead of growing more remote from the minutiae of the company’s operations as it became a behemoth, he had his hands in everything—which is how he wound up with a reported 60 executives answering directly to him.

Over Huang’s 33 years as Nvidia’s CEO, he took risks that no MBA-brandishing suit would have dared. None was gutsier than his 2006 decision to pivot from focusing on 3D graphics to building a high-performance general-purpose computing platform. That prescience paid off when artificial intelligence became an epoch-shifting opportunity, leading to the company’s 90% market share in the chips that power generative AI.

In recent years, as Nvidia has jockeyed for the title of the world’s most valuable company, Huang has been no less daring. Whatever you think of the AI industry’s “circular financing,” in which many players take financial stakes in their own customers, Nvidia’s willingness to write huge checks to companies in its own ecosystem has been critical to AI’s growth. And as expectations for the technology prop up the stock market, it’s not just the tech business that might look very different without Huang’s high-stakes gambling. So might the entire U.S. economy.

Huang the public figure has become as unique as Huang the fearless entrepreneur. Once an unassuming engineer in an Nvidia shirt who went by his birth name of Jen-Hsun Huang, he reinvented himself into the genially bombastic, leather jacket-wearing rock star I interviewed in 2024. Only Altman rivaled his omnipresence as a speaker at tech industry events. Huang, who relished engaging in stunts such as frolicking with robots onstage, was a lot more fun. I can understand why people who never met him take his downfall personally.

If you can’t envision a tech industry without Huang at its core, you have every incentive to wish for any possible outcome that would reinstate him to his previous eminence. But Huang himself took risks that made a happy ending far less likely.

All evidence indicates that his initial strategy for dealing with the mess he created involved misleading Nvidia’s management and board, not to mention members of the press. Huang’s undisclosed relationships seem to have violated Nvidia’s own code of conduct. I have seen many people argue that they should still be forgiven. But to my knowledge, nobody has explicitly maintained that a CEO lying to his board is tolerable, at least if that CEO is Jensen Huang. Maybe that’s because the case is impossible to make.

Another bad bet Huang made was his tight relationship with President Trump, which he was flaunting as recently as September. It apparently influenced the president to dismiss an October 2025 intelligence briefing on Huang’s relationship with Megaspeed’s Alice (no relation) Huang. Trump may not have been doing Huang any favors, given that the story blew up in both of their faces at the worst conceivable time, after the Democrats’ wins in the midterm elections.

As Daring Fireball’s John Gruber noted this week, tech executives have historically steered clear of hugging any political party so close that it would complicate their relationships in Washington, D.C., should the balance of power shift. Huang abandoned this approach during Trump’s second term. So did other executives, but only Elon Musk may suffer graver consequences.

Huang’s unplanned departure also left Nvidia scrambling to deal with a fundamental bug in the logic of founder’s mode. Any company that’s lucky enough to matter for decades will, at some point, be run by a nonfounder—maybe even a dreaded professional manager. Responsible founders, no matter how hands on, should prepare for that day. Many do not.

Along with everyone else in the Valley, Huang apparently couldn’t picture a post-Jensen Nvidia. I hope he was kidding when he said he’d like to run the company for another 30 or 40 years and then be replaced by a robotic version of himself. But his failure to establish any clarity around a succession plan is a deadly serious problem for the company he founded. (His daughter, son, and son-in-law all work at the company, but not in roles that leave any of them as an heir apparent.)

Nobody else in tech ever had a career like Jensen Huang. His present quandary is part of that story, but I don’t expect it to be his only legacy. He might even rebound in some manner that doesn’t involve him getting his old gig back. Assuming the mystery surrounding this chapter eventually subsides, I hope the facts of the matter leave the industry wiser—if sadder—about aspects of its culture that it usually does its best to ignore.

You’ve been reading Plugged In, Fast Company’s weekly tech newsletter from me, global technology editor Harry McCracken. If a friend or colleague forwarded this edition to you—or if you’re reading it on fastcompany.com—you can check out previous issues and sign up to get it yourself every Friday morning. I’m also on Bluesky, Mastodon, and Threads, and you can follow Plugged In on Flipboard.

#10594. Google Home speakers get a major Gemini upgrade ‘soon,’ but only for paid subscribers

Alongside the huge list of new updates Google detailed yesterday, the company also announced that a “faster and more natural” version of Gemini Live is coming to speakers and smart displays “soon.”

Google explains this is from Gemini Live moving to the “latest speech-to-speech architecture” and brings “significantly faster response times.” Gemini Live isn’t the default experience when you start a “Hey Google” command, rather hiding behind a “let’s chat” or other similar command to start a session.

Initially, Live sessions were purely for brainstorming or longer conversations and lacking support for commands and other functions mid-session, but Google now says that this upgrade will add support for actions such as smart home controls, setting timers, and more without leaving a Live session. That’s a big deal.

Coming soon – A faster and more natural Gemini Live experience: Available with a Google Home Premium subscription, Gemini Live is being upgraded to our latest speech-to-speech architecture for significantly faster response times and a smoother experience. Just say, “Hey Google, let’s chat” to brainstorm dinner ideas or plan a trip out loud, interrupt naturally to change topics, and perform actions mid-conversation—such as, setting a timer, adding a reminder,or controlling a smart home device—without stepping out of the chat³.

The “catch” is that you’ll still need a subscription to use this. Gemini Live has always required a subscription on Google Home speakers and smart displays, with the functionality looped into Google Home Premium plans which start at $10/month (or bundled into Google One AI Pro). That’s the same subscription that gives you Nest storage.

#10593. Workers are willing to give up higher pay for one thing, new global survey finds

Time really is money to many workers.

A better salary is no longer the top reason why workers will consider leaving their job in the next year—heck, it didn’t even crack the top three. For the first time since Adecco launched an annual report on the changing world of work, global workers spanning a wide range of industries are making clear that their time is more valuable.

A better work-life balance topped the reasons why workers might quit their current job, according to a survey of more than 40,000 workers in 36 countries conducted by Adecco, a Zurich-based recruitment and consulting agency. But the second and third reasons why workers might switch jobs—seeking out more flexibility and the opportunity to work remotely—also speak to how people are reevaluating how work fits into their lives, and not just the money it provides, according to the report.

At a time when workers are increasingly being asked to operate alongside AI agents or even robots, as the report found, they seemingly want to retain some of their humanity. And organizations that don’t want to see a mass exodus of humans should take note, according to Denis Machuel, CEO of Adecco.

“We cannot simply ask people to keep pace with technology,” Machuel said in a foreword to the report. “We need to build trust and confidence, to adapt, to give people the freedom to learn and experiment, and protect the flexibility and balance they value.”

The survey also highlighted that across seven key indicators, employees generally reported being somewhat less satisfied this year than last. But the biggest decreases were related to flexibility regarding both their working schedule and working location. 

That’s why the report’s findings also speak to the ongoing tension surrounding stricter return-to-office policies, as Machuel told The Guardian.

“Every company that wants to bring people back to the office struggles with actually implementing these measures to their full extent,” he told the outlet. As for companies with a strict policy of five days per week? “It doesn’t work,” he added.

WORKERS WANT AI

As artificial intelligence continues to reshape how people work, employers play a key role in how that plays out. The survey found that when AI tools are well integrated into everyday workflows, workers save time for higher-value work—and they feel less overwhelmed by the technology as a result.

Nearly three-quarters of workers surveyed use AI agents at work, while 14% work alongside robots, the survey found. These “hybrid workers,” as Adecco dubbed them, report feeling far and away more empowered to shape their work going forward. Compared with their non-hybrid counterparts, these hybrid workers were overwhelmingly more likely to say they’re able to perform at their highest level, that their sense of autonomy over their work has increased in the past year, and that they proactively experiment with different ways of using AI at work.

Once again, employers should take note, Machuel says.

“When workers help redesign how work gets done, and receive the training and clarity to adapt, AI creates more value, and change becomes easier to navigate,” he said in a statement. “AI delivers its greatest impact when it happens with people and not to people.”

#10592. Three 'e-waste' Nvidia V100s hit 100 tokens per second on Qwen 3.6 35B in a $1,042 homelab rig

An M5 Max Mac Studio is slightly faster at short context, but the V100s pull ahead at 64K and longer.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

A homelab builder benchmarked three used Nvidia V100s on Qwen 3.6 35B, achieving about 100 tokens per second in a custom setup costing $1,042 with just “e-waste” GPUs. The smaller, dense Qwen 3.6 27B manages about 24 tokens per second on the same platform, and the V100s use about half the energy of the same number of P100s for a given job.

The blog post, “Scaling ‘E-Waste’: LLMs on Multiple Decommissioned GPUs,” compares nine different builds — one to three K80s, P100s, or V100-16GB cards — on a single Supermicro dual-Xeon board. The test uses llama.cpp and covers more than 130 LLM tests, including Qwen 2.5, Gemma 4, and, of course, Qwen 3.6, at Q4/Q8/F16 precisions and from 4K to 128K context sizes. GPU energy per run is also recorded. The builder is “daily driving Qwen 3.6 35B” on the V100s and notes that scaling decommissioned GPUs will only get you so far.

“E-waste,” in this case, refers to decommissioned enterprise GPUs, which are a popular but somewhat underpowered option when compared to very expensive modern alternatives. The Nvidia Tesla V100s in question are tested as a follow-up to a July round which tested 15 “e-waste” GPUs, and this time the board and CPUs stay the same for every build — the Supermicro X10DRG-Q with two Intel Xeon E5-2687W 12-core CPUs, powered by the Corsair HX1500i PSU.

Performance is judged based on what is considered “realtime interaction” which, here, is 70 tokens per second. As to why the number of GPUs is three: Qwen 3.6 35B, the primary tested model, fails on just one or two at Q8, or 8-bit quantization.

Here are the given prices, with the note that they aren’t “100% reflective of what you could find on eBay today,” although the totals count only one of the two CPUs and omit RAM, storage, the case, and any cooling.

The older cards are relatively inexpensive because they “run hot and ship without cooling,” which required the builder to 3D print one blower duct per card and has led to the development of a standalone cooler.

The bench itself involves 10 runs of 512 generated tokens per test with context pre-filled to 4K, 32K, 64K, or 128K. Qwen 3.6 35B is a mixture-of-experts (MoE) model, which only activates about 3 billion out of its 35 billion total parameters per token. The numbers that immediately follow are at Q8.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

The token goal was easily reached…when the context window was small, or 4K. The 100.8 tokens per second there fell to 72.7 at 128K, still usable by the 70 tokens per second goal. The 27B model, which is not MoE, fared much worse, at 24.3 at 4K, 21.6 at 32K, 18.7 at 64K, and just 14.2 at 128K. The MoE ends up about four to five times faster, depending on the context size.

Also tested were other dense models of about 30B parameters. At 4K, Gemma 4 31B achieved 20.9 and Qwen 2.5 32B 21.0, while the MoE Gemma 4 26B cleared the 70 tokens per second bar with just two V100s at Q8. If you move down to Q4, or 4-bit quantization, two V100s can run Qwen 3.6 35B at 77.1 to 105.4 tokens per second, between 128K and 4K context size. This is where it’s claimed that adding more cards gives you more memory, which enables larger models to be run, but not speed.

The P100s, for comparison, only hit 40.2–55.2 tokens per second in the same context range with Q8 Qwen 3.6 35B, and the target 70 only with Qwen 2.5 3B. If we compare to more modern hardware — the Nvidia DGX Spark, MSRP $4,700, and the M5 Max Mac Studio, which starts at $2,499 but costs $5,399 with the tested 40-core GPU, 128GB of memory, and a 1TB SSD — the 3x V100 build holds its own, using sourced data.

The Mac comparison comes from the oMLX benchmark page for the M5 Max with 40 GPU cores and 128GB of unified memory, and that hits 43.9 tokens per second with a ~195K-token prompt on the Qwen 3.6 35B model. The Mac is slightly ahead at 4K and 32K, while the V100s pull ahead at 64K and 128K, by 19% at the longest context. These numbers have to be taken loosely, as the machines use different runtimes.

The Spark run came from Kleybrink’s dgx-spark-bench, using Ollama in thinking mode with short prompts. Its 45.8 tokens per second is less than half the V100s’ 4K figure.

Going further to modern GPUs, which “destroy our decommissioned equivalents,” as the builder puts it, you get about 315 tokens per second on two H100s and about 455 on one B200 at 128K, the B200 roughly six times the V100 box, even though the hosted cards ran the full 16-bit model against the V100s’ 8-bit. Parasail, an inference provider, sponsored that comparison with dedicated H100 and B200 deployments.

As for energy, the V100 setup came out to about 0.02 kWh for a 128K 35B run, with the P100s taking twice that much and two or three K80s as much as 0.35 to 0.45 kWh. The V100-16GB simply “[puts] up better scores while consuming less energy,” in comparison to the P100s. These numbers are for the GPUs and not from the wall.

There’s more to come. The builder wants a benchmark that “maps to ‘software factory’ performance,” a term for models being prompted constantly, around the clock, where instant speed matters less. Then there’s the cooler, which is “almost ready for release,” with beta testers to be recruited from the mailing list, although no date has been given. From the Reddit thread, the builder mentions the intention to compare inference backends and use speculative decoding to improve performance.

#10590. Samsung releases its next major update, One UI 9.5, in beta for Galaxy S26 – what’s new

Samsung is officially launching its One UI 9.5 beta program today on Galaxy S26 series devices, and it’s packing some features we didn’t expect.

Ahead of today’s launch, there have been a lot of leaks around Samsung’s next major update. But, as it turns out, there are some features here we didn’t quite know about.

Samsung details its key new features including:

One of the biggest features here is App Lock, which has been overdue for a launch on Galaxy devices. Google added the feature in Android 17 QPR2 for Pixel phones, and it’s been very common on other Android phones, so Samsung users are finally getting in on the functionality. App Lock lets you hide an app behind added biometric/PIN unlock.

One UI 9.5 is available in beta starting today on the Galaxy S26 series in the US, UK, Germany, India, Korea, and Poland, through the Samsung Members app as per usual. A stable release is expected early next year, likely starting on the Galaxy S27 series.

#10588. AI stock sell-off accelerates as markets head for steep weekly losses

Wall Street’s losses mounted Friday, pulling the S&P 500 10% below its record high set last month, as the sell-off for AI stocks accelerated and the market headed for a sharp weekly loss.

The S&P 500 index was down about 2%. If it closes down more than 10% from its October 6 high, it will have entered what professional investors call a “correction.” The benchmark index is on track for a seventh straight day of losses, which would mark the longest such streak since the onset of the COVID-19 pandemic in 2020.

The Nasdaq composite, which is more greatly influenced by tech stocks, slumped 3.5%. The Dow Jones Industrial Average, which is much less exposed to big technology stocks, fell 289 points, or 0.6%, as of 12:20 p.m. ET.

More stocks were gaining ground within the S&P 500, but AI stocks with hefty market values have a much bigger impact on the market’s direction. They were responsible for Wall Street’s big gains this year and are now behind the big slide for the market.

AI stocks have been losing ground partly over worries that their big market values and heavy spending are unjustified. Fears of a bubble within the sector have also run up against the recent midterm elections, where there has been a shift in power after the GOP, seen as more friendly to the industry, suffered an electoral sweep.

The big market debut for Anthropic, maker of the Claude AI tool, fizzled on Friday with the rest of the tech sector. The stock fell 12.4% to $85.16. It is still above its IPO price of $83 per share. It was the biggest public stock offering ever, at one point surpassing $2 trillion in value.

Market debuts for tech companies are often followed by a volatile period. SpaceX, which previously held the title for the biggest initial public offering (IPO), rose nearly 50% in its first three days, but fell below its IPO price a month later.

Chip stocks, big beneficiaries of the buildout of AI data centers, led the way lower. Nvidia is falling for the ninth day in the last 10, down 7.4%. The chipmaker has been under more pressure over worries about its future following the sudden downfall of Jensen Huang, its former CEO and cofounder.

Advanced Micro Devices dropped 11.7% and Broadcom fell 11.5%. The PHLX Semiconductor Index sank 9%.

Outside of tech, though, most stocks were gaining ground. Cheerios maker General Mills jumped 4.8% and homebuilder Lennar rose 3.4%.

Treasury yields fell in the bond market. The yield on the 10-year Treasury fell to 4.93% from 5.04% late Thursday.

Brent crude oil, the international standard, fell 0.3% to $91.69 per barrel, despite continued tension near the Strait of Hormuz as the U.S. war with Iran continues. Saudi Arabia said on Friday that Iran-backed Houthi rebels had fired more missiles and drones at the kingdom days after it launched a major air campaign across Houthi-controlled parts of Yemen. Iraq apparently reached a compromise with a powerful Iran-backed militia it accused of smuggling oil.

Markets were lower in Europe and Asia.

—By Damian J. Troise and ALex Veiga, AP business writers

AP Business Writers Yuri Kageyama and Michelle Chapman contributed to this report.

#10587. How to Migrate an Elementor Site to the WordPress Block Editor

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Elementor made your site easy to build. It may also be making it slow. Here's how to move a site onto the Block Editor without breaking URLs, losing content or dropping out of Google.

Page builders solved a real problem. Before the Block Editor matured, they were the easiest way to give clients drag-and-drop layouts. Many of those sites are still running today, and a lot of them are slow on phones.

The data backs that up. According to the HTTP Archive Core Web Vitals Technology Report for June 2026, 36.8% of Elementor sites pass Core Web Vitals on mobile. Sites built with the Block Editor pass 53.0% of the time. That's correlation, not proof, since hosting and setup matter too. But in my experience, it matches what happens when you remove the builder from a slow site.

My reason for migrating sites off Elementor is simple: **page builders make websites heavy. **Having said that, Elementor has improved a lot. Its performance settings, Flexbox Containers, and asset-loading options cut much of the bloat, and a carefully tuned Elementor site can pass Core Web Vitals. But tuning only goes so far. You’re still shipping the builder’s framework, wrapper markup and scripts, and every plugin or Elementor update is a chance for the speed to slip again.

For small business sites that don’t need drag-and-drop editing, rebuilding the same design on the WordPress Block Editor (Gutenberg) removes that weight at the source, without changing what the client sees.

This guide follows the order I use. It assumes you're comfortable with WordPress admin, WP-CLI and a staging site. If you need to set those up first, SitePoint's guide to the ultimate WordPress development environment covers the tooling.

Not every Elementor site needs to move. Migration makes sense when:

It's harder to justify when the site leans on Elementor Pro features you'd need to replace, such as complex popups, dynamic loops or WooCommerce templates. Those can all be rebuilt, but the job gets bigger fast.

You can't prove a migration worked without "before" numbers. Capture these on the live site first.

Crawl the site with a tool such as Screaming Frog, and export every indexable URL with its title, meta description, H1 and canonical tag. You can also pull published pages and posts with WP-CLI:

Run Google PageSpeed Insights, which uses Lighthouse for its lab scores, on the homepage and two or three key templates. Save the mobile lab scores and, if the site has enough traffic, the field data from real users.

Export the last three months of pages and queries from Google Search Console. That's what you'll compare against after launch.

Elementor stores each page's layout as JSON in the elementordata post meta field, and marks builder pages with elementoredit_mode. This command lists every page that uses the builder:

Then list everything else Elementor controls, which is easy to miss:

On the small business sites I've migrated, the inventory usually turns up more than the owner expects. The pages are the easy part. The surprises are saved templates nobody remembers creating, a popup still running on every page, add-on widgets from plugins that were installed once and forgotten, and contact forms that live inside Elementor Pro rather than a form plugin. Finding those before the rebuild saves a broken launch.

Work on a staging copy. Install a block theme, such as Twenty Twenty-Five or a lightweight starter theme, and move the Elementor global styles into theme.json. That way colors, fonts and spacing are defined once, instead of in every widget.

Copy the hex values and font names from Elementor's Site Settings so the new site matches the old one. Rebuild the header and footer as template parts in the Site Editor. If you'd rather write a theme yourself, SitePoint's series on building a WordPress theme from scratch covers the basics.

Most Elementor sites repeat the same few sections: a hero, a services grid, testimonials and a call to action. Build each one once as a block pattern, then reuse it on every page. Patterns are plain block markup, so you don't need React to write them, whatever you may have heard about React and WordPress development.

In a block theme, any PHP file in the /patterns folder with a header comment is registered automatically:

For page content, there's a useful shortcut. Elementor saves a plain HTML copy of each page into post_content. Open a page in the Block Editor, and the old content usually appears in a Classic block. Choose Convert to blocks, then delete the leftovers and rebuild the layout with your patterns. It keeps the copy and images, so you only redo the design.

The difference shows up in the page source. Here's typical front-end markup for a single heading on an Elementor page, using the older section and column layout:

Newer Elementor versions with Flexbox containers and the optimized markup settings trim some of those wrappers, but most older sites still carry all of them. Multiply that by every element on every page, and the weight adds up.

To speed up this step, I built a free Elementor to Gutenberg converter. Paste in a page's elementordata JSON from Step 2, and it returns block markup you can paste into the Block Editor's code editor. It handles headings, text, images, buttons, spacers, dividers and column layouts, and lists anything it can't convert, such as forms and popups, for you to rebuild by hand. It runs entirely in your browser, so the page data never leaves your computer.

The code is rarely what holds a migration up. Content is. Once clients see their site being rebuilt, many decide it's time to rewrite their pages too, and the project ends up waiting on copywriters and approvals. My fix is to separate the two: migrate the existing content as it is, launch, then update the copy afterwards. That keeps the speed and SEO gains on schedule, and it means any ranking change can be traced to either the migration or the new copy, not both at once.

This is the step that decides whether rankings hold.

To spot-check a redirect or a status code from the command line:

When staging matches the live site, deploy it. Then:

Leave the old elementordata meta in place for a few weeks, in case you need to roll back. Once you're confident, take a backup and remove it to shrink the database.

Run PageSpeed Insights again straight away for lab scores. Field data takes longer: the Chrome UX Report uses a rolling 28-day window, so give it a month before judging real-user results. Compare Search Console clicks for the same period before and after launch.

In my migrations, the biggest change is usually page weight and loading time, because the builder's CSS, JavaScript and wrapper markup are gone. What doesn't always improve is server response time. If the site is on slow shared hosting, the first byte still arrives late, and that needs a hosting fix, not a rebuild. Search traffic should hold steady if the URLs and metadata were protected. If it drops, the Step 5 crawl comparison is the first place to look.

Moving off Elementor isn't a one-click job, but it isn't a full redesign either. Record a baseline, inventory what the builder controls, rebuild with patterns, and protect every URL. Most of the speed gain comes from removing code the site never needed. For what to tackle after that, see these practical web performance techniques. Most of the risk comes from rushing the SEO checks. Done carefully, the client gets the same site, a faster one, and an editor that comes with WordPress itself.

#10584. Ukraine deploys homemade 'flying modems' in daring drone attack to destroy Crimean Bridge, ditching Elon Musk's Starlink

Ukraine used a network of so-called "flying modems" to maintain a live video feed of the attack despite Russia's attempts to jam the signal.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Ukrainian forces ditched Elon Musk's Starlink wireless system and used their own relay network of "flying modems" to maintain a live feed of an attack on the Crimean Bridge, even amid Russian attempts to block the signal. The attack, which led to the partial collapse of the bridge, was significant for a number of reasons. The bridge is one of Russian President Vladimir Putin's most symbolic assets due to its linking of the occupied Crimean Peninsula and Russia. But perhaps more importantly, the attack's use of Ukrainian connectivity technology highlights the country's lessening of its reliance on Starlink. The modems were crucial to the success of the mission, with the FT reporting that they kept missiles connected to their pilots while they manoeuvred through Russia's air defenses.

Videos of the attack showed Flamingo missiles, carrying a 1,200kg warhead, being remotely piloted towards the bridge. Flamingo missiles are capable of traveling at around 590 miles per hour, requiring a stable and reliable video feed. It's reported that the Ukrainians deliberately chose not to use Starshield, the military version of Musk's Starlink, for the operation, with the company's satellite network having been restricted in recent weeks. That restricted service would have been insufficient for the Crimean Bridge attack, forcing Ukrainian forces to find another solution. Ukrainian President Volodymyr Zelenskyy said this week that he has not been told why Starlink's connection has been throttled, with neither the White House nor the Pentagon being forthcoming. SpaceX, Starlink's parent company, has also so far not provided any explanation. However, relations between Zelenskyy and Musk appear to be strained, with the SpaceX CEO saying that Ukraine had "gone too far" by striking Russian data centers.

With Starlink, or Starshield, no longer fit for Ukraine's needs, it turned to the "flying drones" instead. The flying communications towers were used as a relay, boosting the signal sent from individual missiles so that it could more readily reach their pilots. This approach allowed the weapons to fly farther while still maintaining a reliable connection, allowing them to bypass Russian air defense systems and attempts to block their signals in the process. Without the relays, Ukrainian drone pilots have previously had to program missiles with coordinates and then rely on their autopilot system. They were inevitably shot down because they could not be steered away from Russian defenses.

Perhaps ironically, Ukraine isn't the first country to use flying communications towers. Russian forces were using similar technology as early as 2023, before the system began using a Chinese mesh networking system.

#10583. The one workplace time suck even AI can’t fix

AI can instantaneously crunch numbers, analyze information, and write emails. One thing it still can’t do? Save you the time spent in meetings. 

Gallup surveyed 21,648 U.S. employees and found that among frequent AI users (32% of respondents, who were defined as those who use AI a few times a week or more), 52% spend less time gathering information and 47% spend less time writing. But 83% said their time in meetings hasn’t changed, while 78% reported no change in time spent collaborating with coworkers.

Although meeting time has dropped from its pandemic-era peak, studies show that meetings still eat up an extraordinary amount of the average worker’s schedule. A 2024 survey by scheduling platform Reclaim.ai, for example, found that employees spend nearly 15 hours a week in meetings on average—roughly 37% of a standard workweek.

The survey’s results suggest that while AI is making some tasks more efficient, other aspects of workplace life may be essentially AI-proof. Along with meeting time (and time spent collaborating with coworkers), Gallup also found that 82% of frequent AI users spend about the same amount of time working with customers or clients.

And despite AI’s ability to save time on many tasks, even frequent users say their job hasn’t gotten any easier overall. Nearly two-thirds of frequent AI users (65%) said their jobs require about the same mental effort as before, while 21% reported expending less mental effort and 14% said their work has become even more demanding.

This could be because AI is replacing routine tasks while creating new responsibilities: 21% of frequent users said they now spend more time reviewing and correcting work, and the same percentage report spending more time learning new skills and tools.

However, AI-driven productivity gains have not translated into dramatically higher expectations from employers (or at least not yet). Among employees at organizations that have adopted AI, just 29% told Gallup the technology has raised expectations for how much work they should complete in a day.

Frequent AI users are more likely to feel pressure, however: 36% said AI has raised expectations for their output, and 33% said they felt pressure to use the technology to keep up.

Now, “Alert Dispatch” takes the whole system up a notch.

#10580. The hidden human intelligence of design

Here’s a scenario you can likely relate to in our age of automated systems: Someone underestimates what you do to deliver a product or service. They are solely focused on the result and say, “I could do that with AI.”

Often overlooked are the years of education and experience that shape what you do, along with the complex decisions you make with judgment, empathy, wisdom, and humility.

We are increasingly seeing this in the architectural industry. And it’s more than just a feeling or a hunch. Recently, the U.S. Department of Education moved to alter the definition of professional degrees to exclude architecture. This demonstrates a serious misunderstanding of what architecture entails. The creative professionals delivering complex projects in the built environment are experts: systems thinkers choreographing designs that shape environments, cultures, and communities.

DESIGN AND EMOTION

Design decisions are interconnected, but that complexity is often underestimated. Changes made after work is underway can require significant rework and additional layers of coordination, impacting budget and schedule. Even seemingly small change requests can ripple through other systems and affect project value.

Behind every built environment is a constellation of decisions and balancing acts that are invisible to the end user.

A successful design blends noiselessly into daily life. When everything is working, and spaces are intuitive, comfortable, and almost anticipatory in supporting your needs, you rarely think about the creativity, imagination, and coordination behind each design and engineering decision.

Architecture’s value is its ability to make us feel something as we enter a space before we even understand why we are experiencing those emotions. The reverence of a cathedral, the excitement of a stadium, the safety and security of a home—those emotional and physical reactions do not happen by chance. Each response is a specific result of a team’s design decisions.

IMPACT VERSUS HOURS

There’s an idea that generative AI can replace nearly any profession. This is a tough pill to swallow for those who’ve spent years or decades honing their craft. But this way of thinking raises a deeper question: Is worth measured by the visible impact you create or the hours you log?

I know architects aren’t the only ones who experience this. Leaders in finance, medicine, tech, and other fields hear from people who see the tip of the iceberg and don’t realize how much is below the surface. When they don’t understand the extent of expertise, they don’t value it. They think it can be done quickly and simply.

The final output is neat and clean. But the reality is that the design process is often complex. It involves researching, discussing, debating, ideating, innovating, finding common ground, and making decisions. One skill an architect learns over time is listening beyond the client’s initial brief, identifying added value to the design program. Early in your career, you treat a brief as a rulebook. Over time, you learn how to advocate for a vision often shaped by a process of listening, challenging, and refining.

It’s important for us to embrace productive friction, in life and in this discipline. It’s the moments that must be experienced, including moments of friction when we are challenged and must overcome some adversity, that are more formative and leave a lasting impact.

6 DESIGN AREAS REQUIRING HUMAN SKILLS

Consider these six challenging parts of any design process that require human expertise:

1. Consensus building: While AI can pull data from multiple sources, it cannot negotiate and compromise with diverse stakeholders. People disagree. An architect is a bridge between clients, project partners, and city officials. Architects have to balance vision with constraints, and to resolve disagreements and conflicts among all parties.

2. Constructability and feasibility: It’s impressive when AI can generate a beautiful rendering in seconds. But what if the rendering isn’t practical or realistic? Architects must make the design come alive in the real world. They need to know, through knowledge and experience, how a design will perform when built, and to exercise judgment based on wisdom.

3. Intelligence beyond pattern recognition: Despite the word “intelligence,” does AI think? Or does it recognize patterns, relying on existing data to predict future outcomes? As today’s problems grow increasingly complex, the synthesis of this complex data to anticipate outcomes is a powerful tool. Where AI recognizes patterns, architects bring discernment, interpreting data through a lens that is contextual and time-sensitive, creating solutions tailored to the histories and needs of specific sites and the communities that use them.

4. Risk management and resilience: Architects are tasked with thinking about future stressors on a building. Based on historic precedent, they consider what long-term resilience is required of buildings they are designing. AI can’t assume that human element of taking responsibility and accountability. IBM’s training manual in 1979 stated, “A computer can never be held accountable; therefore, a computer must never make a management decision.” AI doesn’t understand that lives will be seriously affected if its design isn’t resilient; it’s imperative to leverage the tool responsibly.

5. Challenge assumptions as a trusted advisor: We want to assume the best of those we work with, but trust isn’t easy to build. It’s built through real-world troubleshooting and problem-solving. Sometimes that means saying no. Anyone who ever had a chatbot agree with everything they said understands that AI often tells you what you want to hear.

6. Center work around human experience and emotion: Joy Evans Therapeutic Recreation Center in Washington, D.C.—the only public recreation center in the region offering therapeutic recreational programming—exemplifies the importance of designing for a range of human experiences. To make the center a welcoming and inclusive place for people of all ages and abilities, our designers needed a thorough human understanding of what made people feel safe, seen, and inspired. AI can grasp key tenets of accessibility and belonging abstractly, but doesn’t understand human vulnerability.

When we recognize and respect these messy parts of the process, we understand that human professionals can’t be replaced. It’s important we help our clients and customers understand this too, demonstrating how we can use the best of AI while emphasizing the value of human judgment and perspective.

Steven McKay is CEO of DLR Group.

#10576. Intel Diamond Rapids shows signs of life in SC26 demo running live server

Keep exploring and earning more as a member.

Reviews, benchmarks, and updates on current GPUs.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

Diamonds Rapids is set to launch next year.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Intel’s next-gen Xeon Diamond Rapids CPUs appeared at Supercomputing 2026 (SC26) in two separate booths, but neither was from Intel directly. Most interesting was a running server at the CXL booth, showcasing a demo with a Diamond Rapids CPU. The server ran for the first day of the show, and upon returning to the booth, we were informed that it had been shut down due to noise complaints (it’s hard to mistake a running server). Elsewhere at Bull’s booth, a Diamond Rapids CPU was shown installed in a server behind a glass case. Notably, Intel didn’t have a Diamond Rapids demo, instead focusing most of its booth on Xeon 6+ and its new D270 GPU, otherwise known as Crescent Island.

The demo in question is for Montage’s CXL MXC, or Memory eXpander Controller. If you’re unfamiliar, memory expansion via CXL is an increasingly common tactic to give multi-host environments access to a large pool of memory, breaking free of an individual server’s capacity via CXL. In the demo, Montage is showing off its memory expansion controller running across a Xeon 6 and a Diamond Rapids server, each sharing a pool of memory over CXL. CXL is an open standard, built atop PCIe.

Of course, the demo doesn’t say much about Diamond Rapids performance, but it’s the first time we’ve seen a chip running in the wild. The 256 GB memory capacity shown doesn’t provide any hints, either. Intel has announced that Diamond Rapids will support 16-channel memory, with RDIMM support up to 8,000 MT/s and MRDIMM support up to 12,800 MT/s, matching AMD’s recent EPYC Venice CPUs. However, Intel hasn’t revealed capacity, though we anticipate the maximum capacity will be far above 256 GB – for context, last-gen Granite Rapids-AP supports up to 3 TB per socket.

Bull is a French server designer focused on HPC and AI infrastructure, and it showed off two Xeon 7 Diamond Rapids chips installed in the Bullsquana XH31600 server. As expected for a 2P Diamond Rapids server, it supports 32 channels of DDR5 memory, and it’s designed for a liquid-cooled deployment.

As you can see from the image, these aren’t production chips. They’re labeled with “Intel confidential,” and the code starts with “Q,” revealing that this is a sample. Originally, we expected to see Diamond Rapids in the second half of this year, perhaps around this time. However, in August, Intel revealed that Xeon 7 chips would launch in 2027.

Based on the various servers we saw and the live demo, it seems Intel is targeting a launch earlier in the year, likely in Q2; we expect a larger consumer focus in Q1. It could slip later into the year, though that seems very unlikely given that Diamond Rapids was already pushed back, and the insatiable demand for CPUs in AI data centers doesn’t appear to be drying up.

Also pointing to a relatively quick release are the various servers we saw on the floor at SC26. Several partners showed off their next-gen servers built for Diamond Rapids CPU, sporting the LGA 9324 socket, including Dell and Asus (pictured above). However, Bull was the only vendor we could find that had an actual chip on display.

Diamond Rapids is Intel’s true next-gen CPU, following up on the E-core-only Xeon 6+ range that launched earlier this year. Diamond Rapids packs up to 256 P-cores, and it’s built on Intel’s performance-enhanced 18A-P node. The most interesting aspect of the chip is its topology, with Intel breaking from its previous layout and building something much similar to an EPYC CPU. The package features centralized memory and I/O, which were pushed out to the edges of the package with Granite Rapids. You can read more about the design of the chip in our Diamond Rapids recap from Hot Chips on Tom’s Hardware Premium.

#10575. Could Meta fix its glasses privacy issues with an LED redesign?

Meta launched its own-branded smart glasses this summer (read: with no Ray-Ban or Oakley branding), and they seem to have done well for a number of reasons. Some models are much cheaper than Ray-Ban-branded pairs, while other Meta glasses carry Kylie Jenner or LISA names, bringing in a unique note of fashion, but none of these models changed the look of the camera LED indicator on the front.

Meta has come under fire over the past two years as people have abused the company’s smart glasses, giving them the unfortunate term “perv glasses” on social media because some people disabled the camera LED, which is supposed to illuminate when the camera is recording. Meta has issued several different software patches to disable the glasses’ camera when the LED is tampered with, but many have begun to feel like having a camera on smart glasses just feels wrong after people have abused the technology.

Meta glasses product lead David Woodland posted a render of the Gentle Monster cateye style glasses in a now-deleted Tweet on X on November 17, simply saying “I plead the fifth.” Presumably, he was calling out how similar these glasses look to the Meta Starfire Kylie Jenner style, but we’ll likely never know now.

Samsung and Google’s Warby Parker and Gentle Monster glasses certainly resemble Meta’s glasses, but they also do something noticeably different: the camera LED doesn’t look like the camera on the other side.

So this begs the question: can Meta do anything to salvage the unsavory association its glasses now have attached to them? We covered this topic on episode 125 of our Pixelated podcast, and Woodland’s post further highlights the question. A camera LED that’s obviously different from the camera on the other side would likely prompt people to ask about your glasses, while Meta’s design is purposefully stealthy because of its symmetry.

Does this design difference help Samsung’s Gentle Monster/Warby Parker glasses feel more private, and would a similar redesign help Meta’s glasses? I think it would help a lot, but I also want to know what our readers think. What say you, folks?

#10571. Automating Contract Signing in Node.js: Webhooks, Verification, and Storing Signed PDFs

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Sending a document for signature is the easy part of an e-signature integration. One POST request and the signer gets an email. The work that actually breaks in production happens afterwards: knowing when the document was signed, trusting the notification that told you so, handling the same notification twice, and getting the final PDF into storage before the download link expires.

This tutorial builds that whole loop in Node.js with Express. An event in your app (a deal is approved, a client is onboarded, a task moves to "ready to sign") sends a contract from a template. When the signer finishes, a webhook arrives, your server verifies it, and the signed PDF plus its audit trail get saved without anyone touching them.

The code uses Firma.dev, the e-signature API I work on, because I needed a real API to make the examples runnable. The patterns are not specific to it, though. Signature verification, idempotent handlers, short-lived download URLs, and reconciliation jobs show up in almost every webhook-driven integration, and the same structure applies whether you're receiving events from a payment processor, a CI system, or another signing provider.

There's a fifth part that tutorials usually skip: a reconciliation job that catches anything the webhooks missed. We'll build that too, because webhooks are a notification mechanism, not a delivery guarantee.

Before sending anything, wrap the HTTP calls in one place. This is where authentication lives, and it's also where you handle 429 Too Many Requests once instead of in every caller. Most APIs, this one included, return a Retry-After header on a 429, so the client should honour it rather than guessing a backoff.

The 503 branch matters later. When a document has just been completed, the final PDF and its completion certificate take a moment to generate, and the download endpoint says so with a 503 and a Retry-After instead of handing you a half-built file.

The trigger depends on your product. It might be a route handler, a queue consumer, or a CRM automation calling into your service. What matters is that the function doing the sending is small and takes plain data:

Two design choices are worth calling out.

Use a single create-and-send call when you can. Many signing APIs split this into "create a draft" and "send the draft." That's useful when a human needs to review the document first, but for automation it creates a window where your process can crash between the two calls and leave an orphaned draft. A combined endpoint makes the operation atomic from your side.

Store the provider's ID on your own record immediately. Webhook payloads reference the signing request, not your deal or customer. Without that mapping you'll end up searching by email address, which falls apart as soon as one client signs two documents.

Here's the first place integrations quietly go wrong. Webhook signatures are computed over the exact bytes the sender transmitted. If Express parses the body as JSON first, you lose those bytes, and re-serialising the object won't reproduce them (key order, whitespace, and number formatting can all change). Signature checks then fail intermittently, which is the worst kind of failure.

The fix is to use express.raw() on the webhook route only, so the handler receives a Buffer:

Most webhook providers sign payloads with HMAC-SHA256 using a shared secret. The details vary, so read your provider's docs for the exact header format, but the scheme here is common: the header looks like t=1731456000,v1=5f2b..., and the signed string is the timestamp, a dot, and the raw body. Stripe uses a very similar format, and the Standard Webhooks spec describes the same idea as a vendor-neutral convention.

Each line here defends against something specific.

The OWASP guidance on cryptographic storage covers the other half of this: the secret belongs in an environment variable or secrets manager, never in the repository.

Every serious webhook system delivers at least once, not exactly once. If your endpoint is slow, returns a 5xx, or the network drops the response, the sender retries. Firma.dev, for example, makes up to three attempts per event (immediately, then after at least five minutes, then after at least an hour) and treats anything other than a 2xx as a failure, including redirects. Other providers retry more aggressively. Either way, your handler will eventually see the same event twice.

The defence is to record each event's unique ID and skip ones you've seen. In the server above that's alreadyProcessed and markProcessed. For a demo, a Set works:

In production, use your database and let a unique constraint do the work, so two concurrent deliveries can't both pass the check:

Also notice the order in the route: respond with 200, then do the work. Many providers time out deliveries after a few seconds (five, in this case). Downloading a PDF and uploading it to object storage can easily take longer than that, and a timeout counts as a failure, which triggers a retry, which triggers duplicate work. Acknowledging fast and processing asynchronously avoids that loop.

setImmediate is fine for a tutorial, but it has a real weakness: if the process dies after sending the 200 and before finishing the work, the event is acknowledged and lost. In production, push the event onto a durable queue (BullMQ, SQS, a jobs table) before responding. Step 7 covers the safety net for whatever still slips through.

Now the actual business logic. You'll usually see several event types (sent, viewed, a signer signed, declined, expired, completed). Only one of them means "the document is final":

Three things in here are easy to get wrong.

Don't treat "a signer signed" as "the document is done." With multiple signers, each one triggers its own event, and a per-signer "all complete" flag can be true on more than one of them. Key your storage logic off the single completion event.

Don't persist the download URL. Pre-signed URLs expire (this one carries an expires_at). Saving the link to your database and serving it to users later produces broken links a few minutes or hours later. Download the bytes and store them in storage you control. Swapping the fs.writeFile calls for an S3 PutObject or a Google Drive upload is a drop-in change.

Store the audit trail, not just the PDF. If a signature is ever disputed, the PDF shows what was signed, and the audit trail shows who signed it, when, and from where (timestamps, IP addresses, the sequence of views and signatures). That evidence is what makes an electronic signature defensible, which brings us to the legal side.

Retries are finite. Endpoints go down during deploys. Some providers auto-disable a webhook after enough consecutive failures, and events aren't guaranteed to arrive in order. Any one of these can leave a completed document sitting unsaved.

A small scheduled job closes that gap. Keep a list of signing requests your app considers "pending," and periodically ask the API for their real status:

Since storeSignedDocument overwrites by ID, running it twice for the same document is harmless. That's the property you want from every step in a webhook pipeline: doing it again shouldn't change the result.

None of this is legal advice, but a few facts shape how you build.

In the US, the ESIGN Act and the Uniform Electronic Transactions Act (adopted by 49 states) give electronic signatures the same legal standing as handwritten ones for most commercial documents. ESIGN also has requirements around consumer consent and record retention, which is part of why you store the final PDF yourself rather than relying on a third-party link.

In the EU, eIDAS defines three levels: simple (SES), advanced (AdES), and qualified (QES). SES and AdES cover most business contracts. QES requires a qualified trust service provider and certificate-based identity, and some documents (certain property transfers and court filings, depending on the country) require it or exclude e-signatures entirely. Check which level your document types need before you pick a provider, since not every API offers every level.

Across jurisdictions, the evidence that holds up is the same: who signed, how they were identified, when it happened, and proof the document hasn't changed since. That's the audit trail and the completion certificate, which is why Step 6 stores both.

If you're evaluating providers for a workflow like this, the questions that matter at the integration level are mostly not on the pricing page:

The send call is one line. The reliability comes from everything around it: keeping the raw body for verification, checking timestamps and comparing in constant time, acknowledging fast and deduplicating by event ID, downloading files before their links expire, and running a reconciliation job for the events that never arrive. Get those right and contract signing becomes something your app does in the background, with no one chasing signatures or copying PDFs between folders.

For the Firma.dev-specific details used here (event names, header format, rate limits), see the webhooks guide.

#10568. Silicon Motion demos SM8466 PCIe 6.0 SSD controller, expected to ship by 2028 — controller boasts improved quality of service, samples imminent

Keep exploring and earning more as a member.

Reviews, benchmarks, and updates on current GPUs.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Silicon Motion is showing off its PCIe 6.0 SSD controller for data center applications for the first time at the SC26 conference in Chicago. The final iteration of Silicon Motion's SM8466 controller was taped out recently, and drives based on the chip are due in late 2027 or early 2028. However, the new controller also has a secret weapon designed to ensure consistent performance of SSDs under concurrent workloads that may set it apart from competitors.

Silicon Motion introduced its high-end MonTitan SM8466 enterprise SSD controller designed for next-generation data center and hyperscale storage applications at FMS 2025. The controller features a PCIe 6.x x4 host interface and 16 NAND channels to enable sequential transfer speeds of up to 28 GB/s, random performance of up to 7 million 4K IOPS, and capacities of up to 512 TB and beyond as newer 3D NAND devices emerge. Just like other high-end data center-grade SSD controllers released in recent years, the SM8466 complies with NVMe 2.0 and OCP NVMe 2.5 SSD specifications.

To improve performance and reduce latency, Silicon Motion has developed its Separate Command Architecture (SCA), which separates command and address transmission paths within the NAND interface to improve parallelism and increase effective throughput. Additionally, the controller supports Silicon Motion's 2nd Generation PerformaShape technology, which enables programmable performance limits and predictable quality of service (QoS) across concurrent workloads, which is arguably its most important differentiator against rivals who rely on different methods of guaranteeing performance.

In addition to its performance chops, the MonTitan SM8466 incorporates advanced error correction, virtualization, data protection, and security features. The controller's error correction engine supports low-density parity-check (LDPC) codes with 4KiB codewords and Silicon Motion's 16KiB Collaborative Codeword (CoCo) technology to improve data integrity and reliability with advanced NAND flash memory. The controller also supports Single Root I/O Virtualization (SR-IOV), MPF virtualization, SMART diagnostics, and comprehensive data protection mechanisms. Security capabilities include Secure Boot, AES-256 encryption, TCG Opal compatibility, and firmware attestation. Together, these features make the SM8466 suitable for high-capacity data center drives that are expected to deliver high performance, predictable latency, and reliable operation under demanding data center workloads.

Silicon Motion's 2nd Generation PerformaShape technology builds upon the original PerformaShape architecture introduced with the company's PCIe 5.0-based MonTitan SM8366 enterprise SSD controller several years ago. The 1st Generation implementation uses dedicated hardware accelerators and two-stage leaky-bucket regulators to control read and write performance in terms of IOPS and throughput, both for individual workloads and for the SSD as a whole. This approach enables data center operators to prevent one workload from monopolizing SSD resources at the expense of others.

PerformaShape 2 retains these capabilities but doubles the number of supported quality-of-service (QoS) sets from 256 to 512 and introduces more sophisticated performance controls to accommodate a larger number of concurrent workloads with different requirements. One of the key additions to PerformaShape 2 is Multi-Dimensional Shaping, which enables the controller to coordinate performance limits across multiple I/O characteristics rather than enforce each limit independently. This gives data center operators greater flexibility when allocating SSD resources among applications with different performance requirements.

Another important enhancement of PerformaShape 2 is support for NVMe TP4176, a proposed extension to the NVMe specification that aims to standardize performance management and QoS controls. Whereas the original PerformaShape relies on Silicon Motion's vendor-specific NVMe commands and a dedicated host driver, TP4176 could eventually enable compatible host software to configure performance policies through a standardized mechanism. The technology is particularly relevant for cloud environments where SSDs serve numerous virtual machines, containers, and applications whose storage requirements can change considerably over time, according to the company.

Pakistan's telecommunications authority says that Transworld Associates is looking into what caused the issue and when it expects it to be fixed, but traffic has been rerouted for now.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Pakistan's telecommunications authority, the PTA, has confirmed that internet connectivity issues affecting the country's residents are related to the failure of an international submarine cable. SEA-ME-WE 5 is located almost seven miles off the coast of Pakistan, near the Gulf of Oman and the Strait of Hormuz, and connects the region with European networks via the Middle East. The TWA says that the outage may have caused intermittent connectivity issues and a decline in service quality, with Transworld Associates (TWA) working to identify the cause of the issue. No timeframe has been provided for when a fix will be available yet, but for now, internet traffic has been rerouted to minimize the impact of the outage.

The outage comes weeks after the Islamic Republic of Iran described several undersea fiber cables that run through the Strait of Hormuz as "legitimate new targets" in the ongoing tensions in the area. February's start of military action by the United States, Israel, and others has made the Strait of Hormuz a household name in recent months. General Hossein Mohebbi, a spokesman for Iran's Islamic Revolutionary Guard Corps, pointed out that as many as seven "large cables pass beneath the Strait of Hormuz, and all internet and commercial data pass through them," going on to say they are "legitimate" targets for the group.

One of the international submarine cable systems, the SEA-ME-WE 5 (SMW5), have reported a fault at about 11 KM from Pakistan shore which may cause some internet users to experience intermittent connectivity issues and a decline in service quality. Transworld Associates (TWA) is coordinating with the SMW5 Consortium to identify the root cause of the fault and determine the Estimated Time for Restoration (ETTR). In the meantime, internet traffic is being rerouted through alternative international links to minimise the impact and ensure service continuity to the greatest extent possible. #SMW5 #SubmarineCableFault #InternetDisruption #InternetUpdate #PakistanInternet #ConnectivityUpdate #InternetServices #DigitalPakistan #PTA #TransworldAssociates #TelecomUpdate #NetworkRestoration #StayConnected #InternetConnectivityNovember 18, 2026

Undersea cables are vital for maintaining connectivity between nations and continents, and Iran isn't the only one to have taken note. A Russian ship was chased from British waters by a Royal Navy attack helicopter earlier this year after it was found less than a mile from five undersea cables. The ship was in the area for 14 hours, and the cables it was near happened to connect the UK with Spain, Portugal, Canada, and the United States.
