#10561. Preventing API Contract Drift in TypeScript Applications

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

A frontend application can compile successfully and still fail when it communicates with the backend.

Imagine a backend developer changes an API response from:

The backend works correctly. The frontend also builds without errors. However, a component expecting user.name now receives undefined.

This is an example of API contract drift: the frontend and backend no longer agree on the structure or behavior of an API.

The problem becomes more difficult when several developers or teams work on different parts of an application. A change made by one team may affect another team's assumptions without producing an obvious error during development.

TypeScript helps catch many programming mistakes, but its types do not automatically validate data received over HTTP. A type declaration can describe what the application expects without proving that the server actually returns it.

In this tutorial, we'll look at a practical way to make API contracts more reliable.

Consider a typical frontend API function:

The function promises to return a User, so TypeScript allows the application to access properties such as name and email.

The return value of response.json() is not automatically checked against the User interface. The interface only provides compile-time information; it does not validate the actual JSON received from the server.

the frontend may still treat the response as a User even though the data has a different structure.

This is particularly important when working with:

Independently deployed frontend and backend applications.

APIs maintained by different engineering teams.

The solution is to validate data at the boundary where it enters the application.

Zod is a TypeScript-first schema validation library. It allows developers to describe expected data structures and validate actual values against those definitions.

Now create a schema for the user response:

First, UserSchema describes the structure that the application expects at runtime.

Second, z.infer generates the corresponding TypeScript type from the schema.

This means developers do not need to maintain a separate interface and validation definition for the same data structure.

The response is initially treated as unknown, which prevents the application from assuming that untrusted data already has the expected structure.

UserSchema.parse() validates the actual response.

If the backend returns an unexpected property type or misses a required field, Zod throws a validation error instead of allowing invalid data to move silently through the application.

You can also use safeParse() when you prefer to handle validation errors without exceptions:

This approach is useful when API responses are external or when a frontend must provide more controlled error handling.

One common mistake is placing API validation logic directly inside React components.

For a small application, this may seem convenient. As the project grows, however, it becomes difficult to reuse validation rules and identify where data transformations happen.

A better approach is to separate API schemas, service functions, and UI components.

The responsibilities are straightforward:

schemas/user.ts defines the expected user data.

api/client.ts handles common HTTP configuration.

api/users.ts contains user-related API requests.

hooks/useUser.ts manages data fetching for React.

components/UserProfile.tsx displays the validated data.

This structure makes it easier to change an API implementation without rewriting the UI.

It also makes testing more manageable because validation logic can be tested independently of React rendering.

For more ideas on organizing application code, see SitePoint's guide to modern JavaScript modules.

Runtime validation protects an application when data arrives. However, teams also need a shared description of what an API is supposed to accept and return.

OpenAPI provides a standard format for describing HTTP APIs, including:

For example, an endpoint can be described using an OpenAPI document:

This document describes what the endpoint should return.

Both frontend and backend developers can refer to the same contract instead of relying on separate assumptions or outdated documentation.

Swagger Editor helps developers write and validate OpenAPI documents. It also provides a convenient way to inspect API definitions.

Swagger UI turns an OpenAPI document into interactive API documentation, allowing developers to explore endpoints and test requests.

Redoc provides another way to generate readable API documentation from an OpenAPI specification.

These tools are particularly useful when APIs are maintained by multiple developers or consumed by several applications.

Maintaining API types manually across frontend and backend repositories creates opportunities for inconsistencies.

For example, a backend developer might add a new optional property while the frontend continues using an older interface.

One way to reduce this duplication is to generate TypeScript definitions and API clients from an OpenAPI specification.

Orval is a useful tool for this workflow.

It can generate TypeScript clients and types from an OpenAPI document, helping frontend developers work with consistent endpoint definitions.

A typical configuration might look like this:

The exact configuration depends on the Orval version and the structure of the OpenAPI document.

Once configured, the client can be regenerated when the API specification changes.

This reduces the amount of repetitive code developers need to maintain manually.

However, generated types should not be confused with runtime validation. They improve consistency during development, but an application may still need to validate responses received from external systems or services that cannot be fully trusted.

Frontend developers often need to build or test features before the corresponding backend endpoint is ready.

Waiting for a complete backend implementation can slow down development, particularly when multiple teams work independently.

Mock Service Worker (MSW) helps solve this problem by intercepting network requests and returning controlled responses.

You can define a mock handler for the user endpoint:

The frontend can now make normal HTTP requests while MSW supplies predictable responses during development or testing.

You can also simulate an invalid response:

This makes it possible to check whether the application's validation and error-handling logic behave as expected.

MSW is particularly valuable because it allows frontend tests to run without requiring a live backend environment.

Runtime validation and generated clients improve reliability, but they do not automatically guarantee that independently developed services remain compatible.

For systems with multiple services, contract testing can provide another layer of protection.

Pact is a contract-testing tool that allows consumers and providers to verify their expectations about API interactions.

Consider a frontend application that expects the user endpoint to return:

The consumer defines the expected interaction.

The backend provider can then run verification against that contract to check whether its actual response satisfies the consumer's expectations.

This helps identify breaking changes before they reach production.

These tools solve different problems. A project does not necessarily need all of them, but larger applications can benefit from combining the approaches that match their architecture.

A contract is only useful if developers consistently check it when making changes.

A practical approach is to run validation and tests automatically whenever someone opens a pull request.

For example, a GitHub Actions workflow might look like this:

The api:validate script should be configured to validate the project's OpenAPI document or run the relevant contract checks.

The test command should execute the application's automated tests.

For a production project, you may also want to include:

The objective is to detect incompatible changes before they are merged.

A failed pipeline gives developers an opportunity to investigate the change while the context is still fresh.

API contracts become harder to maintain when several teams contribute to the same system.

For example, a company might have separate teams responsible for:

If nobody owns an API contract, small changes can create unexpected problems for other teams.

Assigning clear ownership helps establish who reviews changes, maintains documentation, and coordinates breaking updates.

This becomes particularly important when an organization expands its engineering capacity across locations or adds several specialized teams. Companies planning that kind of growth may also need to consider enterprise IT recruitment as part of their wider engineering workforce strategy.

Regardless of how a team is assembled, its development process should establish clear responsibilities for API design, versioning, testing, and release coordination.

A few practical conventions can make a significant difference:

Assign an owner to every important API or service.

Document breaking changes before implementation.

Require contract-related tests in pull requests.

Keep generated clients synchronized with the API specification.

Communicate deprecations before removing existing fields.

Monitor production errors after deploying API changes.

These practices help prevent a growing engineering organization from becoming a collection of disconnected services.

API contract drift is not simply a TypeScript problem. It is a coordination problem that becomes visible when applications and services develop different expectations about the same data.

TypeScript provides useful compile-time checks, but it cannot guarantee that an HTTP response matches a declared interface.

A more reliable approach combines several techniques:

Use OpenAPI to document API expectations.

Generate TypeScript clients with tools such as Orval.

Use MSW to test frontend behavior without a live backend.

Use Pact when consumer-provider contract verification is appropriate.

Run validation and tests automatically in CI/CD.

You do not need to introduce every tool at once. Start by validating external API responses, document important endpoints, and gradually automate contract checks as the application grows.

The result is a codebase where developers can make changes with greater confidence and where frontend and backend teams have a shared understanding of how their systems communicate.

#10560. Fitbit Edge after 30 days: Life in the middle lane [Video]

The biggest compliment you can pay Google at the moment is that alongside the Pixel Watch, recent Fitbit releases have been a huge return to form – the Fitbit Edge included.

I need to nail my colors to the mast; I’m a Fitbit Air fanboy. I’ve talked about it multiple times on the Pixelated podcast, here on the site, and it made me switch away from the Pixel Watch 5 because of how easily it fit into my existence without insisting on itself.

The Fitbit Edge worried me at first because it comes with a screen, which is something I realized isn’t that important to me day-to-day. After 30 days of wear, I’m conflicted because maybe I was too hasty to ditch a display on my wrist.

The design is somewhat in a halfway house because it feels like it’s trying to be both a classic Fitbit while integrating some of the Pixel Watch features we know and love. Initially, I loved it, but it has become something that isn’t quite enough in either direction to suit my own tastes.

My love for the simplicity of the Fitbit Air has colored how I feel, but the Edge has an excellent core design. The display bezels remind me heavily of the original Pixel Watch; yes, they’re there, but you only notice them when the light catches the frame edge.

After a month of daily wear, the display has lost a bit of its luster. The oleophobic coating is just about holding up, but now we’re into the cooler months; it’ll be put through a barrage of jacket cuffs and hoodie sleeves. This is one of the only durability concerns I have had so far.

Despite that admittedly minor concern, the Fitbit Edge is not something you need to baby. I’ve bashed it with weights, caught my wrist on zips (zippers for you, my American friends), and bashed it around with reckless abandon. It’s solid and hasn’t picked up as much as a single scuff. I don’t care too much if a fitness tracker gets a beating, whereas I would be more concerned about damaging my Pixel Watch.

If you come in wanting a smartwatch-like experience from the Fitbit Edge, I think you’ll instantly be frustrated by the UI. It can feel cramped when you see notifications, and the large bezels don’t help all that much, but Google has well optimized almost all of the key sections outside of this.

Tiles are very well implemented; they’re just a smaller, squatter take on the Pixel Watch tile system, and it’s one of the best things about the Fitbit Edge interface. It’s also really snappy, which is something that isn’t the case with cheaper fitness trackers such as this. I’m constantly checking the weather or using the Google Health tile, but I have loved the quick access complication on the default watch face.

I tried a few of the other designs over the past 30 days; a lot of them are great. The default has become my favorite. It’s information-dense without being too overloaded. Tapping the complications takes you to the exact data point. I genuinely love how fuss-free an experience it is, save for a few minor complaints.

We could criticize the accuracy of the data or the lack of tracking for certain types of workouts, but I think that misses the point of the Fitbit Edge. It’s a simple tracker with a screen, and the fact that you get even basic ECG on a device of this size is impressive.

I truly have worn the Edge daily for a full month, and while the bands are comfortable, I have found that they cause me to get a rash on the underside of my left wrist just under the palm. It’s actively stopping me from wearing it all the time now. I hope that the new connector doesn’t slow down third-party development for bands, because I’m desperate for a fabric band to alleviate this issue.

This is exacerbated when the rubber band gets wet or sweaty. If you have sensitive skin, you might have the same problem as I have. Using some moisturizer has helped, but it is something I wasn’t prepared to suffer with before the Fitbit Edge turned up. I get that this is likely a “me” problem, but I wanted to note it.

When the screen gets wet or moist, it can bug out quite a lot. Taps don’t register properly, and a few water droplets can activate all the menus and submenus. Luckily, it won’t force your phone to place a call or anything like that, but the water protection mode is a must. I have kept it on extensively.

Swimmers will probably get super frustrated; heck, I was on a rainy day as the screen kept bugging out. Overall, though, I have enjoyed the fitness tracking. Unlike on the Fitbit Air, it is nice to have a screen to start a workout rather than head over to my phone. That said, the experience is the same as on your phone, but scaled down to fit the watch face.

I dislike that you don’t get a phone notification to show the current workout progress by default, like the Fitbit Air. I had become accustomed to that. Luckily, you can enable it in the Google Health settings. I like having this as I forget to stop workouts so often without a prompt to hit “stop.”

With audio playback, you’re also getting the partial benefits of watch-phone controls, but it’s a two-step process unlike on Pixel Watch. You either have to go to the “Media” app or have the tile active and navigate to it to access play/pause and all your other streaming app controls. I tend not to bother using these as it’s finicky.

This had me thinking about NFC. I think a lot of people would have liked NFC for Google Wallet on the Fitbit Edge, but I think having to enter a PIN on such a small screen would really have been a nightmare. I’ve made wireless payments with my watch less than five times, as it’s just easier to use my phone rather than contort my wrist to a wireless payment terminal. I figure this was something that was thought about and makes a lot of sense given the constraints.

One of my other complaints is that the DND and Bedtime modes from my account haven’t synced or at least don’t appear to sync. Unless I’m mistaken, you need to activate these settings manually every time, which has been super annoying when in bed, as a flick of your wrist can blind you in a dark room. I figure this is something that can be fixed with an update, so here’s hoping.

I’ve been super impressed with the battery life. A week is easily attainable for me with up to three gym visits per week, so I’ve charged 5 times in a month. That, to me, is excellent. I picked up a cheap charging dock, as the Edge uses the same charger for the Fitbit Air, so I figured why not.

It’s not the best-looking dock, but I quite like how it airs off my Edge so it’s not as stinky after gym sessions – as I wipe with an alcohol pad and clean as regularly as possible.

The experience is fine; it’s almost a third of the price of the Pixel Watch and lacks a few features due to the various compromises – compromises I can deal with, as smartwatches have become information overload for me over time, and I like that the app selection is ultra-refined. At no point does the Fitbit Edge give you information overload.

Coming from a Charge 6 or an older Fitbit, this is an amazing replacement. It’s the new “default” fitness tracker for people who want all the hallmarks of what makes a great Fitbit.

There is just enough. Nothing more, nothing less. To me, this feels like the perfect middle ground for a lot of people. You want something simple, touchscreen-enabled, and that doesn’t get in the way. As impressed as I have been with the Edge, though, I’ve loved a fuss-free tech accessory so much recently that I’ll probably go back to the Fitbit Air.

#10557. Vivo X500 Pro camera review: Battle of the small(er) phones vs Pixel 11 Pro

The compact Vivo X500 Pro has some camera downgrades compared to the Pro Max, so are you better off saving money and getting the base Vivo X500 instead?

Over the past few years, Vivo has made a name for itself as the camera powerhouse smartphone company. The first time I used a Vivo X100 Pro blew my mind in a way smartphone cameras hadn’t for a few years, but does the company’s 4th iteration — yes, Vivo skipped the X400 — of the X series still impress?

The answer is mostly yes. The Vivo X500 series includes five models: the base X500, the X500 Pro, and the X500 Pro Max. Unlike some other phones, all three of these models feature different camera hardware that takes advantage of the physical size differences. The X500 Pro Max’s hardware is the best of the bunch, but that phone is substantially larger and heavier as a result.

So I’m comparing the more reasonably sized models to see which semi-compact Vivo phone you should consider this year. I tossed the Google Pixel 11 Pro into the comparison since Google also offers a compact flagship camera phone.

Most camera specs between these phones are surprisingly comparable, so the differences you’re about to see are largely caused by some pretty massive differences in processing. I already covered Vivo’s wild new AI camera mode, so let’s go for something a little closer to normal smartphone camera use: a day at the Biltmore.

Perhaps the biggest difference between these phones is Vivo’s pursuit of “perfection,” while Google seems to embrace a more “natural” look. At 10x and 5x, the photos from both Vivo phones are essentially noise-free, have clear sharpening applied to them, and are generally quite vibrant. Despite having similar specs, the Vivo X500 Pro’s more advanced Sony LYT telephoto sensor pulls out more detail.

Google’s images are notably softer, although not in a low-resolution sort of way. They look more like they’re taken from a standard camera rather than a smartphone, which some people may prefer. While telephoto image quality between the Vivo X500 Pro and Google Pixel 11 Pro is mostly down to preference, Google’s phone is substantially faster while taking the actual photo. In all of the darker shots, the Pixel 11 Pro took the picture instantly. Both Vivo X500 models took a 2-3 second exposure shot, making me stand there for longer than I’m used to.

Ultrawide performance is where I feel we see the largest gap between these phones. Both the Vivo X500 and X500 Pro cameras offer bright, well-balanced shots that are vibrant but not unrealistic. The light sources in the room emit a lovely warm glow, yet the bulbs themselves aren’t overblown. The X500 Pro’s slightly warmer image across all three scenarios looks best to me.

The Pixel 11 Pro’s ultrawide images are often underexposed and lacking in dynamic range. The staircase shot exhibits this problem the most. This can come off as natural in some scenarios, as modern smartphone photography is notorious for brightening dark areas too much, but too often the Pixel 11 Pro’s ultrawide shots just feel unnaturally dark.

The main camera is much more balanced, although it’s fun to push these phones to find their weaknesses. One of the best examples of differing priorities is in the wreath shot, which was quite dark in real life thanks to the bright sunlight outside. The Pixel 11 Pro valiantly tried to brighten up the shadows so you could actually see the wreath (since it’s clearly the subject), causing the rest of the scene to look forcefully bright.

The same issue cropped up in the image of the wine rack, where both Vivo phones captured a nicer-looking shot. The image of the trellis walkway is a tie, but the photo of my ridiculous bowl of ice cream looks best on the Vivo X500 Pro, once again.

Portrait mode is where I think the Vivo X500 Pro is the clear loser, but it’s not for any of the reasons in the other categories above. Rather, the X500 Pro seems to have some weird warping happening in the bokeh processing that’s not present on the base Vivo X500. This is in both front-facing and rear camera portrait shots, so it’s not a lens issue or some weird setting.

Google cleaned up its portrait shots so much this year that it’s borderline ridiculous. Pixels had the worst portrait mode for years, but this year’s overhaul of the portrait algorithm made all the difference in the world.

Overall, the Vivo X500 series is another solid release for Vivo, and while neither the base nor Pro models offer the same top-tier camera specs as the Pro Max model, it’s clear Vivo pulled off something great. The Vivo X500 Pro retails for €1,399 in the EU, while the base X500 global model is a little harder to find, but should be quite a bit cheaper if you can get your hands on it.

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

And inks long-term supply agreement with Microsoft.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Lip-Bu Tan, chief executive of Intel, said on Thursday that yields of chips made using the company's latest 18A (1.8nm-class) fabrication process have achieved 70%, which is more or less in line with what one would expect from an all-new manufacturing technology at this stage of its ramp. In addition, Intel and Microsoft have signed an agreement under which Microsoft will secure Intel Xeon supply capacity for years to come and will use Intel's EMIB advanced packaging technology for in-house silicon.

"When I took over [as CEO in March, 2025], 18A yield was horrible; now we are at 70%," Lip-Bu Tan said at the TomorrowX conference (via @firesidealpha).

Lip-Bu Tan did not specify whether he meant functional yield, parametric yield, or percentage of sellable silicon. However, it is reasonable to expect a CEO of a semiconductor company to talk about functional die yield, the percentage of dies that pass basic electrical and functional testing.

Intel began volume production of compute tiles of its Core Ultra 300-series CPUs codenamed Panther Lake using 18A in the second half of 2025, so a 70% functional yield seems decent at this stage of the product ramp, though for Intel's economics a lot depends on parametric yield and how many advanced SKUs of Core Ultra 300-series 'Panther Lake' processors Intel can supply to customers at a premium. The same applies to Intel's Xeon 6+ Clearwater Forest processors with up to 288 cores that are in high-volume production now and which use 18A-based compute chiplets along with Foveros Direct 3D stacking.

In addition to announcing an important 18A milestone, Intel also signed a long-term agreement with Microsoft, under which the latter will secure multi-year Intel Xeon supply capacity, including supply of next-generation Xeon processors codenamed Diamond Rapids, and will use Intel's EMIB advanced packaging technology for multi-chiplet data center solutions developed in-house.

The EMIB agreement is arguably the biggest news. Microsoft is the first company to publicly commit to Intel's advanced packaging technology for its own silicon (i.e., it does not mean that Microsoft is the first third party to use EMIB; it is the first party to confirm usage of EMIB), which represents a significant commercial development for Intel Foundry in terms of publicity. However, the announcement does not identify the processors involved, their manufacturing process, production volumes, or deployment schedule. Microsoft was the first company to commit to use Intel's 18A for its data center silicon in 2024, though it is unclear whether the newly announced EMIB packaging agreement relates to the same solution or not. As the majority of high-end data center solutions tend to use multi-chiplet design, it is reasonable to expect Microsoft's future solutions for data centers to use EMIB for multi-die processors.

"Through use of our advanced packaging capabilities with EMIB to scale future generations of Azure infrastructure, Intel and Microsoft can combine the flexibility of heterogeneous integration with the manufacturing scale required for the AI era — giving customers new levels of performance and economics when interacting with Azure," said Naga Chandrasekaran, EVP, Chief Technology and Operations Officer and General Manager of Intel Foundry.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

The Xeon agreement provides Intel with greater visibility into future server CPU demand. Importantly, Microsoft has committed to using Intel's next-generation Xeon 'Diamond Rapids,' although the announcement does not disclose purchase volumes or financial terms.

The collaboration extends beyond processor procurement and EMIB. Specifically, Intel and Microsoft are jointly developing infrastructure capabilities that encompass processor architecture, heterogeneous integration, CXL memory expansion, QuickAssist Technology (QAT) acceleration, and Trust Domain Extensions (TDX) security.

#10554. Google Play wants Android apps to nail foldable basics by April 2027

Google Play is introducing new adaptive app and game quality requirements next year to elevate the Android foldable experience.

Starting April 2027, the Play Store will require developers to meet Tier 3 of the Android adaptive app quality guidelines for foldables.

Referred to as “Adaptive ready” or “basic” support, Tier 3 requires apps not to use letterboxing (or other compatibility modes) and to fill the entire screen. During rotation, resizing, and folding/unfolding, apps must preserve scroll position, text input, and media playback. Apps must also support multi-window environments.

Apps that do not meet this quality bar will no longer be able to publish or update on Google Play, and may have reduced visibility on the store.

At the same time, Google Play will require foldable device screenshots for app listings: “Providing foldable screenshots by April 2027 will become a standard submission requirement when publishing new apps or updating your store listing.”

Curious to see whether the requirements will actually be as strict as they make them seem (on paper).

If so, then also Google will have a ton of work to do to modernise all of their apps.

By August 2027, new and updated apps will be required to target Android 17 (API level 37).

Over time, this “quality framework will progressively expand to incorporate Tier 2 and Tier 1 standards across foldables, tablets, Googlebooks, and Android XR.”

Apps that meet Tier 2 (better) or Tier 1 (best) will get “premier store badging, editorial spotlighting, and enhanced merchandising across Google Play as the platform bar elevates.”

On the gaming front, titles will need to add foldable screenshots to listings by April 2027, while Tier 3 large screen quality guidelines go into effect in August 2027.

#10551. Google is forcing Android app developers to properly support foldables

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Now that the iPhone Duo is foldable is here, Google wants to make sure apps on Android foldables keep up.

Now that the iPhone Duo is foldable is here, Google wants to make sure apps on Android foldables keep up.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Now that Apple’s foldable is here, Google wants to make sure apps on Android foldables are a good experience. Starting in April 2027, Google is going to require Android developers to make sure their apps have a baseline level of support for foldables. If they don’t, developers risk not being able to publish or update their apps or have reduced visibility for their apps on Google Play.

Google says Android apps must meet its “Tier 3 — Adaptive ready” standard. “Apps must fill the available display area without letterboxing or compatibility modes,” Google says. “Apps must preserve state (scroll position, text input, media playback) during rotation, folding/unfolding, and resizing, while supporting multi-window environments.” (The other two tiers are “Adaptive optimized” and “Adaptive differentiated” and take further advantage of the possibilities of foldables.) As of the April 2027 deadline, developers also will have to include three foldable screenshots for their app listings. Developers can start publishing their foldable screenshots today.

Similar requirements will apply to games, though developers have until August 2027 to get their titles ready for Android foldables, Google says. (Google “plans to extend” its requirements to other large-screen devices like tablets and Googlebook laptops in “future phases.”) However, game developers will still need to share three foldable screenshots for app listings starting in April.

The changes are being announced just weeks after Apple launched the iPhone Duo, which already has more than 200,000 optimized apps, though some developers are still figuring out Apple’s foldable form factor.

#10549. 10 Best AI App Builders I've Used in 2026 (and What Each One Gets Wrong)

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

AI App Builders are exploding in popularity, and for good reason. It’s never been easier to build real, functional apps and internal tools using app builders. To test them all, I built the same app on 10 of the most popular AI app builders and tested each across usability, cost, correctness, and ease of use. Most of them can turn a paragraph of description into something that looks like software within a single session. The differences show up around the tenth prompt, when you learn what each edit costs and who has to fix the code once something breaks. I took every price from the vendor's own pricing page on October 29, 2026.

TLDR: Of the best AI app builders I've used, Softr is the one I'd hand to a team that needs auth, data, user groups or integrations. The other nine I tested include code generators such as Lovable and Bolt and visual platforms such as Bubble and Glide, and each one carries a cost or upkeep trade-off worth seeing before you commit.

I looked at the same seven things in every tool, from the first prompt onward:

Softr gets used a lot by operations and business teams as their AI app builder for secure client portals, internal tools and other business apps, none of which require code. Write a short brief in your own words, and the AI Co-Builder turns it into a database plus the core app that sits on it: tables, pages and layouts, the data connections behind them, plus user groups and permissions, so real users can sign in from day one. Workflows come as the next step, either by prompting the Co-Builder again or by adding them in the visual editor.

The Free plan is $0 for unlimited apps, with room for 5 app users, 3 builders, 5,000 records and 5 AI credits each month. Basic runs $19 per month, Pro $99 and Business $329, all on annual billing. Enterprise pricing is custom and includes SSO and SCIM for organizations managing access at scale. The full plan table sits on Softr's pricing page.

Lovable builds full-stack web apps through conversation, writing React code while it takes care of hosting, SSL and the backend. It's designed to move a founder or product team from a prompt to a shareable prototype, and the preview updates as the agent works.

Base44 calls itself a no-code AI platform: you explain the app, and it generates the structure, design and logic while running the backend itself. Authentication, a database and analytics come on every plan, Free included.

Base44 is free for 25 message credits a month. Starter costs $16 per month and Builder $40 per month, both on annual billing, with Pro at $80 and Elite at $160.

Bolt is a coding agent that writes full-stack projects in the browser and runs them on Bolt Cloud, which supplies hosting, databases and integrations. It suits people who want to watch the code take shape and edit it themselves.

Bolt Free is $0 with 1M tokens a month and Bolt branding. Pro costs $25 per month with 10M tokens, and Teams runs $30 a month for each member on monthly billing.

Replit pairs its Agent with a cloud workspace, so a description turns into a running app whose sign-in, database, hosting and monitoring come wired up. The homepage pitches it to people who don't code, though the result underneath is a code project.

Core costs $20 per month ($18 with annual billing) and Pro costs $100 per month ($90 with annual billing). Enterprise is custom and adds SSO, SCIM and audit logs.

Glide starts internal business apps from whatever you hand it, whether a spreadsheet, a written prompt or a PDF, and its newer platform, GlideOS, reads a sheet's structure and relationships to draft screens and workflows. Its apps grew up on phones, and that heritage still shapes the product.

Glide Free is $0 with limited credits. Basic costs $25 per month, Plus $50 per month and Pro $125 per month; annual billing isn't offered yet, according to Glide.

Bubble Free gives you 50K workload units and 500 AI credits each month. Web plans run $32 per month for Starter, $119 for Growth and $349 for Team, all on annual billing.

Retool is an internal tools engine aimed at engineering teams. You describe or assemble an app that connects straight to production databases, APIs and LLMs, with auth and access controls in place at deployment.

v0 generates full-stack Next.js web apps from a prompt and deploys them to Vercel, with Design Mode for visual tweaks and GitHub sync for the code. It's designed around a developer's workflow.

v0's Free plan gives new users $5 of one-time credits and caps messages at 7 per day. Plus runs $30 a month per user, and Business $100 a month per user.

Figma Make turns prompts and Figma design context into code-backed prototypes and web apps you can publish at a URL. It can also scaffold a Payload backend with a database, APIs, auth and an admin panel, or connect to Supabase for sign-in and stored data.

Figma's Starter plan is free with up to 500 AI credits a month. Figma lists a Professional Full seat at $16 per month with 3,000 AI credits, and Organization Full seats cost $55 per month on annual billing.

Collins Dictionary named "vibe coding" its Word of the Year for 2025, and the phrase now covers any workflow where you describe software in plain language and an AI produces it. AI app builders package that workflow with hosting, data and sign-in, so a non-developer can get from idea to something clickable without installing anything.

The questions buyers ask have turned plainer since then: what each edit costs, who keeps the app running, and whether a client can sign in and see their own records alone. A generated front end makes a good demo, and a team using it every day needs permissions and a predictable bill for the next hundred edits.

Softr is now my default when recommending AI app builders. It handles teams, auth, and real company data better than any other product. The AI Co-Builder drafts tables, pages and user groups, workflows follow as the next step, and the app keeps running on maintained platform components with no codebase to patch. App-user pricing and an editor that works without AI credits keep later changes from turning into a running meter.

Three situations call for a different tool. Glide makes a better mobile-first choice when the app spends its days on phones in the field. Bubble gives more front-end design freedom when a project calls for bespoke layouts, interactive animation or heavy brand styling. If your engineers want version control and audit logs and can handle a technical setup, Retool covers that need.

Agents are moving inside the apps these tools produce. Gartner expects task-specific AI agents inside 40% of enterprise applications by the close of 2026, against under 5% in 2025, so a 2027 app builder will need to host agents that respect the same permissions as human users.

Building is also leaving the builder's own window. The Model Context Protocol now sits under the Linux Foundation's Agentic AI Foundation and reports more than 97 million SDK downloads a month, with client support in ChatGPT and Claude, among others. Softr's changelog dates its official Claude and ChatGPT connector to October 6, 2026, and I expect more teams to start an app from the chat assistant they already use.

The caution comes from Gartner too: it predicts organizations will scrap upward of 40% of agentic AI projects before 2028, citing escalating costs, unclear business value and inadequate risk controls. Going into 2027, I'd judge any builder on whether it keeps agent costs and access rules visible to the team running the app.

Neville Naidoo is a technology journalist covering AI, startups, and the digital economy, with a focus on how emerging technologies are constantly evolving and shaping our everyday life.

New OS is ‘Built out of love for the NT architecture’ and released under GPL3.0.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

There’s a new vibe-coded OS in town going by the name of LiberNT. Its name provides a big clue to its nature: Liber (free), NT (New Technology architecture, as in Windows NT). As it has been released under GNU’s GPL3.0 license, LiberNT also provides a free new way to run apps designed for Microsoft’s Windows 11.

LiberNT has been under development for some time, but on Wednesday it made a big leap. Reading the intro, you might have wondered if this work had a ReactOS connection, and indeed there is. But what started as a fork of ReactOS has already got a new boot loader, graphics stack, memory manager – and yesterday a new kernel. “LiberNT is not affiliated with or endorsed by the ReactOS project or Microsoft,” insist the devs behind this OS.

This still fledgling operating system is designed (mostly) for modern hardware and focuses on 64-bit platforms: amd64, arm64 and riscv64. Ambitiously, the goal is to develop this OS to deliver a free Windows alternative that is “stable, reliable and efficient.” On that latter point, yesterday’s blog update relays some impressive efficiency and performance stats.

The LiberNT team has benchmarked their eponymous OS against Microsoft’s Windows NT, and it beats it hands-down in 112 of 132 system tests. The testing assessed performance in the following areas: file caching, memory handling, threads and locks, plus FAT32 and NTFS storage. Interestingly, both the free and commercial OS were benchmarked on a Mac with an Apple M3 Pro chip. We scanned through the results, and the worst loss for LiberNT was a 28% performance deficit vs Win11, but it claims some astounding relative performance wins showing the free OS being over 2000% faster.

The devs are entirely clear about the fact that the operating system was vibe coded. “AI is part of how LiberNT is built. It helps us review and write code, and everything it writes is then reviewed by a human,” explains a section of the GitHub page for the project. “Before anything reaches the main branch, a person runs and validates the end-to-end tests. We care about this project too much to let a regression slip through.”

If the above highlights about this new OS's scope, mission, compatibility, and performance have whetted your appetite, there is the full code available via the GitHub link (top), and even some ready-made builds as bootable disk images. Indeed, I’m quite eager to give this new OS a spin. Hopefully, it works as well as the ambitious developers suggest, and I won’t be disappointed by any stability issues using my daily productivity apps like Firefox, Word, and an old release of Photoshop.

#10546. Review: Amazon’s Alexa Tablet 8 is a Fire Tablet with Google Play for $150 – what’s not to love?

Amazon shocked everyone in October when it launched its new Alexa Tablet series with Google Play support built-in, addressing the biggest shortcoming of its older Fire Tablets. For Black Friday 2026, the Alexa Tablet 8 is a mere $150 and, with Google Play on board, this feels like a Fire Tablet that’s all grown up.

The Alexa Tablet 8 is one of three new Amazon tablets that launched in October with Google Play on board, and it’s also the one I was personally the most excited about. I love a good tiny Android tablet, but there just aren’t that many out there – and some of the ones we do have are exorbitantly expensive.

Right up there with Amazon’s long-running reputation in the tablet space, Alexa Tablet 8 is very much an affordable device, and that’s reflected in its fairly meager specs:

Yet, I didn’t find myself put off by the device.

I specifically asked Amazon for a review unit of the absolute base model – 4GB of RAM and lockscreen ads – and I can say with confidence it’s Not Bad at the end of the day.

The display is objectively not good, but it’s not really trying to be flashy either. The panel gets bright enough for most indoor use and, for general browsing or watching videos around the house or even on a flight, it gets the job done. If anything, my biggest complaint on the display was just how smudgey it gets with fingerprints.

Hardware is a strong suit. The Alexa Tablet 8 has an aluminum build that comes in this gorgeous teal color as well as a softer “Rosette” pink (or “Graphite,” if you want to keep it boring). It’s thin and light and slips into a bag easily, and even fit into the back pocket of my jeans. Buttons are clicky and aspects such as the speakers actually do well enough – like the display, the speakers aren’t good, but they’re not bad by any stretch either.

Amazon has a folio cover available that’s simple but nice, with a fabric finish that’s grippy. It’s an extra $40, though, which feels like a bit much for a super-affordable Android slate.

The biggest question was around the software experience, and this is where this new effort really shines.

Alexa Tablet 8 ships with Android 16 out of the box, not FireOS or whatever Amazon was calling its forked experience on Fire Tablets (I genuinely refuse to go double-check because it’s a memory I’m thrilled to never have to revisit). It’s a pretty “stock” experience, with Amazon leaving aspects such as the notification shade/quick settings alone, and even the Settings menu feels pretty clean.

That’s not to say nothing has changed. The homescreen has a fairly standard layout , but with these neat little corner widgets – one a “dynamic” widget with scrolling content (mostly ads) and the other a shortcut for Alexa search. Amazon is pushing Alexa as a voice assistant pretty heavily on these tablets, but I’m happy to report it’s pretty easy to ignore if you choose. These tablets will be great additions to your Alexa ecosystem if you’re invested, but I’m simply not.

The biggest change you’ll find is the lockscreen, where Amazon’s base models all come with lockscreen ads. This saves you about $20 on the hardware, and it’s not that bad of an experience. That said, I’d absolutely spend the extra $20 at purchase to go without them, especially seeing as this blocks the ability to set a lockscreen wallpaper at all. It doesn’t cost anything more to remove them later, though, with a $20 charge through your Amazon account removing ads.

But what’s important here is that, for the fire time, Amazon is admitting defeat and installing the Google Play Store.

Alexa Tablet 8, and the rest of the lineup, has Google Play out of the box and access to any app you want. This means that you can, if you choose, fully ignore Amazon’s own services and do whatever you want. The smallest of examples: While Amazon’s built-in keyboard is fine, it’s wonderful that I don’t have to jump through hoops to switch over to Gboard. Fire Tablets were always an enticing value with a big catch, but Amazon’s change in this regard truly makes Alexa Tablets an attractive option for everyone – not just those willing to put up with the restriction or put in the work to hack Google apps on board.

Performance, despite the meager specs, is not awful. MediaTek’s low-end chip has just enough horsepower for general use, and 4GB of RAM isn’t as detrimental as I would have expected. It’s not fast by any means, but you could do a lot worse. There are 4GB Android tablets I’ve literally decided not to review because I couldn’t use them past the setup process, so the fact that Amazon is delivering a completely usable experience is a win in my book, even more so that I don’t actually mind using it. There’s a caveat to that, of course, and it’s that you shouldn’t expect to do a ton on this base model. For light browsing, reading Kindle books (or whatever other reading app since, you know, Google Play), or watching videos, this is great. For games, you’ll want to look elsewhere (and expect to spend more).

Alexa Tablet 8 isn’t some shining example of a perfect budget tablet, but I think it really nails the fundamentals. At its usual $230~ starting price, it’s a plenty-good deal for those in the market for an affordable Android slate, but the Black Friday pricing of just $150 is all the better – I just wish the model with 8GB of RAM was also on sale.

Amazon’s pivot to Alexa Tablets is great – though I hope the company knows full well that everyone is still going to call these Fire Tablets or Kindles or whatever combination of brands that spring to mind. But that reputation might work in Amazon’s favor at the end of the day. While there’s a huge portion of the audience that’s been left frustrated for years at the lack of Play Store support on Fire Tablets, they’ve still been a market success for the most part, so this shift is just a win for everyone. Users get a better experience, Amazon gets to shuffle its priorities away from a dead app store, and the market as a whole gets an affordable piece of hardware that doesn’t suck.

#10545. Our favorite accessories to level up your gaming setup ahead of GTA VI

Looking for a headset or a pro-grade controller for your return to Vice City? We have some recs.

Looking for a headset or a pro-grade controller for your return to Vice City? We have some recs.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

If you buy something from a link, The Verge may earn a commission. See our ethics statement.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

With Grand Theft Auto VI set to finally launch tomorrow, November 19th, our long-awaited return to Vice City is nearly here. But after all this time, some of the gear you’ve used in San Andreas might feel out of place on the glamorous streets of Leonidas County. Luckily for you, we’ve gathered a few of our favorite Xbox and PlayStation 5 accessories to help your long gaming sessions run as smoothly as possible.

The 8BitDo Ultimate 3E — an upgraded pick for Xbox Series X and Series S consoles — is a very capable, comfortable controller with elite-level features, including a high polling rate and adjustable triggers. It’s on the expensive side at $149.99, but the customization and clean execution justify the cost. Read our review.

PlayStation 5 owners looking for a controller upgrade, meanwhile, might consider the DualSense Edge, Sony’s first-party pro-style controller. In addition to a variety of extra function buttons and paddles, the wireless gamepad sports adjustable trigger heights and swappable joysticks (in case the potentiometers start to drift). It’s a little bit heavier than Sony’s regular DualSense, and the battery life isn’t quite as good, but it offers customization you won’t find on the console’s default controller. Read our review.

Sony’s pro-style controller, the DualSense Edge, might suffer from shorter battery life than the standard DualSense model, but it features great software integration with the PlayStation 5 and plenty of customizable components.

Nothing’s worse than having to pause a heist so you can go hunt down a fresh set of AA batteries. The PowerA Duo Charging Station includes a rechargeable battery and replacement rear doors for two Xbox Series X / S or Xbox One controllers. Simply set them on the dock, and they’ll start charging.

PowerA’s PlayStation equivalent charges two DualSense Wireless Controllers simultaneously, with an included AC adapter, and comes with a two-year warranty.

The 2026 Roku Pro Series is the brand’s first OLED, and the 55-inch version retails for $999.99, which is on the low end of the price spectrum for OLED. Games feel richer and more immersive on OLED panels, thanks to their deeper black levels, which are the result of unlit pixels or sections of the screen. Each Pro Series panel also sports a 120Hz refresh rate, four HDMI 2.1 ports, and both low latency and variable refresh rates for gaming.

For a more premium offering, the Sony Bravia 8 II is one of our favorite screens for movies, with an engaging, immersive picture and excellent image processing. It has great gaming features, too, like a 120Hz refresh rate with variable refresh and an automatic low-latency mode. It’s not cheap, but the OLED makes for an excellent upgrade to your setup. Read our review.

Sony’s latest first-party headset, the $99 PlayStation Pulse Wireless, launches the same day as GTA VI. My colleague Cameron Faulkner recently went hands-on with the wireless headset ahead of its launch on November 19th, and came away impressed with its overall audio quality and comfort. The newest Pulse is also straightforward to set up and use, especially when paired with devices like the PlayStation Portal that support PlayStation Link, Sony’s proprietary low-latency audio connection. It also offers Bluetooth for use with other devices.

For Xbox gamers, it’s hard to beat the SteelSeries Arctis Nova 3X, which retails in multiple colors for $109.99 or less. The wireless headset is lightweight, comfortable, and compatible with a wide variety of other consoles and mobile devices as well, for those rare moments when you aren’t playing GTA VI.

If you’d rather the simplicity of a wired option, Sony’s InZone H6 Air skips the batteries and dongles, instead opting to plug straight into your controller. This open-back gaming headset is lightweight and comfortable, which is great for long hours and late nights. My colleague Cameron Faulkner described its sound as “spectacular,” with several equalizer options available for helping fine-tune the sound to your liking. Read our review.

Ikea’s Poäng chair balances affordability and comfort, with wide, supportive armrests that are just the right height for holding a controller. It comes in a variety of frame materials and cushion colors, too, making it easy to match your other furniture or console. This is my personal chair for console gaming, and I recently added a matching ottoman to the set for maximum relaxation.

#10543. Google Pixel officially re-releases App Lock in beta, Samsung Galaxy gets it too

It’s been a wild ride for Google Pixel’s App Lock feature, but the company is officially re-releasing the feature today, right alongside its debut on Samsung Galaxy devices.

The feature is still only in Android 17 QPR3 Beta 3 for Google Pixel devices, an update that should be released to the stable track in early 2027.

Pixel owners on stable Android 17 QPR2 should already have this! Why would Google make Pixel owners wait until March 2027 to get this?! Release it now to Pixel owners. 🤨

You can still access App Lock by long-pressing on an app icon and adding it to the app lock list.

In a post on Reddit, Google’s Community Engagement Manager details that App Lock on Google Pixel was removed for improvements before getting a full release:

…the product team decided to temporarily pull back the feature from the QPR2 Beta to allow for additional polish, thorough testing, and robust integration across all system surfaces.

That’s why Samsung’s One UI 9.5 beta release today also brings support for App Lock. The feature is built into Android 17 QPR2, seemingly the release that One UI 9.5 is based on, but Google Pixel devices aren’t getting it until QPR3. As far as release timelines go, this means that Samsung’s Galaxy S27 series will probably ship with this feature out of the box, where Pixel phones won’t get it until around March or so. One UI 9.5 won’t roll out for older Galaxy devices until May, if One UI 8.5 serves as an example.

#10542. Tech lawsuits defined 2026 — and lawmakers want more

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Congress is scrambling to provide legal recourse for deepfakes and online grooming.

Congress is scrambling to provide legal recourse for deepfakes and online grooming.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

With just a few weeks remaining, lawmakers are trying to pass new protections for victims of online harm before the end of 2026, targeting deepfake creators and online platforms that block users from filing lawsuits.

On Tuesday, members of a Senate Judiciary subcommittee hosted a hearing calling for the end of forced arbitration for online harms. Their immediate target was Roblox, which they accused of evading legal complaints about children allegedly groomed on the platform by enforcing its own forced arbitration clause against users who agreed to the terms as minors, in some cases even under the age of 10. Separately, a few blocks away, Reps. Alexandria Ocasio-Cortez (D-NY) and Laurel Lee (R-FL) brought hotel heiress Paris Hilton to Capitol Hill to advocate for the Defiance Act, which would let victims of nonconsensual intimate deepfakes sue their creators.

“The best thing we can do in terms of enforcement is we can open up the courthouse doors,” Sen. Josh Hawley (R-MO), who chairs the Judiciary subcommittee, told reporters after the Roblox hearing. “The more I see these companies evade regulations, the more I think there is nothing as powerful as a jury.” Hawley introduced a new bill with ranking member Dick Durbin (D-IL) to render arbitration agreements unenforceable for disputes over alleged child sexual exploitation or “child cyber injury,” which could include “reputational harm, mental suffering,” or death.

“The best thing we can do in terms of enforcement is we can open up the courthouse doors”

Roblox has sought to enforce its arbitration clause in lawsuits it’s facing over allegations that users were groomed on the gaming platform as minors, despite the company’s claims of protections. Roblox is facing about 180 lawsuits in a consolidated case over its alleged role in failing to protect children. Under the arbitration process, the public would not get to see legal discovery like the kind that’s come to light under recent cases against Meta. Hawley called the jury system “corruption-proof” and “an incredibly big hammer.”

Roblox has denied that it’s trying to dodge accountability. “Safety has been central to Roblox since we launched. We require age checks for chat, place users under 16 in age-based accounts with protective defaults, and block images and video in chat,” Roblox’s global head of public policy Nicky Jackson Colaço said in a statement. “A legal position about arbitration is not a measure of a company’s safety record, and a hearing built around it fails to advance safety.”

Congress has only a little more than three more weeks left in session before the end of the year, and a new Democratic majority will be sworn in come January. Republicans are expected to do their best to push through President Donald Trump’s nominees for judgeships and executive branch posts like FCC commissioner in the lame-duck session, as well as try to pass year-end bills like the National Defense Authorization Act, which often includes a range of other policy items. But some lawmakers are holding out hope that they can open up courthouses to new litigation over deepfake abuse and child predation.

“You will be amazed by how quickly the company will change its behavior once it is sued in public court”

The Defiance Act would let victims of nonconsensual intimate deepfakes sue their creators for damages, in an attempt to raise the cost of creating these kinds of images. Hilton visited Washington earlier this year to share how an intimate non-deepfake video of her at age 19 shared without her consent impacted her life, and she used her story to advocate for the Defiance Act. At Tuesday’s press conference, she said she would come back to Congress as many times as necessary for the bill to become law. “Some people might have thought that was the end of my fight,” Hilton said. “But when I make a promise to survivors, I keep it.”

Hilton said over 50 million people have been victimized by sexually explicit deepfakes. “For me personally, strangers have created over 100,000 AI-generated explicit images and videos portraying me in the most graphic and disturbing ways imaginable,” Hilton said. “If this can happen to someone with my resources and my platform, imagine how powerless a young girl might feel facing this completely alone.”

Hilton’s comments were followed by Elliston Berry, an advocate who said a classmate made an explicit deepfake image of her friend group “from an innocent social media post” when she was 14, and distributed it throughout her school. “In a matter of seconds, my sense of security disappeared,” she said. “For me, the Defiance Act means recognition that victims deserve to be heard, that their experience matters, and that their right to seek justice should not depend on how quickly technology advances.”

#10540. Motorola Razr Fold finally gets its Android 17 update – hopefully the next 7 years go faster

Motorola has kicked off its Android 17 rollout for the Razr Fold, marking the first major update for the company’s first book-style foldable, and the start of a potentially interest 7 years of software support.

The Motorola Razr Fold launched with Android 16 back in May 2026, and it remains a really good option for those looking to pick up a book-style foldable in the US. On top of solid specs across the board, it also carries the promise of 7 years of software support, something that few other Moto devices can tout.

But nowhere was it promises that Motorola would speed things up at all. A little over 3 months after Motorola announced the update and several weeks after other Motorola devices were updated to Android 17, the Motorola Razr Fold is finally getting its own Android 17 update.

Rolling out now, Android 17 for the Razr Fold is a 4.15GB~ update that appears to be available at least somewhat broadly. Reports are popping up on Reddit including one report from the EU (Hungary, specifically), with another user apparently in a Spanish-speaking region also seeing the update.

We’re still waiting to see if the update is available in the US (there’s a backlog of updates on our unit), but it’s certainly an update to look forward to. Apparently, Motorola finally fixed a weird UI issue that affected the Telegram app, something we ran into when using the device earlier this year.

It’s great to see the update out, but it’s noteworthy that Motorola is not setting a particularly encouraging precedent here. Razr Fold is one of the company’s first devices to be promised 7 years of OS updates and software support, and its flagship-tier status certainly would have most expecting it to be updated ahead of some of Motorola’s more affordable, lower-tier devices that were updated over a month ago. Whatever the case, hopefully Motorola will speed things up over the next few years, especially with its $1,600 flagship Signature 27 on the horizon with the same software promises.

#10537. Top 6 AI Detector Tools in 2026: Features, Accuracy Claims and When to Trust Them

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

AI writing tools are now part of everyday work for students, marketers, developers and editors, and a parallel category of tools has appeared to answer one question: was this text written by a person or a model? Teachers, publishers, hiring teams and content managers use these detectors to screen submissions, but the technology is probabilistic, its accuracy claims vary, and the cost of a wrong call can be high.

This guide compares six AI detector tools, CudekAI, GPTZero, Copyleaks, Originality.ai, Turnitin and Sapling, on what their own pages state. It then explains how detectors work, summarizes the research on their limits, and ends with a short script you can use to test any detector on your own samples. The tools are listed in no ranking order. Feature details, limits and prices change often, so confirm current information on each vendor’s site before you commit.

• A detector gives a probability, not proof. Every vendor in this list warns that results can be wrong, and several advise against using a score alone.

• Limits differ widely. Character limits, supported languages, models detected and free allowances vary between tools and even between pages of the same tool.

• Bias and short text are known weak spots. Research has found detectors misclassify some non-native English writing, and most vendors say accuracy improves with longer text.

• Vendor accuracy claims are not comparable. Figures come from different datasets, thresholds and models, so test on your own material.

• Use detection as one signal. Combine it with drafts, version history, interviews or other evidence before acting on a result.

CudekAI’s AI detector returns an AI percentage and a human percentage for pasted text, with paragraph, sentence and word-level analysis for mixed documents. According to its page, it also accepts PDF and Word files with per-page scores and downloadable reports, and offers separate code and image detectors. The page lists models it says it detects, including ChatGPT, Gemini, Claude, Llama, DeepSeek and Grok, and says it supports more than 100 languages.

Strengths: sentence-level highlighting, file uploads with reports, and extra detectors for code and images in the same product.

Limitations and gaps: the page shows different character limits in different places, including 1,000, 5,000 and 15,000, so check the limit that applies to your plan. It says to start free but does not state a free allowance, and it lists detection as costing one credit. A headline accuracy figure of 99 percent appears in its page metadata, while the body refers to independent benchmarking without naming sources, and its FAQ says accuracy may vary for mixed or paraphrased text and that false positives can occur. API access is listed under an enterprise plan.

Fits: writers and teams who want a quick check with sentence-level detail and file reports, and who will verify results rather than rely on a single percentage.

GPTZero gives an overall score and highlights flagged sentences, and adds a Writing Replay feature that records the writing process, including typing patterns and paste events. It also lists a plagiarism checker and integrations with Google Docs, Google Classroom, Canvas, Zapier and a Chrome extension, plus an API.

Strengths: process-based evidence through Writing Replay, strong education integrations and published benchmark figures. On the RAID benchmark, the page reports 95.7 percent of AI text detected and 1 percent of human text wrongly flagged.

Limitations and gaps: it claims full support for five languages (English, German, Portuguese, French and Spanish) and says other languages lack stated accuracy levels. The company’s own page says no detector is 100 percent accurate, that results should not be the final verdict, and that accuracy is higher on longer English text. The free scan box has a 10,000-character limit, and plan prices are not listed on the page.

Fits: educators and editors who want writing-process evidence as well as a score.

Copyleaks describes an AI detector covering more than 30 languages and models including ChatGPT, Claude, Gemini, DeepSeek and Llama. Its AI Logic feature explains why text was flagged, and it says it can detect AI-generated code. It offers a browser extension, a Google Docs add-on, learning-management-system integrations and an enterprise API.

Strengths: explanation of flagged text, code detection, broad integrations and published per-language accuracy figures, which range from 93.08 percent for Portuguese to 99.20 percent for English on AI text, according to the page.

Limitations and gaps: the figures are the vendor’s own. It claims a 0.03 percent false-positive rate and cites a 2023 study, so treat the number as a claim to verify on your data. Free scans cover up to 25,000 characters, but paid plan prices sit on a separate pricing page.

Fits: organizations that need multilingual coverage, code detection or integrations.

Originality.ai combines AI detection with a plagiarism checker and a fact and hallucination checker. It lists sentence-level highlights, a mixed AI and human category in its newer detection models, a Chrome extension with a writing replay feature and an AI detection API.

Strengths: several checks in one product, an API with documentation and a clear statement of limits. The page acknowledges false positives, says very short text under a 100-word minimum and AI rewriting tools can affect results, and advises against using the score alone.

Limitations and gaps: it is credit-based, free scans are capped at 100 credits per account and credits expire two years after purchase. Its accuracy claims cite third-party studies and internal benchmarks that you should review yourself.

Fits: publishers, agencies and SEO teams that want detection, plagiarism and fact checks in one workflow.

Turnitin is built for educators and institutions. Its AI writing detection estimates how much of a submission is human-written versus likely AI-generated, including likely AI-paraphrased content, and works inside learning-management systems alongside similarity checking.

Strengths: deep institutional integration and unusually explicit framing of results as probabilities. For documents above its 300-word minimum, the page reports false-positive rates of 0.014 for English language learners and 0.013 for native English writers and concludes there is no statistically significant bias. It also links guidance on handling suspected false positives.

Limitations and gaps: it is aimed at institutions rather than individual freelancers, the page does not give an overall false-positive figure, and it describes detection as just one piece of a broader challenge. Mention of language support is limited, with a linked post about Arabic.

Fits: schools and universities that already use Turnitin and want detection within the same workflow.

Sapling offers an AI content detector with a free check of the first 2,000 characters, and Pro and Enterprise accounts can check up to 100,000 characters. It provides a free API key and documentation, and it publishes unusually candid guidance on limits.

Strengths: transparent caveats and a developer-friendly API. The page says false positives and negatives may occur, that accuracy varies with dataset, text length and threshold, and advises measuring results on your own examples. It also warns that some tools claiming to be undetectable falsely say they passed its detector.

Limitations and gaps: accuracy improves after about 50 words, non-English text is scored by the English model for unsupported languages and is described as less reliable, and prices are not listed on the page. A reported 60 percent drop in false positives comes from internal evaluations that the page does not publish.

Fits: developers and teams that want to integrate detection and test it on their own data.

The table summarizes what each page states. It is not a benchmark, and “not stated” means the page did not say, not that the feature is absent.

• False positives. Human writing can be flagged, especially when it is short, formulaic or highly predictable.

• Bias risk. A research paper titled GPT detectors are biased against non-native English writers found that the detectors it tested consistently misclassified non-native English writing as AI-generated, and warned against using such detectors in educational settings. Turnitin’s page reports the opposite for its own tool, which is a reason to test on your population.

• Evasion. Paraphrasing or editing AI text can lower scores. The same research found that simple prompting strategies could both reduce bias and get text past detectors.

• Mixed authorship. A document that is partly AI-assisted can get a low overall score while individual sentences score high, as Sapling’s page points out.

• Moving targets. New models change the text distribution, so a tool trained on older models can lose accuracy.

• Shifting benchmarks. Vendors report different datasets and thresholds, so numbers are not comparable.

OpenAI’s own experience is a useful reminder. Its AI text classifier correctly flagged 26 percent of AI-written text on a challenge set while mislabeling 9 percent of human text, and the page states that the classifier was withdrawn on July 20, 2023 due to its low rate of accuracy. Detection has improved since, but the underlying difficulty has not gone away.

Most detectors are classifiers trained on large collections of human-written and machine-generated text. They look for statistical patterns, such as how predictable the word choices are or how uniform the sentence structure is, and output a probability. Some systems add features such as sentence-level scoring, writing-process data, or checks for hidden provenance signals.

• Statistical signals. Text that a language model finds highly predictable tends to look machine-like, which is why formulaic human writing can be misflagged.

• Trained classifiers. Detectors learn from labeled examples and update as new models appear.

• Writing-process evidence. Tools such as Writing Replay record how a document was typed or pasted, which is stronger evidence than a style score.

• Provenance. Standards such as C2PA Content Credentials aim to record the origin and edits of content when the creating tool supports them, which is different from guessing after the fact.

Because vendor figures are not comparable, the most reliable approach is to measure. Build a small labeled set that reflects your real content, including human writing from different groups of authors, and calculate false-positive and false-negative rates by group. The script below wraps any detector behind a single function. The stub is a placeholder, so replace it with a call to the tool you are evaluating and follow that vendor’s API documentation.

is_ai: bool        # ground truth you control

group: str = "all" # e.g. "native", "non-native", "technical"

def evaluate(detect, samples, threshold=0.5):

"""detect(text) -> probability the text is AI-generated (0..1).

Wrap any vendor API or tool export in this function."""

g = stats.setdefault(s.group, {"fp": 0, "human": 0, "fn": 0, "ai": 0})

g["fn"] += not flagged          # AI text that slipped through

g["fp"] += flagged              # human text wrongly flagged

fpr = g["fp"] / g["human"] if g["human"] else float("nan")

fnr = g["fn"] / g["ai"] if g["ai"] else float("nan")

print(f"{group:12} false positive rate {fpr:.1%}  false negative rate {fnr:.1%}")

return 0.9 if "delve" in text.lower() else 0.2

Sample("Let us delve into the topic.", True, "native"),

Sample("I wrote this note by hand.", False, "native"),

Sample("We delve into methods used here.", False, "non-native"),

Sample("The results were reviewed twice.", True, "non-native"),

With only a handful of samples the numbers mean little, so use a larger, representative set and report results per group, because an acceptable overall rate can hide a high rate for one group.

• Never treat a score as proof. Use it to start a conversation, not end one.

• Gather corroborating evidence. Drafts, version history, writing-process data and discussion with the author carry more weight than a percentage.

• Tell people how detection is used. Publish a policy so authors know what to expect and how to appeal.

• Check for bias. Test on writers like those you evaluate, including non-native speakers.

A tool that estimates how likely a piece of text is to have been generated by an AI model, usually as a percentage, often with sentence-level highlighting.

They can be useful, but accuracy depends on the text, length, language and threshold, and every vendor in this guide states that errors can occur. Test on your own samples.

Yes. False positives are acknowledged by vendors and documented in research, particularly for short or formulaic text and some non-native English writing.

Free tiers are fine for a quick check, but they have limits on length and features, and the result should not be the sole basis for a decision.

Sometimes. Paraphrasing and editing can lower scores, and partly AI-generated documents can produce mixed results.

A plagiarism checker looks for matches with existing sources. An AI detector estimates whether text resembles machine-generated writing, which is a different and less certain task.

AI detectors are useful screening tools, but none is a verdict. The six reviewed here differ in language coverage, limits, integrations, explainability and how candidly their pages describe error, and their accuracy figures come from different tests. Compare them on the factors that matter for your content, measure false positives on your own samples, and combine any score with other evidence before acting on it.

#10535. The leaked Lenovo Legion Go Beam could be the affordable handheld we need

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

It looks comparatively cheap — and that might be a good thing in this economy.

It looks comparatively cheap — and that might be a good thing in this economy.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

For a while there, it seemed like Lenovo had given up on selling handheld gaming PCs, hiking the price of its Legion Go 2 and Legion Go S far higher than other RAM-starved competitors. A decent one can easily cost $1,000 if not $2,000 now. But it looks like Lenovo might be about to announce a relatively cheap handheld once again— the Legion Go Beam.

Windows Latest has leaked images of the handheld, and there’s no question it’s designed to be a value alternative to even the company’s least expensive Legion Go S variant. While it’s got the same big stippled grips and triggers for comfort, there’s only one USB-C port on the top instead of two, a comparatively cheap-looking D-pad, no touchpad, and only a single tiny intake and exhaust for its fan.

There’s no SteamOS logo where that would go, so it likely runs Windows instead… but then there’s that name, “Beam.” Perhaps you’ll “beam” your games to it because it’s a streaming and cloud gaming handheld, instead of one that primarily plays games on its own chip? Maybe that’s why it doesn’t need as much airflow as previous handhelds?

Perhaps there’s a partnership with Xbox Cloud Gaming specifically, and that’s why it’s all lit up in Xbox green?

But it might just be green because it looks nice with white, which has been the “value” color for handhelds so far (see: ROG Ally, Xbox Ally, and the Windows-based Legion Go S). And it might be “Beam” because there’s a literal beam of light on the bottom, even though the first thing I do with most handhelds is turn any and all distracting RGB lighting off. Or maybe it’s “Beam” just because it sounds like a friendly, fun, value-oriented name, instead of something “S” or “Pro.”

I won’t begin to speculate on the Legion Go Beam’s price, except to say that “cheap” may be relative. The Legion Go S was originally supposed to cost $499, but wound up costing far more except on the occasional sale. The Legion Go 2 was supposed to start at $1,099 but wound up costing $1,500 and up after RAMageddon. Streaming-first handhelds have been far cheaper, though, like the $249 Sony PlayStation Portal.

Lenovo has a history of announcing its new gaming devices at CES in January each year, including previous Legion Go handhelds, so I’m expecting to see an official announcement of the Lenovo Legion Go Beam then. Even if so, that doesn’t mean it’ll be available in January — there’s often a many-month lag.
