Intel has reduced its workforce by approximately 27,800 employees under Lip-Bu Tan, based on the company's reported headcount figures, but this does not seem to be enough. The company has reportedly initiated another round of layoffs, this time across its global information technology (IT) organization, as its chief executive continues to reduce expenses and simplify the company's operations, reports Business Insider.

The latest cuts in Intel's IT organization reportedly began on November 16, although Intel has not disclosed how many employees will lose their jobs, notes TrendForce citing CTech. Some employees claim that the reductions could affect a double-digit percentage of the department's workforce of around 2,607 people at 55 sites in 53 countries, so we are talking about at least 260 people if we are talking about a 10% reduction, or as much as 520 if we are talking about a 20% reduction. One has to keep in mind that these estimates have not been independently verified. Intel has neither confirmed the number of affected employees nor identified the specific teams involved. Instead, the company reiterated to Business Insider that it regularly reviews its workforce to ensure that personnel and financial resources correspond to its strategic priorities.

The latest reductions follow several rounds of layoffs throughout 2026. In July, Intel eliminated positions in its Data Center and AI Group. Between July and October, the company submitted nine Worker Adjustment and Retraining Notification (WARN) notices in California, which covered 222 employees, according to Business Insider. The most recent notice, filed on October 22, concerned 67 permanent job cuts at Intel's Folsom campus (which mostly specializes in R&D, hardware validation, and software development), effective October 31. As of November 17, no additional WARN notices had been reported.

Intel employed 81,100 people as of September 27, 2026, including 76,500 employees at Intel itself and another 4,600 at Mobileye and other subsidiaries. This compares with 85,100 employees at the end of 2025 and 108,900 a year earlier, which means that the company's workforce declined by 4,000 since December, 2025, and 27,800 since December, 2024. respectively.

Intel's workforce reductions began well before Lip-Bu Tan became chief executive in 2025. In 2024, his predecessor Pat Gelsinger announced plans to eliminate more than 15,000 positions, or approximately 15% of the company’s workforce. Tan has continued to reduce expenses, eliminate management layers, and discontinue lower-priority projects as Intel attempts to cut costs and reduce losses/improve profitability.

#10530. Grand Theft Auto VI understands just how important a smartphone is

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Text messages, social networking, selfies — the device is integrated into every aspect of your virtual life.

Text messages, social networking, selfies — the device is integrated into every aspect of your virtual life.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Grand Theft Auto VI, in many ways, tries to simulate real life. And life in 2026 would not be complete without a smartphone. GTA VI understands this and makes the phone not just a central part of the experience, but an object that exists within the world. You may try to escape into a fantasy of robbing banks and fighting drug lords, but you can’t get away from texts from your mom or nagging fitness app notifications.

Seeing a smartphone in a game isn’t a new phenomenon. The device has become a natural stand-in for the main menu in many games, giving players an interface that feels intuitive, because a phone is something they use every day. But the phone in GTA VI is different for a few reasons. One is that it behaves much like any other object in the game, whether it’s a gun or a bottle of beer. The action doesn’t pause when you use your phone. Instead, the game switches to a first-person viewpoint, where you can still walk around — or drive, if you’re feeling ambitious — while reading texts or flipping through a fictional version of Instagram.

Rob Nelson, the co-studio head at developer Rockstar North and producer on GTA VI, says that it would’ve felt “archaic” to have a static phone that worked like those in other games, or the one in GTA V, which appeared as a pop-up at the bottom of the screen across from the minimap. “We felt that it was important to make it physical so you have to look at it and put your attention on it, including while driving,” he explains. “It’s about finding the line between realism and player convenience. But the act of being lost in your phone felt like something worth doing, even though it could mean slightly more inconvenience. In this game you are committing to using it.” (Of course, texting and driving, like much of what you do in GTA VI, is both very dangerous and very illegal.)

You have to interact with the phone in the game constantly. Often the story plays out through phone calls and text messages, and you have a surprisingly large array of apps to use that serve a real purpose in the game. You manage your money in a banking app, use a rideshare app when you need a lift, track your workouts in a fitness app, scroll through a social network complete with comments and the ability to like posts, and sideload an AI tool that scans nearby cars so you can see how easy they’ll be to steal. You can even change the wallpaper and ringtone. (GTA VI has two main characters that you swap between, and each has their own phone, though they’re functionally identical.)

What’s most impressive is how all of these apps interact with each other. For instance, after pulling off a major heist, a friend might text you a news story about it, asking “was this you!?” You can then click the link, open up the news app, and read an actual article about what went down. These kinds of interactions can be functional, like when another character texts you a location, which you can then add to your map so the GPS can guide you there. Other times, they add texture to the world or depth to the characters. GTA VI is a love story about two criminals named Jason and Lucia, and you play as both. At one point, while controlling Lucia, I got a text asking for a selfie. I opened up the camera, shot a pic of Lucia drinking a latte, and sent it over. Jason replied immediately — and their relationship got a boost.

Phones are so fully integrated into the game that they can even interrupt the characters’ sleep. At the end of each day, you can click on a bed to rest for the night. But neither Jason nor Lucia will instantly fall asleep when you do — instead, they’ll sit down and pull out their phone, and you have the option to doomscroll or catch up on texts instead of going to sleep right away. Nelson says that this was implemented because the developers wanted to include the ability for the characters to sit on the bed at night, much like a real couple in a shared living space might do. “We needed something to do aside from just sitting there staring at nothing,” he says.

If nothing else, GTA VI’s phone shows how alike we all are: Even bank robbers being hunted by the police can’t seem to stop scrolling. “Phones are a constant part of our lives,” Nelson says of the evolution, “even more than they were in 2013.”

#10525. How Much Latency Does a Self-Hosted LLM Gateway Add? Langfuse vs LiteLLM vs AcruxCore

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

A self-hosted LLM gateway sits between your app and the model provider, so every call pays for one extra hop. We measured that cost for three open-source gateways: the Langfuse AI Gateway, the LiteLLM proxy and AcruxCore. Each one ran on the same machine, in front of the same OpenAI model. LiteLLM added 61 to 67 ms to every non-streaming call. AcruxCore added 15 to 24 ms. Langfuse's number could not be told apart from zero.

The bigger lesson was about the measurement itself. Before we controlled for it, one OpenAI network address moved our results by more than any gateway did.

Each number is the median time a gateway added to a direct OpenAI call, with its 95% interval in brackets. A gateway can log each call, and logging takes time, so we ran the test three times with three logging levels.

The direct call took 781 to 831 ms at the median, so even LiteLLM's cost is under 9% of a short call. Read the tables this way:

The obvious test is to time 100 calls through the gateway, time 100 direct calls, and compare the two medians. That test gives the wrong answer, for two reasons.

The first reason is noise. OpenAI's own response time moved by hundreds of milliseconds between calls in our runs. The direct call's p95 was 1,047 to 1,233 ms against a median near 800 ms. A gateway that adds 20 ms is hidden inside that spread unless the test removes it.

The second reason is the network path to OpenAI. It does not stay the same between a direct client and a gateway, and that difference can be larger than the gateway itself.

api.openai.com resolves to more than one address. From our network, DNS returned two Cloudflare addresses, and one was much farther away than the other. We measured both with a script that pins one address at a time:

On a connection that is already open, both addresses answer in the same time. On a new connection, the far address adds about 250 ms, because the TCP and TLS handshakes take that much longer.

A gateway keeps its connection to OpenAI open between calls. It pays the setup once and then stays on whichever address it reached first. A client that opens a new connection for each call pays the setup again on every call. How much it pays depends on which address DNS returns that time.

Node's built-in fetch closes an idle connection after about 4 seconds. So a test client that calls OpenAI every few seconds reconnects on almost every call. In our first, uncontrolled run, the Langfuse gateway came out 43 ms faster than calling OpenAI directly. A gateway cannot be faster than the call it forwards. The direct client paid for new connections, and the gateway did not.

The fix has two parts. First, pin api.openai.com to one address on every path. In Docker, that is an extra_hosts entry on each gateway container:

Second, let the direct client keep its connection open, as a gateway does. With undici, the HTTP client behind Node's fetch, that is a custom Agent:

We then listed each container's open connections to confirm that every path used the same address. The edge-check script is in the repository linked at the end, and it takes about a minute to run from your own network.

This advice also applies outside benchmarks. If your app calls OpenAI directly from Node, keep the connection open between calls. Otherwise some of your calls pay the handshake, and on a far address that costs about 250 ms.

Each test sent 150 rounds after 5 warm-up rounds. One round calls OpenAI directly and through each gateway, once without streaming and once with streaming. The order rotates every round, so no path always goes first or last.

For each round, the script takes the gateway's time minus the direct call's time in that same round. Calls in one round are made within a few seconds of each other, so a slow moment at OpenAI hits all four paths together and cancels out of the difference. The script then reports the median of the 150 differences.

The interval comes from a bootstrap. The script draws 150 differences at random, with replacement, and takes their median. It repeats that 2,000 times, and the middle 95% of those medians is the interval. This is a shortened version of the script's function:

An interval that includes zero means the test could not separate the gateway's cost from zero. That is what the Langfuse rows show. It does not prove that the cost is zero.

Every call used OpenAI gpt-6-luna with reasoning_effort: "none", the same short prompt and a cap of 5 output tokens. A short answer keeps the model's own time small, so more of the measured time belongs to the path. Each streamed call measured the time to its first token. Each non-streamed call measured the time to the full response.

All three gateways did real work on every call, which made the comparison fair:

The three logging levels mapped to these settings:

The machine was an AMD Ryzen 9 5900X with 62 GB of RAM, running Fedora Linux and Docker 29.9. All three stacks ran on it at once, and the test was run on 10 November 2026.

LiteLLM added 61 to 67 ms to the full response, but only 9 to 43 ms to the first streamed token. The first token arrives as soon as the model starts to answer, so time spent before that point shows up in both numbers. The larger cost without streaming suggests that most of LiteLLM's extra time is spent after the model has answered. We did not profile LiteLLM to confirm this.

Logging level made no clear difference for any gateway. Adding LiteLLM's Langfuse callback did not raise its cost. AcruxCore's median rose from 15 ms with logging off to 24 ms with full logging, but its intervals overlap across all three runs. AcruxCore's median is 16 to 24 ms above Langfuse's, and those intervals overlap too, so this test does not rank the two of them.

If 60 ms per call matters to you, as in a chat app that makes several model calls per user message, LiteLLM's cost is real and repeatable. Langfuse and AcruxCore stayed near or within the noise. If your calls take several seconds, any of the three costs less than OpenAI's own variation from one call to the next.

Before you trust a gateway number, your own or anyone else's, check two things. Was each gateway compared with a direct call in the same round? And did the direct client keep its connection open on the same OpenAI address as the gateway? Without both, the result measures the network path and not the gateway.

The scripts, Compose files and raw results are on GitHub: scripts/comparison/langfuse-gateway-vs-litellm-vs-acruxcore.

Talha Anwar is a senior AI and full-stack engineer and writes about building and running AI systems.

#10523. The wait begins for GTA VI on PC

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Given how good the game is on console, the wait will almost certainly be worth it.

Given how good the game is on console, the wait will almost certainly be worth it.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Grand Theft Auto VI is finally here for millions of players who own a PlayStation 5 or Xbox Series X / S, and those who plan to buy a console, despite rising costs this year, just to play it. As you have likely already noticed, the launch is inescapable, and that’s especially tough if you’re a PC gamer.

I’m sorry to remind you that the clock has only just now started ticking down to the unannounced yet inevitable release of the PC version. Good luck with avoiding Twitch and browsing the internet while trying to remain oblivious to the violent (and romantic) delights that await you.

Seeing Leonida in true 4K at an unlocked frame rate with full ray-tracing effects and extended draw distances, among other graphical improvements, might be a thing you can do on your PC as early as sometime in Q1 2027, if recent reports are accurate. Regardless, early 2027 is not right now. Speaking as someone with a capable gaming desktop, I’m sad to say that FOMO has already gotten the best of me. I bought GTA VI on the PS5 and will probably double-dip and buy it again on PC, just like I did with GTA V and Red Dead Redemption 2. The psychology of me, a weak-willed consumer, is one that publisher Rockstar Games and really any other company in its position would be happy to exploit.

The PC version of GTA VI will launch, eventually, in a very different landscape than the PC ports of Red Dead Redemption 2 and GTA V did. Despite the recent downturn in PC sales driven by increased RAM prices, there are considerably more players on PC now than there were a decade ago. SteamDB’s charts show that Steam’s concurrent users alone have more than doubled (from around 20 million to over 40 million now) since 2020.

A chunk of those people are likely made up of console expats who decided to migrate to PC for various reasons. Some wanted to experience the fun of building their own desktop, while some were mainly convinced by the fact that you can get more power for your money with a PC than with a PlayStation 5 Pro or Xbox Series X. For a subset of people, the convenience of getting something like a Steam Deck to bring your PC game library with you anywhere more than makes up for its lack of power. There are many other justifiable reasons for making PC your main gaming platform, like getting faster access to discounts on digital games, not paying for online multiplayer, ultrawide monitor resolutions, being able to buy games from one of several platforms, and having one device that you can use for work and play.

Rockstar seems poised to deliver the definitive version of GTA VI on PC on its own terms, and I’m interested to see what the end result will be. In addition to building the best-looking version of the game made to run well across countless configurations, its PC optimization efforts are likely also more focused than before on ensuring decent performance on lower-end hardware (including PC handhelds). With a 200-plus-gigabyte install size on consoles, I dread how big it’ll be on PC.

Ideally, the PC version will launch alongside an improved version of GTA Online (whether it’s free to play is anyone’s guess), and maybe with upgraded GTA roleplay tech to support it. I’m hopeful that the PC version of GTA VI will come in early 2027, but if the numerous delays of the console versions were any indication, the developer will take all the time it needs to get it right.

Update, 9:55AM ET, November 19th: The SteamDB data cited now specifies that it’s concurrent users, not the number of users total.

#10521. AI data center power demand will double by 2030 thanks to $4 trillion in spending, adding an entire Great Britain to energy strain

Reviews, benchmarks, and updates on current GPUs.

Join for quick access. Enter your email below and we'll send confirmation plus sign you up to our newsletter.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

The real bottleneck isn't AI chips, but power grids.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

The AI data center build-out is pushing electricity grids across the globe, with many regions facing demand from projects that would exceed the current peak load that each system could support. According to the International Energy Agency’s (IEA) World Energy Outlook 2026 report, a total of 120GW of capacity is currently available across all types of data centers across the globe, with another 120GW expected to be completed by the end of the decade, 75% of which would be dedicated to artificial intelligence. Aside from that, it’s also tracking 93 large “AI factories” that are either under construction or being expanded via satellite imagery. It estimates that the capacity for these sites grew from under 2GW to 17GW in just two years, and that they’re expected to hit a total of 50GW upon completion, the equivalent of adding a second Great Britain to power demand.

This massive power consumption is one of the reasons for the opposition against their construction in communities, with residents blaming them for increased utility prices. However, the IEA says that its analysis “does not yet show a clear relationship between growth in data center capacity and increases in electricity prices.” It noted that if a data center uses existing capacity and pays for its share of the cost, then it could potentially lead to lower utility bills for all customers. However, if the grid operator is required to upgrade its system to accommodate the large consumer, then it could also lead to increased monthly bills if the costs are spread across all users.

Emissions are another issue that the IEA has raised. Renewable energy dominates data centers’ power purchase agreements at nearly 50%, but actual power generation puts coal- and natural-gas power sources closer to 60%. This is likely due to the need for reliability, with the latter kicking in when the former cannot deliver the power that the data center needs. Aside from that, a few projects are deploying their own power, like Elon Musk’s Colossus 2 in Southaven, Mississippi, with about 180GW planned or under construction in the U.S. So, even though renewable energy is growing to accommodate demand, it’s also expected that coal- and natural-gas emissions from data centers will rise by 70% by 2030.

Investments in AI data centers are expected to hit $32 trillion by 2050, putting the infrastructure spending for the industry larger than railways, electrification, and the internet, combined. Despite that, the IEA says the grid remains a significant bottleneck — renewable power plants take between one and five years to complete, which is around the same time that some massive data center projects take if they do not face an extended moratorium, while transmission works can take three to five times longer. This is probably one of the reasons why some AI hyperscalers are choosing to put jet engines on their sites instead, although these are also facing shortages, especially in turbine blades.

Analogue has long been good at expanding the features and functionality of its consoles through regular firmware updates, but installing those updates has always required moving files around with a memory card. Once the update is installed, the Wi-Fi setup process will begin automatically when the console is booted, but there’s now an added Wi-Fi section in the Analogue 3D’s settings menu for updating passwords and other details later.

#10516. You can now buy a Steam Machine without getting on a waitlist

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Valve’s 512GB Steam Machine is available to buy now, but the 2TB model is out of stock.

Valve’s 512GB Steam Machine is available to buy now, but the 2TB model is out of stock.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

If you buy something from a link, The Verge may earn a commission. See our ethics statement.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Valve announced some updates about Steam Machine availability today, including good news if you want to buy one right now and potential bad news about future supply due to RAMaggedon.

The good news: You can now purchase a 512GB Steam Machine without getting on a waitlist. Initially, interested buyers had to get on Valve’s waitlist if they wanted to purchase one, but the console-like PC is now available in an “add-to-cart” status, according to the company. As I write this, the 512GB versions with and without a bundled controller are showing estimated delivery dates of three to five business days on Valve’s website. If Valve sells through its current inventory, the company says it will indicate that the product is out of stock instead of making another waitlist.

The bad news: If you’re on the waitlist for a 2TB model, you might not get a chance to buy one at all, as Valve says there are more people on the waitlist than there are units available. The company is closing the waitlists for its 2TB models.

Valve says that while it’s still building more Steam machines, “memory and storage costs continue to increase at unprecedented levels” and that it doesn’t know the number it can make or “at what price we’ll be able to offer them.” The 2TB Steam Machine is “especially constrained.” The company anticipates “having more information” to share about additional supply in January.

After delaying the Steam Machine’s expected early 2026 launch due to the memory and storage crisis, Valve launched the product in June with a price starting at $1,049 for the 512GB model without a bundled controller. A 2TB model without a controller costs $1,349.

#10512. Find Orphan Pages and Buried Content: Build an Internal Link Graph for Your Site

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

The pages of a site form a graph. Think of it as many nodes connected to each other via edges. Each of the edges are the links between the pages, and the nodes themselves are the pages. Now if you start to visualize this as a graph, you will quickly see that there are multiple ways to traverse this graph and reach a particular endpoint.

A search crawler usually uses one of these pages as a starting point and crawls through all of the pages (especially when pages are not fully covered in a sitemap).

If a page lies very deep from the home page, it means that it's not an important page. It's an indication to the search engine that this is not something that you yourself consider important, and the search engine has a lesser incentive to rank this page over pages that are linked better within your site.

The key metric here is link depth. That is the number of clicks it takes to reach a particular page from your home page.

Another key metric is broken links. If you have too many broken links, it indicates to the search engine that your site is outdated and not well maintained, and the search engine will then not want to rank your site.

The way to identify both these problems is the same: to actually walk through your site, do a crawl of your site outside in, and try to figure out what the link depth is and if there are broken links on your website.

Seed the crawl with the home page and all URLs mentioned in the sitemap. Technically, the sitemap is not required if you're doing a crawl, but going from the sitemap will allow us to detect orphaned pages as well.

Fetch the pages and follow redirects as required. Record when you're following a redirect: we do not let fetch follow it silently, we record each hop and add its target to the crawl. Record HTTP errors as they occur.

Drop URL fragments and tracking parameters. Keep trailing slashes because sometimes there can be mismatches around expectation of trailing slashes in different URLs of a site.

In order to navigate from the home page, we can use BFS. So we know that the first time we reach a page, that is the page's shortest path.

We can cut off the depth over a certain threshold, say 5, to indicate that pages beyond that are too far or too deep to be reached.

Noindex pages will still be crawled. However, we need to make a record of them being not indexed, to reflect that they will not be present in the search engine.

The desired page depth is as close to the home page as possible. Important pages should be close to the home page. The deeper you go from the home page, it signals to the search engine that the page is less important.

A couple of examples of how a deep page is present:

The suggested fix for this is that we link the pages from as close to the home page as possible:

Wasted links are those internal links that do not do the job they're supposed to do. The different types of wasted links:

We have seen here how to look at our own website through the lens of a search engine. You can use some of the code over here to create your own crawler and identify internal linking issues; the full script is in this gist. Alternatively, we can use a free internal link visualizer to identify these issues and focus only on the fixes to improve our site's internal linking.

#10511. New Windows 11 lock screen zero-day unlocks BitLocker-protected PCs with just a few keystrokes

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Considering the current status of the cybersecurity landscape, some may well say that "0-day" has lost its meaning, The latest high-stakes vulnerability pertains to a bug in Windows 11 (and possibly 10), wherein an attacker can partially bypass the lock screen and run software as the logged-in user, and seemingly access their data. If true, the vulnerability means even BitLocker won't help keep users' files protected.

Unsurprisingly, the bug was discovered by Abdelhamid Naceri, the prolific Windows hacker known to the world as Nightmare Eclipse, and is responsible for about a dozen zero-day findings in Microsoft's operating system. The exploit requires physical access to the machine, but that's precisely the scenario that lock screens and/or BitLocker are supposed to protect against.

To trigger the vulnerability, all you need is a keyboard. In the Windows 11 lock screen, click "Forgot my PIN," then hit Win+U to bring up the voice narrator. The window will be hidden behind the lock screen, which, for practical purposes, is just a veneer, but you can interact with it using the keyboard if you hold down the Caps Lock key and use the arrows and Enter.

After some brief navigation through Narrator, you'll actually be in Settings, and then have multiple avenues for further shenanigans. One obvious one is going into Bluetooth and Devices and setting Autoplay to show a USB stick's files and folders, then plugging in your own with your software of choice to run. Additionally, on a personal machine, the user is bound to have administrator permissions, so you can just create your own account.

This bug is quite fresh, so there isn't widespread reporting or testing. The author claims "the vulnerability seems to be extremely consistent in every machine I tested it in." We did encounter a sysadmin on Reddit, danrhodes1987, who claims they've tested it on machines they manage, and sure enough, it works, though only on their InTune-enrolled devices. He says that "you can launch anything you want in the context of the user logged onto the device."

Additionally, they found it possible to invisibly navigate whichever programs the user had open before they locked their screen, which all but guarantees that the victim's entire data and programs will be fully accessible. As a Windows sysadmin friend of mine remarked, Windows' lock screen implementation always felt like a kludge, and he posits this won't be the last hack found for it.

#10510. Are the Galaxy Buds 4 Pro the best earbuds for you?

Picking the right earbuds is a tough task, but Samsung’s Galaxy Buds 4 Pro are among the best on the market, and in this guide, we’ll be looking at whether or not they’re the best earbuds for you this holiday season.

In short, yes, the Galaxy Buds 4 Pro sound great. Samsung’s flagship earbuds have a balanced sound profile that’s customizable by the listener to suit their tastes, and is generally recognized as having excellent sound quality by experts and owners alike.

Galaxy Buds 4 Pro are award-winning earbuds with balanced sound quality for music lovers, offering a clean and crisp profile that lets you hear music the way you’ll enjoy it most.

Samsung’s flagship earbuds deliver their sound through a new, larger main driver that’s paired with a dedicated tweeter. They support 24-bit/96 kHz UHQ audio when paired with a compatible Samsung Galaxy device. That’s paired with active noise cancellation that adjust to your surroundings in real-time. They also sound good for those on the other end of a call, with Samsung’s Super Clear Call tech reducing noise from your environment, and the blade stem design positioning the microphones closer to your mouth.

Like everything else in Samsung’s product lineup, Galaxy Buds 4 Pro are packed with useful features.

This includes active noise cancellation (ANC) to filter out noise in your surroundings. Samsung even adapts ANC strength to what’s around you, raising the ANC level when you’re somewhere like on a plane, but lowering it when you’re in a quiet park or café. Voice and Siren Detect can automatically switch the earbuds to Ambient mode when you begin a conversation or when an emergency siren is detected.

Live Translate offers support for 22 languages to help you have a conversation beyond the language barrier, with speech translated into your ears via Buds 4 Pro and the transcript shown on your phone. Bixby and Gemini are accessible through the earbuds for AI assistance, too. Samsung also offers an FDA-approved hearing aid feature to help amplify sounds around you.

Head gestures allow you to answer or decline calls with a simple shake of your head. Pinch controls manage playback, while swiping adjusts the volume; pinch-and-hold can be customized for supported shortcuts.

No, you don’t have to have a Samsung Galaxy smartphone to use Galaxy Buds 4 Pro.

Because they work over standard Bluetooth, Samsung’s best earbuds work with any smartphone, tablet, and other devices. Galaxy Buds 4 Pro can also connect to Windows PCs as standard Bluetooth audio, although advanced features are more limited outside the Galaxy ecosystem.

That said, you’ll get the best experience with Samsung devices. That’s in part because EQ, noise cancellation, and other controls are baked into One UI, but also because Samsung’s best audio codec on Galaxy Buds 4 Pro is proprietary to Galaxy devices. In other words, you’ll get the best sound quality on these earbuds when paired to another Samsung device.

Galaxy Buds 4 Pro are typically sold for $249.99, but it’s not hard to find a good deal, especially during the holiday season. Best Buy is going to be one of the best resources here, with discounts available throughout the holiday season alongside convenient in-store pickup.

It’s hard to believe, but Grand Theft Auto VI is now a real thing that we all can play. Thirteen years after GTA V, and eight years since Red Dead Redemption 2, Rockstar is back with its latest sprawling, extravagant open world. There’s a lot to experience — and a lot to talk about.

Perhaps the most impressive thing about GTA VI is how flexible it is, letting you mold the experience to your own play style. You can still be a maniacal criminal, but you can also focus on the new love story that includes an entire button dedicated to holding hands. Or you can spend the hours fishing or driving around listening to the radio. Maybe you just want to doomscroll through a fake version of Instagram. As GTA VI producer Rob Nelson told me just ahead of launch, “It’s a risk. It’s a real change for Grand Theft Auto. You can still play it the old way, you can reject all of this stuff. But for people who do engage, it’s meaningful — you’re earning it, you’re working at it, just like in a normal relationship.”

#10506. All my favorite XREAL glasses are at their lowest price ever for Black Friday

Xreal’s early Black Friday sales have just begun, and they get you a fantastic pair of smart display glasses for as low as $249.

Xreal’s smart display glasses are a fan favorite, giving you a giant virtual screen with top-tier optics and micro-OLED displays, plus spatial tracking that most other glasses simply don’t offer. The three best Xreal pairs are on sale right now through November 30, giving you ample time to decide which pair is best for you.

Personally, I would recommend the least expensive of the bunch, the Xreal xbx a01+. It’s on sale for $249 ($50 off) and sports the most stylish looks of the three options, plus the lightest weight at 62g, the brightest display at 1600 nits, and class-leading 3,840Hz PWM dimming, plus light spatial tracking that’ll keep you from feeling sick when using them in a moving vehicle. I find these the most comfortable, by far, and have taken them with me on every flight since they launched in the summer.

Choosing between the Xreal 1S and Xreal One Pro is a little harder, so let’s cover the pricing and differences that you’ll care most about. Xreal 1S is on sale for $379 ($70 off) and offers true native 3DoF spatial tracking, built-in real-time 3D conversion of 2D content, and a fully adjustable screen size and distance. Those are all upgrades over the xbx a01+ model.

Xreal One Pro is on sale for $499 ($100 off) and upgrades the Xreal 1S experience with prism lenses and a wider field of view. There are no other functional differences between One Pro and 1S other than the wider field of view and flatter lenses, so if those aren’t a big deal to you, the Xreal 1S is the “Goldilocks” pick because of its price and the range of features it offers.

If you haven’t used a pair of smart display glasses from Xreal before, the setup is simple. Just plug the USB-C cable into your phone, laptop, or gaming handheld, and you’ll be greeted with a giant virtual screen that floats in front of your face. Making those kinds of speeds available throughout your house, however, requires a competent router that can handle both the high throughput and a fairly high number of connected devices. Enter the TP-Link Archer BE9500 Wi-Fi 7 router with 10 Gbps WAN, which, as of right now, is on sale at Best Buy for under a single Benjamin, at $89.99 — or under half its regular price of $199.99.

TP-Link networking gear has earned a good reputation with techies thanks to good reliability and offering great specs at quite affordable pricing, as is the case in point. Unlike some routers that outright lie about supporting WiFi 7, the Archer BE9500 actually has the magical combo that fully enables the power of the latest protocol: a 6 GHz band, along with Multi Link Operation (MLO) support.

The TP-Link Archer BE9500 is a full-featured Wi-Fi 7 router with full 6 GHz and MLO support and a 10 Gbps WAN port for high-speed internet connections.

That grants the BE9500 an entire 9.5 Gbps of combined wireless bandwidth across all three frequencies, broadcast into the airwaves by six beam-forming antennas. For physical connections, there's one 10 Gbps WAN port, along with two 2.5 Gbps connectors for your big workstations or gaming rigs, and an additional two gigabit ports.

As befits a modern router, the Archer BE9500 supports EasyMesh for wiring additional devices, as well as separate guest and internal networks for segregating IoT and other classes of devices. There are four VPN flavors on offer for acting as both a server and a client, including support for the modern WireGuard protocol.

A USB port for connecting storage media, parental controls, automatic firmware upgrade, VLAN tagging, and miscellaneous features round out the main features. Get this router while stocks last, for only $89.99 at Best Buy.

#10499. Running an AI Agent on a Local LLM: What I Measured on One GPU

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Running an AI agent on your own GPU sounds simple: point it at Ollama and stop paying per token. I set up OpenClaw, an open-source AI assistant, with a 9B model in Ollama on one RTX 3090 and connected it to Discord. Then I read the Ollama log for every request.

The agent worked. The numbers were not what I expected, though. A one-line question turned into a prompt of more than 18,000 tokens, the first reply took over a minute, and the small model sometimes guessed instead of using its tools.

This article covers what I measured and what each number means if you plan to run an agent on local hardware.

OpenClaw ran in Docker and reached Ollama on the host at http://host.docker.internal:11434. Discord was the chat interface, and OpenClaw's web panel showed the sessions and context use.

This article is about the results, not the installation. The full step-by-step setup is in a separate guide to running OpenClaw in Docker with Ollama and Discord. That guide covers the Compose file, the Discord bot and how to keep the ports off your network.

All timings below come from the Ollama log on the test machine. Ollama loaded the model with a 32,768-token context.

My first message in Discord was short: "Which model are you running on, and what is 17 * 23?" The bot answered correctly: it ran on ollama/qwen3.5:9b, and 17 × 23 is 391.

The Ollama log showed what that question cost. The model processed 18,415 prompt tokens and generated 33 tokens.

The extra tokens come from the agent itself. With every request, OpenClaw sends:

On the RTX 3090, reading that prompt took 10 seconds at 1,838 tokens per second. A slower GPU, or a CPU, needs much longer to read the same prompt.

Prompt tokens per model call, from the Ollama log. Only the first message had to process the whole prompt.

OpenClaw counts tokens with its own estimate, which was higher than the Ollama count. The gateway log estimated the prompt at 24,608 tokens, against a prompt budget of 24,576 tokens for this model. By OpenClaw's own count, the base prompt alone used up the budget before any conversation history.

The web panel showed a similar figure: 68 to 69 percent of the context window was in use for the Discord sessions after only a few messages.

The first message arrived while the model was not loaded in GPU memory. That reply took 1 min 10 s:

Reply times on the test machine. Loading the model took most of the first reply.

The speed-up comes from prompt caching. The system prompt, tool definitions and workspace files at the start of the prompt stay the same between requests. Ollama kept that part cached and processed only the new tokens. The model wrote at 91 to 96 tokens per second in every test.

Ollama's documentation says models stay in memory for 5 minutes by default before they are unloaded. After that, the next message pays the load time again. You can change this with the OLLAMA_KEEP_ALIVE environment variable on the Ollama server, or with the keep_alive parameter per request. A negative value, such as -1, keeps the model loaded. I did not change this setting in the test.

If you run Ollama in Docker, pass the variable with -e OLLAMA_KEEP_ALIVE=-1 or in the environment: section of your Compose file.

Two things used the GPU besides my chat messages.

A heartbeat. OpenClaw's web panel listed one scheduled automation, heartbeat-main, which runs every 30 minutes. The agent wakes on that schedule, so the model gets called even when nobody chats. With Ollama's 5-minute default, the model is unloaded between heartbeats unless someone chatted recently, so a heartbeat most likely starts with a model load. I did not measure this.

An embedding model. During the first message, OpenClaw also called Ollama's /api/embed endpoint twice to index its memory. It used qwen3-embedding:4b, which was already installed on the Ollama server. I did not set OpenClaw to use that model; it picked it on its own.

The two models took this much GPU memory:

That is 11.4 GB for the models alone, almost half of the RTX 3090's 24 GB.

What this means for you: plan GPU memory for the chat model, the embedding model and the context, not the chat model alone. If the GPU also runs other models, the heartbeat can compete with them.

The first two tests in Discord: a question, then a file written with a tool. The user name is blurred.

The mistakes showed up when the model had to check something:

After the restart, the bot claimed it had lost the earlier session. When asked to use its read tool, it read the file correctly.

The fix for the first problem was simple. When I wrote "use your read tool to open homelab-test.md", the model opened the file and listed all three points correctly. Naming the tool made it check instead of guess.

The model listed four files and missed the test file and a folder, because it answered from its prompt.

Based on this test, a local 9B model on one consumer GPU works for:

If you need better answers, OpenClaw also works with hosted models, but then your prompts leave your machine.

In this test, qwen3.5:9b used 6.3 GB and the qwen3-embedding:4b memory model used 5.1 GB. Together the two models needed 11.4 GB.

The model had to load into GPU memory first, which took 59 seconds of the 70-second reply. Ollama keeps a model loaded for 5 minutes by default, and the OLLAMA_KEEP_ALIVE setting changes that.

Every request includes OpenClaw's system prompt, its tool definitions and its workspace files. A one-line question became an 18,415-token prompt in this test.

Yes. A heartbeat automation runs every 30 minutes and calls the model even when nobody sends a message.

It handled questions, arithmetic and file creation correctly in this test. It sometimes answered from its prompt instead of using its tools. When it wrongly said its memory was lost, asking it to use its read tool made it check the file and answer correctly.

#10495. Google Fi app redesign brings floating toolbar & Ask Fi

Google Fi Wireless is the latest first-party app to adopt a floating toolbar as part of a small navigation redesign.

Previously, the companion app still used a tall Material 3 bottom bar. With the network switching update announced last week, Google Fi now has a floating navigation toolbar.

The tabs remain Home, Plan, Billing, Shop, and Support. A pill-shaped tab indicator is leveraged, while there’s a prominent shadow effect. Given the floating nature, you see what’s around the toolbar, while Fi Wireless now extends underneath the system navigation bar.

However, the Shop tab loads a webpage as before, resulting in a gap and wasted space.

There’s now a floating action button for the new Google AI-powered Ask Fi chatbot. It provides “instant account help and troubleshooting.” If you ask for human support, the contents of the conversation carry over.

This redesign is rolling out with version 171 of the app as a server-side update. Google Fi joins Google Photos, Chat, and Pixel Now Playing in adopting floating navigation, which is likely the new first-party mandate for a more edge-to-edge design.

Secondary network switching is not yet widely available, with Google telling us last week that it’s a gradual rollout. The MVNO is also ending Pixel exclusivity for Wi-Fi Auto Connect+ with availability on “select” Samsung and Motorola phones.

#10487. Best Ecommerce Platforms for Developers: Six Options and the Layers Each Leaves You

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Picking an ecommerce platform is really a decision about which layers you want to own: the storefront, the catalog and order logic, the payment flow, and the hosting underneath. Every platform below draws that line in a different place. If you have already settled the front-end question with a comparison like SitePoint's Astro vs Next.js guide, the next question is what sits behind it. Prices are from each vendor's pricing page as of November 2026.

Shopify is the fully managed option. Basic costs $29 a month with card rates from 2.9% plus 30 cents, and a 2% fee applies to orders processed through a third-party gateway on that plan. Developers get two routes: Liquid themes, or Hydrogen, Shopify's headless toolkit, where your framework owns routing and rendering and Hydrogen supplies the Storefront API client and cart. Templates exist for React Router on Oxygen and for Next.js on Vercel. Plus starts at $2,300 a month.

Whop is a business platform for taking payments and sending payouts, and it sits under a storefront you have already built rather than replacing it. The Checkout element runs in the browser with no server code and no API key on the page. You pass your own order ID as metadata, Whop copies it onto the payment, and your server fulfills from a signed webhook that follows the Standard Webhooks spec. Domestic cards cost 2.7% plus 30 cents with no monthly fee, buyers see payment methods chosen by their location across 195 countries, and the same balance pays out in over 200 countries, including to affiliates or sellers you onboard. Whop's own guide shows how to integrate a checkout API into a Next.js storefront end to end. The trade-off is that there is no catalog or shipping engine; you collect address fields at checkout and trigger fulfillment from the webhook.

WooCommerce is a free plugin on WordPress you host yourself, so you own updates, security, and performance. Its payment gateway, WooPayments, charges US merchants 2.9% plus 30 cents per card transaction, 1.5% more for international cards, 2% for currency conversion, and $25 per dispute. Everything is PHP hooks and filters, which makes it the natural pick for agencies already living in WordPress and the wrong pick for anyone who does not want to run a server.

BigCommerce hosts the back end and lets you bring any front end. Core is $29 a month billed annually, capped at $30,000 in trailing annual sales, and plans auto-upgrade as volume grows. There are no platform fees when you use an embedded payment provider, and a 2% fee on Core if you use another one. Catalyst, its reference storefront, is built on Next.js, React Server Components, and the Storefront GraphQL API, and deploys to Vercel.

Medusa is an open-source commerce framework in Node.js and TypeScript, with an MIT-licensed core and a commercial enterprise edition. SitePoint covered it when it was still a young Shopify alternative; it now has over 37,000 GitHub stars. Self-hosting is free. Medusa Cloud starts at $29 a month for development, $99 for launch, and $299 for scale, with no percentage of sales, and the company says you can export your data and self-host at any point.

Saleor is a GraphQL-native, API-only commerce platform written in Python and released under the BSD-3 license. Self-hosting is free. Saleor Cloud starts at $1,599 a month for up to $200,000 in monthly sales, with 0.8% on orders above that, and $3,999 a month at the next tier. It is built for teams with a dedicated engineering function and a large catalog, which is exactly who should pay that price.

Own only the storefront, and Shopify or BigCommerce will carry the rest. Own everything, and Medusa or Saleor give you a back end you can read. Already on WordPress, and WooCommerce is the obvious fit. Already have a storefront in Next.js or Astro and only need the money to move, and Whop is the shortest path. Whichever you pick, the checkout is where disputes start, so log what SitePoint's friendly fraud guide for developers recommends from day one.

This is the most nimble and graceful John Deere we’ve ever seen.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Videos of an incredibly acrobatic, nimble, and graceful John Deere 5-ton skid-steer loader have been shared by a group of researchers from the U.S. and Switzerland. A recently published paper titled Heavy Metal Acrobatics provides the reasoning behind this eyebrow-raising autonomous robotics feat. It is hoped this research will be useful in the future automation of heavy machinery in industries like construction, mining, and material handling. It also demonstrates that reinforcement learning in a simulation can transfer remarkably well to the real world.

Researchers have used reinforcement learning to teach a 5-tonne John Deere skid-steer loader to perform complex acrobatic manoeuvres autonomously. Trained in simulation and transferred to a real machine, the system can balance on two wheels, navigate rough terrain, spin, and control its bucket before landing safely. The research demonstrates the potential of AI-driven automation for heavy construction and industrial machinery. Video Credit: @leggedrobotics / @ETH Paper: https://t.co/MT7fyZ54Hk #engineering #technologyNovember 17, 2026

If you unfurl the video embed above, you can see a hulking 332G skid-steer do some astonishing tricks. It hops back and forth on two wheels, pirouettes, and balances while flexing its lift arm. Moreover, it does these moves while never looking like it will teeter over. The researchers claim that “only a handful of elite human operators” have ever been able to pull off stunts as you see in the demo videos.

The technology behind the remarkable acrobatic skid-steer is mostly Reinforcement Learning (RL), with physics modeling and neural network models. As machinery like this is so big, powerful, and bulky, undertaking RL directly on the hardware wasn’t deemed practical. Instead, the research team decided to use a simulation programmed with the John Deere 332G’s complex array of controls for training. This data was subsequently applied to the actual skid-steer via a sim-to-real transfer. The simulations must be extremely accurate.

This acrobatic goliath is impressive, but of course there’s an underlying goal driving Heavy Metal Acrobatics’ research. The scientists are laying the foundations for the enabling of “dynamic autonomous control of heavy machinery.” It is hoped this will help promote greater automation in heavy equipment sectors of industry. Eventually, autonomous control should be safer, faster, and more efficient than any human.

With these video demonstrations of nimble and highly maneuverable heavy machinery, it is clear that there’s more scope for AI-driven automation than one may have previously expected. Furthermore, it looks like being a skilled heavy machinery operator is definitely not an AI-proof occupation.

#10481. Build Generative UI Apps with A2UI & CopilotKit Agents

Agent-User Interactions are now evolving from text exchanges to rich UIs. Until recently, most conversations looked like this:

User: "Find me a holiday package to Bali in March." Agent: (six packages in prose, each with dates, hotel, flights, price) User: "The five-night one with breakfast." Agent: "Here's the link. You can book it on the operator's site."

Agents could plan the trip, but everything came back as walls of text. They had no standard way to render a selectable list of packages, a map of the destinations, or a date picker to carry you through the rest of the booking, inside the conversation itself.

Generative UI is the layer that lets agents build interfaces at runtime, shaped by what each request needs - giving users a rich, interactive experience. Implementing that takes two pieces: a way to describe the UI and an agentic stack that connects your agent to your application and renders that UI.

Generative UI covers a spectrum of approaches, from using predefined higher order components, to enabling your AI agent to have the flexibility to decide how to build the UI.

At the controlled end, you build each component ahead of time, and the agent picks which one to render and with what data. It's brand-safe but it does not allow for deep customization and personalization based on the user’s intent. At the open end, the agent returns a raw UI program written in HTML, Javascript and/or Dart that renders inside a sandboxed iframe. That covers anything you did not plan for, at the cost of a surface that drifts from your design system and executes code a model wrote.

A2UI works through the entire spectrum. It participates in controlled generation with decision models like Typesafe’s Jev and also open-ended code generation that may output A2UI JSON messages. The declarative mode, where agents assemble pre-declared components and functions, is the most popular. A2UI is the framework-agnostic standard for declaring UI intent, so local or remote agents can generate your UI from your existing component catalog using a common language.

Frontend developers can now ship the building blocks once, and the agent composes them for each request.

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

The Basic Catalog ships 18 general-purpose primitives across layout, display, input, and containers. The agent can compose entirely different interfaces from the same 18 primitives, depending on what the request needs.

The Basic Catalog is deliberately sparse, so most production apps define their own custom catalog. A custom component can be anything your frontend already renders, including a live map the agent fills with coordinates while your renderer draws the route.

CopilotKit is built on AG-UI (Agent-User Interaction Protocol), which supports A2UI payload and provides Agent SDKs across many agentic backends.

Your agent emits A2UI operations. AG-UI carries them to the client as typed events. When the user takes any action, the values are sent back along the same stream.

A2UI works with any AG-UI compatible agent. Frameworks like Google ADK, LangChain, Mastra and Strands are all AG-UI compatible, so the setup is identical whichever one your agent runs on.

Setup comes down to two things: connecting your agent to CopilotKit, and defining which components the agent is allowed to use.

Using Google ADK as the example, the ag_ui_adk package wraps any ADK agent and serves it as an AG-UI endpoint on FastAPI:

On the app side, you register that endpoint with the CopilotKit runtime at /api/copilotkit. It's a few lines of standard setup, covered in the Google ADK quickstart, and it needs nothing A2UI-specific.

A2UI itself turns on from the client, where your components already are. Define what the agent is allowed to draw, map each definition to a renderer, and pass the catalog to the CopilotKit provider.

The description goes into the agent's prompt, so the model knows when to reach for FlightCard instead of a generic card. The Zod schema validates the props it sends back.

The A2UI configuration can be set up ahead of time on the server, or it can be created dynamically, shipped from the client to the server with the request. The ADK code above used dynamic configuration which is why it used generate_a2ui without defining it. The docs cover both approaches.

That’s the CopilotKit path. You can also consume the event stream directly with @ag-ui/client and hand the A2UI operations to any compliant renderer: @a2ui/react, or the Flutter, Lit, or Angular equivalent.

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

Most agent-generated UI is one-shot. The agent draws something, you read it, and it goes stale the moment anything changes.

A2UI surfaces are bi-directional and the components are reactive, which makes multi-step flows possible. Any action the user takes on the interface (picking dates, choosing a flight, entering passenger details) travels back along the same stream, and the agent re-emits the surface from there using those values.

Here is a high-level architecture example of an end-to-end booking flow, with an AG-UI agent on Cloud Run streaming A2UI surfaces to a CopilotKit frontend.

Two things make the round trip work: inputs bound to a data path, and a button carrying an action that names which paths to send back.

Validation runs on the client, so a bad email shows an error inline without a model call. Nothing reaches the agent until the button fires.

Then the client sends the bound values, and the agent re-emits against the same surfaceId. That is how the whole flow lives on one surface: flight search, results, passenger details, and confirmation.

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

Every AG-UI integration inherets these benefits, so you don't have to build them yourself:

Create a complete A2UI + CopilotKit application. This creates a starter A2UI application with Copilotkit Agent integration.

The CopilotKit Dojo has runnable A2UI demos: fixed server-side schema, dynamic schema against a custom catalog, and an error-recovery example where an invalid first render heals into a valid one.

Explore how to build Generative UI Agents with CopilotKit, or dive into A2UI documentation to learn how to integrate A2UI with any framework of your choice.

Distributed LLM Training Across Metros: Streaming DiLoCo in MaxText

One years' worth of chip development allegedly condensed into under 5 months.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

OpenAI just revealed new details about its ongoing partnership with AMD, in which engineers used undisclosed AI models to help optimize the hardware maker's upcoming flagship GPU and CPU designs. The leading figures are up to 14% PPA (power, performance, and area) improvement for the GPU circuitry, and unspecified single-digit PPA gains on the processor.

OpenAI says that its model spotted "further improvements by rewriting the code that describes the chip’s circuits." This work was done in SystemVerilog RTL, the language commonly used for high-end chip design. The firm claims that its model's optimization efforts are equivalent to condensing a year's worth of conventional chip design work into "under 5 months" — a time crunch it says is unprecedented in this arena.

Furthermore, OpenAI says its model was reasoning on RTL "at the level of an expert microarchitect," and that it expects to squeeze further PPA improvements in both GPU and CPU designs, up from the now-improved baseline.

This isn't AI's first time working on chips. It worked with Broadcom on designing OpenAI Jalapeño, an inference-targeted chip that was already running actual workloads with engineering samples as far back as June 2026.

The company expected to have small initial Jalapeño deployments around late 2026, and pinned a broader rollout to 2027. Test benchmarks have been out for a while, displaying purported excellent savings on power draw and overall latency versus the market-leading Nvidia Blackwell GPUs in single-token prediction workloads.

The blog post also explains how GPT's chip-design acumen evolved. The researchers tested its high-end LLMs by asking them to improve PicoRV32 and Ibex chip cores. The models were given 112,000 tokens per run, told to optimize for throughput (a combination of Instructions-per-Clock and clock-cycle duration), and scored the results.

A fat zero was given if the chips didn't work or used more than 16x the previous area, and 1.0 was given for the best designs under the test constraints. The test also didn't tell the models which workloads the chips were going to be used for. As expected, GPT-6.2 Astra had by far the best showing, on both axes: both the lowest overall cost and the highest optimization score.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

A second test also revealed an interesting but unsurprising fact: the models provided better results when essentially redesigning the chips according to their initial specifications, rather than optimizing existing code.

There's no word on whether the scientists used unmodified versions of GPT to perform this work. Task-specific high-end LLMs exist for those who can afford them, and OpenAI itself says it's working with Synopsys to train GPT-Synopsis, a model for using chip design tools.

#10476. AI features could be turned off with a simple toggle, Canva proves

AI has taken over everything in the past few years, with AI features bombarding virtually every app or service available. If you’ve ever wanted to just turn off AI features, Canva proves that it could be a simple toggle, if companies actually wanted to give you a choice.

Canva today announced “AI Controls,” a new toggle that completely turns off generative AI features within the editing app.

Generative AI can be quite helpful in working on posters, editing images, and more, but it’s not everyone’s preference. Canva puts this well:

For some people, AI is the most exciting creative tool in a generation, while others prefer bringing their ideas to life without it. We believe the future of creativity should give everyone the freedom to choose their own path, whether that’s creating with AI, without it, or somewhere in between.

To give its users the choice, Canva’s Permissions menu now has a simple toggle for “Enable Generative AI features.” Toggling it off disables features such as Magic Write and Magic Edit. There’s also an option to limit how much AI-generated imagery appears in the Canva library.

AI has its place, but everyone should have a choice like this, especially when it comes to apps and services used for artistry in the same vein as Canva. Yet, the tech industry continues to go in the opposite direction, shoving AI into every corner whether it’s actually useful or not. Canva proves, though, that it doesn’t have to be that way.

#10474. Proton Pass is up to 60% off for Black Friday—and it does way more than just store your passwords

There’s a familiar interruption that comes with shopping online: you’ve found what you want, you’re ready to check out, and the store asks you to sign in. Was this the account with your usual password? Did you change it last time? Is it easier to reset it again?

Proton Pass is designed to take that friction out of your day. It creates strong, unique passwords, remembers them for you, and fills them in when you log in. But it also handles something a password alone can’t: keeping your real email address out of another retailer’s database.

For Black Friday, Proton Pass Plus and Pass Family are both 60% off their regular monthly pricing with a one-year subscription, while Proton Unlimited is 50% off. Pass Plus starts at $1.99 per month, billed annually, making this a good opportunity to get your passwords — and a few other important parts of your digital life — organized.

The problem with strong passwords isn’t understanding why you need them; it’s keeping track of a different one for every account.

Proton Pass does that work for you. When you sign up for a website, its password generator can create a strong password that’s completely unique. Save it in your vault, and Proton Pass can fill in your credentials when you return.

That’s useful whether you’re creating an account for a single purchase or signing into a service you use every day.

Unique passwords also help limit the fallout from a data breach. If a retailer exposes your login credentials, using a different password elsewhere means those same credentials won’t unlock your other accounts.

With unlimited logins and encrypted vaults that sync across devices, Proton Pass lets you keep those details together as you move between your phone and computer.

Proton Pass is available for practically every platform, from Chrome and Firefox on the web to native macOS, Windows, and Linux apps to, of course, iOS and Android. This makes it trivially easy to access your passwords, passkeys, and API credentials wherever you find yourself.

Black Friday shopping often leaves something behind long after the packages arrive: a steady stream of promotional emails from stores you barely remember visiting.

Proton Pass includes unlimited hide-my-email aliases, giving you an alternative to handing out your real address every time you create an account.

An alias is an extra email address that forwards messages to your normal inbox. You can give it to a shopping website and still receive your order confirmations and delivery updates, without sharing the underlying address you use for everything else.

If that alias starts receiving spam, you can switch it off. Your real email address stays the same, and you don’t have to update all your other accounts.

It’s a practical way to put some distance between your primary inbox and the websites you’re trying for the first time. A separate alias for each service also gives you more control: you can stop messages arriving through one address without disrupting the others.

Passwords are only part of what’s worth keeping in a secure, accessible place.

Proton Pass also stores notes, credit card details, and other confidential information in end-to-end encrypted vaults. That makes it useful for the small, everyday moments when you need something sensitive and don’t want to go looking for it.

When you’re booking a flight, saved passport details let you look up the information you need without digging through a drawer halfway through the reservation.

When you’re shopping online, saved card details mean you don’t have to leave the checkout page to find your wallet.

When you’re traveling, keeping a digital copy of an important document within reach gives you a convenient reference while you’re away from home. It doesn’t replace an original document where one is required, but it can save you from searching through scattered files and messages.

Pass Plus also includes a built-in two-factor authenticator, Dark Web Monitoring, and secure link and vault sharing. Those features give you more ways to manage access to your accounts and share sensitive information when you need to.

Pass Plus is the straightforward choice for one person who wants password generation, autofill, unlimited aliases, and secure storage.

Pass Family includes six Pass Plus accounts and a family admin panel. If several people in your household need a password manager, it’s worth considering rather than setting everyone up with separate subscriptions.

Proton Unlimited bundles Pass with Proton VPN, Proton Mail, Proton Drive, and more, including 500 GB of cloud storage. It’s the option to consider if you also want a VPN, encrypted email, and cloud storage alongside your password manager.

A password manager isn’t the most conspicuous Black Friday purchase, but it’s one you can put to work immediately: the next account you create, the next password you forget, or the next time a checkout asks for details you don’t have handy.

Proton’s offers are available to new users and include a 30-day money-back guarantee.

#10471. Your SSL certificates can now last 200 days at most: The real risk isn’t renewal

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Every SSL certificate comes with a limited lifespan. Following the latest update from the CA/Browser Forum, the maximum lifetime of a certificate has fallen from 398 days to 200, and it will drop to 100 days in March 2027 and 47 days in 2029. Renewal is no longer an annual chore. It’s an ongoing task that only stays manageable with automation.

So what does this mean if you already look after sites with SSL certificates, and will it bring drastic changes? For many setups, very little will change. For others, one missed renewal can mean an outage. This article explains what’s changing and what to do if you’re in the second group.

Today, an SSL certificate is valid for 200 days at most. The limit applies when a certificate is issued, so the ones you already hold keep their original expiration date.

Where you feel it depends on how your certificates renew. Sites on managed hosting platforms or behind a content delivery network (CDN) usually renew automatically already, often every 90 days, so the new limit changes little. The pressure lands on setups where someone buys a certificate once a year and installs it by hand. At 47 days, that becomes about 12 renewals a year.

A certificate is a promise from a certificate authority that a key really belongs to a domain, and that promise gets less reliable the longer it runs. If a private key leaks, or a certificate is issued by mistake, it stays usable until it expires. Cutting the lifetime shrinks that window, so a problem that once lingered for over a year is now fixed in weeks.

Shorter lifetimes also make revocation, the process for canceling a bad certificate, far less critical. Revocation has never worked perfectly, but a certificate that expires within weeks matters much less if a cancellation doesn’t reach every browser.

Another reason is about people. A task done once a year is easy to forget and hard to check from outside. Browser makers have pushed for years to move the industry onto automatic renewal, on the logic that a process that runs every few weeks gets tested constantly. If it breaks, you notice quickly instead of discovering it on the day the certificate expires.

For most people, the first effect is simply more work. A certificate that once needed attention every year will soon need it every few weeks. Plus, each renewal is a small chance for something to go wrong. Multiply that by dozens of sites, mail servers, and devices, and the chance of one slipping through grows quickly.

The safety nets are also thinner. If you relied on the renewal emails, nothing is watching your certificates now. Additionally, with a shorter life, there’s less time between this should have renewed and this has expired.

An expired certificate doesn’t make a site slow or dated. It makes it unreachable for most visitors. Chrome, Firefox, and Safari all show a full-page warning that the connection isn’t private, and most people leave instead of clicking through. If the site uses HSTS, a setting that tells browsers to connect only over secure links, there is often no way to click through at all, and visitors are blocked outright until the certificate is fixed.

The damage doesn’t stop at the home page. Checkouts, contact forms, and logins that depend on the secure connection fail along with it, often without an error message that points at the real cause. Other systems break, too, because apps and integrations that call your site over a secure link refuse to connect to an expired certificate.

Nothing about your site has to change for any of this to happen. A date passes, and a site that worked on Friday is down on Monday. In a 47-day lifetime, that date comes around about every six weeks.

Automatic renewal tools do the heavy lifting well. The failures happen around them:

Look at the five failures above and you’ll see what they share. Each one happens quietly. The renewal job ran, or looked like it did, and nobody checked what the server was actually serving afterwards. Automation can tell you it tried. It can’t tell you the result reached your visitors.

What closes that gap is a check that never relies on your server’s own account of events. Site24x7’s SSL/TLS certificate monitoring does exactly that. It reads the certificate installed on your web server from the cloud, the way a visitor’s browser would, and alerts you before it expires, using thresholds you choose. If a renewal fails quietly, you hear about it from the monitor and not from a customer.

For each certificate, it records the issuing authority, the owner, the issue date, the expiration date, and the days remaining, including on servers that host several certificates behind one address. That last number matters more as lifespans shrink. When a certificate lasts 47 days, the days left is the figure that tells you a renewal is overdue, and you can set your warning against it using the quarter-of-the-lifetime rule covered next.

Once a monitor is watching your certificates, the next question is when it should speak up.

Most expiration alerts are set to a fixed number of days: warn at 30 days left and escalate at seven. That works for a certificate that lasts a year and renews with a month to spare. It breaks when the whole certificate lasts for a limited number of days.

Here’s why: a healthy certificate renews with about a third of its life still left. If your alert appears earlier than that, it goes off on certificates that are doing nothing wrong, and soon everyone ignores it. As lifespans shrink, a fixed 30-day alert will start doing exactly that.

The fix is to set the alerts based on lateness, not on how close the expiry date is. Warn when a quarter of the lifetime is left, since a renewal is overdue by then, and escalate when a tenth is left. Because these are shares of the lifetime, they keep working, however short lifespans get.

The next cut lands on March 15, 2027, so a few checks now will save you from scrambling later:

Shorter lifespans won’t break your site, but a missed renewal will, so fix the quiet failures now while a mistake still costs an afternoon to fix, not a Monday morning outage affecting lots of customers.

If you’d like a second pair of eyes on your certificates, you can start a 30-day free trial of Site24x7’s SSL/TLS certificate monitoring.

A Redditor from the UK was flying from the United States with their desktop PC in a carry-on but was forced to check it. According to u/FattyLeopold, they’ve carried their PC as a carry-on on different flights, and they brought it with them in the cabin all the way across the Atlantic. The PC also has to deal with the vibration and turbulence of flight. It’s a miracle that the computer was able to make it through the journey without a scratch, but the Redditor is not out of the woods yet. They said that even though the computer looked undamaged on the surface, they haven’t been able to boot it yet because they do not have a proper adapter for outlets in the UK. Hopefully, they could get it working after finding the right plug or adapter for their computer, or with some basic troubleshooting (like re-plugging the components) if it won’t turn on after the first try. GPUs and memory sticks are quite expensive these days, after all, and it’s unlikely that AMD would replace their graphics card because of this misadventure, unlike this $10,000 RTX Pro 6000 that Nvidia replaced after a customer who bought it new broke the GPU in transit.
