#10467. Which Waterdrop water filters are actually worth buying this Black Friday?

Black Friday can turn something as simple as a home water upgrade into a real headache. Do you need a system that fills a glass in seconds, or one that keeps always-ready hot water  available right on the counter? Maybe you’re more worried about finding one that leaves enough room in the budget for the rest of your holiday shopping. Whatever your hydration demands might be, there’s an easy answer at a great price: Waterdrop Filter.

Waterdrop Filter’s Black Friday & Cyber Monday deals run from November 19th through November 30th, making this month the best time to make a meaningful upgrade to your everyday water.

This year, Waterdrop has everything from premium reverse osmosis (RO) water filtration for a house full of holiday guests to budget-friendly, installation-free setups for cozy apartment evenings. And the best part? No matter which you pick, you’re on your way to better-tasting water for drinking, cooking, and those extra demands that come with holiday hosting — complete with some exclusive 9to5Google discounts.

If your kitchen transforms into your home’s central hangout spot whenever guests are around, the Waterdrop X16 Reverse Osmosis System makes a strong case for going premium. Its 1,600-gallon-per-day rated capacity is the highest of the under-sink models here, perfect for a kitchen where glasses, pitchers, pots, and mugs are filled constantly throughout the day.

The X16 RO System is as powerful as that capacity makes it sound. It features Waterdrop’s excellent 11-stage reverse osmosis filter, which traps and removes debris, contaminants, and everything else you don’t want in your water, while its alkaline remineralization process restores that much-needed calcium and magnesium your body needs. Its compact, tankless design tucks neatly under your kitchen sink, while a digital faucet shows water and filter information and lets you select the exact amount of water you want to dispense.

The X16 also features IAPMO certification to NSF/ANSI 42 and 58 for specified performance claims, plus NSF/ANSI 372 for lead content in the parts that contact water. You don’t just have to take its word, though: Wired called the Waterdrop Filter X16 the “Rolls-Royce of water filtration.” The cleanest, clearest water available, ready for your refreshment every day of the year.

The X16 usually costs $1,999, but Black Friday pricing knocks 35% off to bring it down to $1,299. This year, you can use promo code 9TO5GBF26 to take another 5% off the sale price, bringing your final cost down to $1,234. Those are some incredible savings over the X16’s typical list price, and considering its faster flow and dispensing controls over competing models, it’s one of those rare Black Friday purchases you’ll feel great about for years to come.

Under-sink reverse osmosis doesn’t have to break the bank. For a family that wants a smart faucet and plenty of filtered water ready for cooking, coffee, and everyday drinking, the Waterdrop G3P800 Reverse Osmosis System is absolutely the model for you to consider this Black Friday.

The G3P800 RO System features an 800-gallon-per-day rating, 10-stage filtration, UV treatment, a tankless design, and a 3:1 pure-to-drain ratio. Those are well beyond basic specs for water filtration, but at a price you can feel good about. And since the G3P800 is certified to NSF/ANSI 42, 53, 58, and 372 standards, you can rest assured that every sip features reduced chlorine, lead, PFOA, and PFOS.

Waterdrop has the G3P800 RO System marked down 30% for Black Friday, bringing the price to just $699, but 9to5Google readers can use code 9TO5GBF26 to another 5% off for a grand total of just $664.

If you’re just getting started with reverse osmosis water filtration — or maybe you’re looking to introduce your friends or family to the benefits of cleaner hydration — the Waterdrop G5P500A Reverse Osmosis System might be the best fit this holiday season. It keeps the tankless setup from the G3P800, combining it with a smaller 500-gallon-per-day rated capacity, eight-stage filtration, and alkaline remineralization. While you do lose out on the G3P800’s smart faucet and UV treatment, the price point here simply cannot be beat.

At $190 — down 23% from its $259.99 MSRP, plus 9to5Google’s additional 5% discount — the G5P500A RO System gives you under-sink reverse osmosis without making a premium faucet part of the purchase. After all, the best water filter is the one with the features you’ll actually put to good use, and for a smaller household, this model still goes well beyond the everyday basics.

If under-sink installation isn’t an option for your home, Waterdrop Filter has two very different answers, both new to its lineup in 2026. Your choice comes down to whether you want hot and cold water ready and on-demand, or a pitcher you can keep filled in the fridge for perfectly chilled water whenever you want it.

The Waterdrop M6CG Mineral Hot & Cold Reverse Osmosis System dispenses mineralized water from 59°F to 203°F. That means that, from sunup to sundown, morning coffee to that midnight glass of water, you’re drinking clean, crisp, filtered water. The M6CG features five volume presets for one-touch refills, a detachable 40-ounce glass pitcher for serving guests, and a customizable Favorites mode for that perfect cup. Plus, separate clean-water and wastewater tanks and a 3:1 pure-to-drain ratio prevent backflow pollution and extend your filter’s lifespan.

If you’re someone who’ll use hot and cold water regularly, the Waterdrop M6CG RO System is a no-brainer. You can pick it up for just $390 after promo code 9TO5GBF26, way below its typical $539 list price.

If you don’t need the temperature controls, the Waterdrop C2SL Mineral Reverse Osmosis System keeps things simpler and costs much less. The G3P800 keeps smart features at a lower price. The G5P500A makes under-sink RO more affordable than ever. And for those with limited space, countertop models let you decide how much hot-and-cold convenience you want without ever touching your home’s plumbing system. Whichever fits your routine, don’t wait — Waterdrop Filter’s Black Friday and Cyber Monday offers run through November 30th.

#10465. At $600, the Pixel 10 is this year’s no-brainer Black Friday smartphone deal

Phones have gotten a lot more expensive over the last year or so, and it’s a trend we’re expecting to get worse before it can get better. If you’re in the market for an upgrade, this year’s Black Friday might be one of the final truly good times to buy a new device, and for just $600, last year’s Pixel 10 should be high on your list.

While buying year-old hardware typically comes with some pretty deep sacrifices between generations, that’s not particularly true with the Pixel 10 this year. From a hardware perspective, nearly everything is either identical or close enough to not particularly matter. Its design and display are all but unchanged, Qi2-ready magnets come on both models, and both devices are built with the same level of battery longevity in mind. And much to the chagrin of many a Pixel die-hard, Tensor G5 and Tensor G6 are practically interchangeable.

So what are you giving up that matters? When the Pixel 11 arrived, software features made up the bulk of this list, but with last week’s Pixel Drop, a lot of the gaps between these two generations have been closed. Gemini Intelligence with Rambler, app automation, and more are in the process of rolling out to existing Pixel 10 users, and new features like Create My Widget arrived on the Pixel 10 and 11 at the exact same time. From a software perspective — Google’s bread and butter, in my eyes — the only big sacrifice comes from the year of software support that’s already complete, leaving you with just six instead of seven.

Realistically, the camera is the one place you’re likely to feel a difference. The Pixel 10 was the first non-Pro device in Google’s Tensor era to ship with a telephoto lens, but it came with some big sacrifices. The main sensor is effectively an A-series lens, and taken together, the entire package might leave something to be desired. It’s far from a bad camera though — I used it as my main shooter through most of its lifecycle — and while the Pixel 11 did get some upgrades here, I think real shutterbugs would be better off looking at Google’s Pro lineup anyway. You’ll also lose out on things like HiLight, though really, are you missing that much?

What makes this deal so good is really its place in the market, not as a direct comparison to the newer Pixel 11. For $600, Google’s competition is going to have a hard time competing with the value the Pixel 10 delivers. Hell, even Google might find it difficult to compete in the coming months; at full price, $600 is what the Pixel 10a goes for now, a decidedly less-impressive device compared to this model. No, this is a flagship smartphone through and through, and for $600, you’re effectively guaranteeing a reliable, powerful smartphone experience from now until 2032 — hopefully enough time for this whole RAM crisis to finally simmer down a bit.

#10460. Honor just supercharged its smartwatch lineup with a new Pro model

The Honor Watch 6 Pro has a 5,500-nit OLED display, ceramic bezel, 18-day battery life, and costs less than it looks.

In the world of athletic smartwatches, it isn’t often that you could mistake one for a luxury watch, but Honor seems to want to flip all that on its head with the Honor Watch 6 Pro. Just take a look at this excerpt from the company’s press release:

Framing this brilliant display is a Precision Nanocrystalline Ceramic bezel crafted through a 1400°C high-temperature integrated sintering process, delivering a delicate texture and rich visual depth.

Inspired by modernist architecture, its diamond-cut octagonal rotatable crown features a dual-layer horizontal and vertical cut, complemented by a side button for intuitive navigation.

That sounds pretty wild to me, and it’s backed up by specs that sound just as legit, too. On top is a 1.5-inch LTPO Tandem AMOLED display that outputs 5,500-nit peak brightness. Underneath is a bevy of new sensors called the Intelligent Sense System, which Honor says combines “multi-wavelength monitoring, multi-optical path patrol technology, and anti-interference glass technology to reach deep skin layers.”

Runners will be pleased to know that the Honor Watch 6 Pro not only incorporates a dual-band GPS, but combines it with a “star-pointing 3-antenna design” and an AI fusion algorithm, which should deliver ultra-accurate tracking in any location and no matter how you hold your wrist. Despite its luxury looks, this one tracks over 120 sports modes, including Honor’s Professional Running Coach feature, which tracks running posture, lactate threshold, heart rate targets, and keeps you on pace with real-time voice coaching.

#10456. Power and cooling limits force AI clusters with 100,000+ accelerators to use multiple data centers

Keep exploring and earning more as a member.

Reviews, benchmarks, and updates on current GPUs.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

And software gains importance for such distributed data centers.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

The rapid growth of AI clusters is pushing computing infrastructure beyond the physical and power limitations of individual data centers, which creates demand for new networking architectures capable of connecting compute resources across multiple facilities and even geographical regions. At Supercomputing 2026 (SC26), researchers from The Ohio State University discussed the evolution of high-performance networking technologies. At the conference, they explained how efficient distributed computing at such a scale requires considerably more than faster network connections, highlighting the importance of scale across domains to stretch workloads across different data centers in separate geographical regions.

Modern AI clusters typically accommodate more than 100,000 AI accelerators and potentially millions of CPU endpoints, placing tremendous demands on network bandwidth, latency, and congestion management. Meanwhile, an accelerator's computational performance continues to increase faster than communication capabilities, which means that faster AI GPUs do not necessarily deliver proportional improvements in application performance. As a result, the efficiency of network infrastructure determines how effectively large-scale AI systems can use their computational resources.

Traditionally, high-performance computing systems have relied on two major interconnect domains: scale-up and scale-out. Scale-up networks connect processors and accelerators within tightly integrated systems using technologies like AMD's Infinity Fabric, Intel's UPI, Nvidia's NVLink, or emerging alternatives such as UALink. Scale-out networks, which typically rely on Ethernet, InfiniBand, HPE Slingshot, or, more recently, Ultra Ethernet, connect individual servers or rack-scale systems into larger clusters. These interconnects have different requirements for bandwidth, latency, and scalability, which is why they serve different purposes.

However, the massive size and power consumption of modern AI clusters create demand for a third interconnect domain, known as scale-across. Unlike scale-out networks, which typically connect systems within a single data center, scale-across architectures extend high-performance connectivity between different buildings, campuses, or even regions.

Such an approach enables operators to combine computational and other resources from multiple locations to achieve a certain level of performance rather than to bring an excessive number of accelerators to a single data center, which may be hard, or even impossible, due to power availability, cooling requirements, or physical space constraints.

The transition toward scale-across architectures has already begun. Google introduced its Virgo network architecture earlier this year to support AI workloads that exceed the space and power capacity of individual data centers. The company intends to treat an entire campus of computing facilities as a single system, albeit with specialized networks for communication between accelerators, servers, and data centers. Cisco and Marvell are also developing technologies for scale-across deployments, including high-capacity routers, switching chips, and optical interconnects designed to connect geographically distributed clusters.

Extending AI infrastructure beyond individual facilities introduces substantial technical challenges. While modern scale-up networks offer extremely high bandwidth and low latency over relatively short distances, communication between data centers must contend with the physical limitations of long-distance connections. Even optical networks cannot eliminate propagation delays, whereas congestion, packet loss, and synchronization overhead can further reduce effective performance. Consequently, connecting two large GPU clusters with high-speed links does not necessarily make them behave like one tightly integrated computing system.

These challenges are particularly important for AI training, where accelerators frequently exchange model parameters, gradients, and intermediate results. Large-scale workloads require efficient collective communications, as delays that affect individual accelerators dramatically reduce the performance of the entire cluster. Therefore, the efficiency of distributed computing depends not only on raw network bandwidth, but also on network topology, congestion management, communication protocols, and software implementations.

To address these limitations, network adapters and switches are becoming more sophisticated than ever. Modern network interface controllers can perform Remote Direct Memory Access (RDMA) operations without continuous CPU involvement, whereas technologies such as HPE Slingshot hardware collectives and Nvidia SHARP can offload certain communication operations to the network infrastructure itself. SmartNICs and data processing units further reduce the burden on host processors as they handle selected network and infrastructure functions in dedicated hardware. Such capabilities can improve efficiency of distributed applications without necessarily requiring faster physical interconnects.

Interestingly, software plays an equally important role in the performance of large-scale AI infrastructure, as communication frameworks like libfabric, MPI, or UCX determine how applications exchange data and use the capabilities of modern network adapters. Also, GPU-aware communication and technologies like GPUDirect RDMA can eliminate unnecessary transfers through host memory, while optimized collective operations can reduce communication overhead across large numbers of accelerators, according to the researchers.

The growing complexity of AI infrastructure also changes how operators should evaluate network performance. Although the industry continues to introduce faster interconnect technologies, including versions of GbE with nominal bandwidths of 800 Gb/s and 1.6 Tb/s, peak transfer rates alone do not fully describe the capabilities of a given system because the performance of actual applications depends on multiple factors, including how effectively network hardware and software cooperate to transfer data, prevent congestion, and synchronize computations across thousands of processors.

Ultimately, the emergence of scale-across interconnection architectures reflects a fundamental change in the design of AI infrastructure. As the computational capabilities of individual accelerators increase and AI clusters expand beyond individual facilities, efficient communication becomes just as important as processor performance. As a result, the next generation of AI systems will depend not only on faster AI accelerators or CPUs, but also on sophisticated network adapters, switches, and, perhaps more importantly, software that can unite geographically distributed computational resources without excessive communication overhead.

Anton Shilov is a contributing writer at Tom’s Hardware. Over the past couple of decades, he has covered everything from CPUs and GPUs to supercomputers and from modern process technologies and latest fab tools to high-tech industry trends.

#10452. Logging AI Prompts and Responses in Java Without Leaking Sensitive Data

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Picture this. A customer writes to your support chatbot: "Hi, I'm Asha, my card ending 4111 was charged twice, call me on 98400 12345." Your Java service wraps that message in a prompt, sends it to a model, and logs the whole thing so you can debug it later. Congratulations, you now have a customer's name, phone number and card details sitting in a plain text log file that half the engineering team can open.

This happens more often than people admit. Logging AI calls is genuinely useful, but prompts and responses are some of the most personal text your application ever touches. Here's how to keep the usefulness and drop the risk.

A regular web request is easy to log: URL, status code, time taken. AI calls are messier.

The output changes from run to run. If a user says "your bot gave me a wrong answer yesterday", the only proof of what happened is what you sent and what came back at that moment. You can't just replay it and expect the same result.

Prompts are also built, not typed. The final text is usually a template, a few retrieved document chunks, some chat history and the user's message stitched together. When something looks off, the bug could be in any of those pieces, and only the assembled prompt shows you which one.

Then there's cost. You pay by the token, so without per-call records you can't say which feature is burning money. And quality drifts over time as models get upgraded and templates get edited. Old logs are your only baseline.

So yes, you need these logs. The real question is what goes into them.

Before writing any masking code, agree on what counts as sensitive. In most business apps that means:

Names, emails, phone numbers and home addresses

Card numbers, bank details and government ID numbers

Health, legal or job-related details people mention in free text

Internal material like contract wording, pricing rules or source code

The first three groups follow patterns, so they're easy to catch. The last two don't, and that's where most teams get caught out. Keep that in mind, because it shapes the design below.

The safest sensitive value is the one that never reaches the log. So split what you record into two buckets.

The first is metadata: a request ID, a hashed user or tenant ID, model name, prompt template name and version, token counts, latency, retry count, error type and estimated cost. None of this is personal, and it answers most day-to-day questions.

The second is content, meaning the actual prompt and response text. You only need this when you're investigating quality, and even then you rarely need all of it. A sensible default is to log metadata for every call and masked content for a small sample, maybe five percent, plus every failed call.

Here's a mistake that's easy to make: letting the log appender clean things up afterwards. By then the raw text may already be in a buffer, an async queue or a crash dump. Mask first, then log.

The cleanest way is one small component that every AI log call has to pass through:

public interface PiiMasker {     String mask(String input); }

Nothing else is allowed to write raw prompts. One chokepoint is much easier to review and test than masking sprinkled across fifty classes.

For structured data like emails, cards and tokens, regular expressions do the job well:

public class RegexPiiMasker implements PiiMasker {     private static final Map RULES = new LinkedHashMap<>();     static {         RULES.put(Pattern.compile("[A-Za-z0-9.%+-]+@[A-Za-z0-9.-]+\.[A-Za-z]{2,}"), "[EMAIL]");         RULES.put(Pattern.compile("\b(?:\d[ -]?){13,19}\b"), "[CARD]");         RULES.put(Pattern.compile("\b(?:\+?\d{1,3}[ -]?)?\d{10}\b"), "[PHONE]");         RULES.put(Pattern.compile("(?i)bearer\s+[A-Za-z0-9.~+/=-]+"), "Bearer [TOKEN]");     }     @Override     public String mask(String input) {         if (input == null || input.isBlank()) return input;         String result = input;         for (var rule : RULES.entrySet()) {             result = rule.getKey().matcher(result).replaceAll(rule.getValue());         }         return result;     } }

A few small choices matter here. The patterns are compiled once, not on every call. The order is fixed, because a card number can otherwise get half eaten by the phone rule. And the replacement labels such as [EMAIL] keep the sentence readable, so whoever debugs it later can still follow what the user was asking.

Regex won't catch a name like "Asha Raman" or an address buried mid-sentence. For those you have a few options, and they work best together.

You can run a named-entity recognition model that tags people, places and organisations, then mask anything above a confidence threshold. You can do exact replacement of values you already know, such as the customer's name from the current session. Or, best of all, you can mask at the field level while building the prompt. If your code knows that customer.getEmail() is going into the template, swap in a placeholder right there instead of trying to spot it afterwards. Knowing beats guessing every time.

Plain redaction can remove too much. If the same email shows up five times in a conversation, five identical [EMAIL] tags hide the fact that it was one person.

The fix is consistent pseudonyms. Turn each real value into a short token using a keyed hash, so the same email always becomes something like EMAIL_7f3a. You can still follow the thread, but nobody can read the original from the log. Use an HMAC with a secret from your secrets manager. A plain unkeyed hash of a phone number can be brute-forced in minutes, because there just aren't that many phone numbers.

One warning: if you also store a lookup table to reverse the tokens, that table is now sensitive data too. Most teams are better off with one-way tokens.

Free-text log lines are hard to search and easy to leak through. Write one JSON record per AI call with clearly separate fields:

log.info("ai_call",     kv("requestId", requestId),     kv("model", model),     kv("promptVersion", "support-reply-v4"),     kv("inputTokens", usage.inputTokens()),     kv("outputTokens", usage.outputTokens()),     kv("latencyMs", elapsed),     kv("promptMasked", masker.mask(prompt)),     kv("responseMasked", masker.mask(response)));

Now dashboards for cost and latency never need to touch the text fields at all. You can also keep the two kinds of data for different lengths of time: metadata for a year, masked content for a couple of weeks. Put the request ID into your logging framework's MDC so every line belonging to one call, including retries and tool calls, carries the same ID.

Streaming trips people up. Tokens arrive in tiny pieces, and a card number can be split across two chunks, which means masking each chunk on its own will miss it. Collect the full response in a buffer, mask it once the stream ends, and log that. If the stream dies halfway, run the partial buffer through the same masker.

Tool calls are the other trap. When the model calls one of your Java methods, the arguments and return values are often the most sensitive thing in the whole exchange: order details, account balances, addresses. Treat them as part of the prompt and response. Log the tool name, duration and outcome by default, and log arguments only after masking.

Masking lowers the risk but never removes it, so surround it with some ordinary discipline:

Give content fields a short retention period. Old prompt archives are liabilities.

Limit who can read AI logs. Debugging a model doesn't mean someone needs to read customer messages.

Encrypt log storage and the pipeline that feeds it.

Log the hashed user ID so you can find and delete one person's records when they ask.

Allow verbose logging in development with fake data, and enforce strict settings in production through configuration, not through people remembering.

A masker that quietly stops working is worse than having none, because everyone assumes the logs are clean. Write tests for it:

@Test void masksEmailAndCardNumber() {     String raw = "Contact me at asha@example.com, card 4111 1111 1111 1111";     String safe = new RegexPiiMasker().mask(raw);     assertFalse(safe.contains("asha@example.com"));     assertFalse(safe.contains("4111"));     assertTrue(safe.contains("[EMAIL]"));     assertTrue(safe.contains("[CARD]")); }

Build a small set of messy, realistic samples: numbers with odd spacing, mixed languages, addresses inside sentences, text pasted from spreadsheets. Run them in your CI pipeline, and add a new case every time a leak turns up. A scheduled job that scans stored logs for patterns that should never appear makes a good safety net too.

A few I'd watch for. Logging raw text at debug level and assuming production never enables it; debug flags get switched on during incidents, so mask at every level. Masking only the user's message while forgetting retrieved documents, system prompts and tool results. Letting SDK exceptions carry the full request body into the logs. Leaving wire-level HTTP logging on in production, which bypasses your masker completely. And treating masking as finished once it ships, when every new prompt template can bring new kinds of data.

Every AI call writes a structured metadata record with a request ID

All prompt and response text goes through a single masker first

Streams are masked after assembly, and tool arguments are masked too

Content logging is sampled, short-lived and access-controlled

Tests cover the masker, with a regression case for every past leak

Client libraries aren't dumping raw payloads into the logs

Good logs turn an AI feature from a black box into something you can actually debug, price and improve. The same logs can also expose the people who trusted you with their details. The way through is fairly plain: record metadata for every call, push all content through one well-tested masker, use pseudonyms when you need to follow a conversation, handle streams and tools carefully, and keep retention short. Set that up before launch, not after the first incident, and you get visibility without regret.

#10445. Google homepage rolling out AI-focused redesign that includes your Gemini chats [Video]

The Google Search homepage is embracing Gemini in a big way, with a new sidebar redesign that puts your chat history just a click away.

Google’s AI ambitions have been anything but subtle in their integration with Search, but Gemini and Search have had some clear separations. But, with its latest update, Google is very much intertwining the two.

At first glance, you’ve got the typical blank canvas with a Google logo and Search bar at the center, but the new changes start with Gemini-powered shortcuts underneath for actions such as creating images, analyzing files, and “brainstorming” with Gemini.

People are coming to Search with more questions that aren’t just one and done – and in testing, this sidebar made a big difference for making progress & continuing to explore the web.

#10444. MSI EdgeMesa N AI+ review: RTX Spark power comes to Windows on Arm desktops, but there are trade-offs

MSI's EdgeMesa N AI+ mini PC brings Nvidia RTX Spark power to a Windows on Arm desktop. It offers broad app compatibility and the same strong local AI performance we've come to expect from Nvidia's GB10 SoC, but it's still very expensive and less expandable than DGX Spark systems.

Solid local AI performance for LLMs and image generation, as expected

Familiar Windows 11 user experience and app compatibility

Could use more peripheral I/O and smarter port placement

Loses the DGX Spark's clustering capability

Getting the best gaming performance requires patience

Why you can trust Tom's Hardware Our expert reviewers spend hours testing and comparing products and services so you can choose the best for you. Find out more about how we test.

Nvidia’s RTX Spark platform, powered by the RTX Spark N1X SoC, passed its most difficult test yet last month at the heart of Windows on Arm laptops. Now we get to see how that chip serves as a Windows on Arm foundation for compact desktops.

We’ve got our hands on the MSI EdgeMesa N AI+, a $7,500 RTX Spark mini PC that gives Windows-native creators and local AI trailblazers access to the same powerful 6144-core Blackwell GPU and 20-core Arm CPU that we recently put to the test in the Surface Laptop Ultra, along with a 128GB pool of unified memory and 4TB of storage in our tested config.

We’ve been testing Nvidia GB10-powered desktop systems for almost a year now in the form of the DGX Spark and friends, and the EdgeMesa N AI+’s design is typical of the breed. It’s wrapped in a slightly hollow-feeling matte aluminum shell with shiny diamond-cut edges and a white plastic back plate.

The design and build quality of this system is a tad underwhelming for the price. It feels nice enough, but It’s hardly a precision-milled statement piece for your desk like a Mac Mini or Mac Studio.

The EdgeMesa draws cool air in through its perforated front panel, which then passes through a dual-fan heatsink with a vapor-chamber base and exhausts through the back panel. The rounded rectangle on the top half of the front panel might look like an optical drive tray, but it’s a purely cosmetic accent.

Around back, the EdgeMesa comes equipped with a 10 Gigabit Ethernet jack, an HDMI 2.1 port, and three USB Type-C 20 Gbps ports with DisplayPort Alt Mode support. A fourth USB-C port is dedicated to the system’s 240 W USB-PD power adapter. The EdgeMesa also has Wi-Fi 7 and Bluetooth 5.4 radios inside.

Even with a wireless keyboard and mouse, three 20Gbps USB-C ports feel a bit skimpy as the sole expansion options on a $7,499 PC that’s positioned as a do-it-all desktop for content creation and gaming. If you need to connect multiple monitors to an RTX Spark via USB-C, those scant options for peripheral connectivity shrink even further.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

For comparison, a similarly priced Mac Studio gives you four 80Gbps Thunderbolt 5 ports around back and two more USB Type-C ports, plus an SD card reader, up front. The Mac even provides a headphone jack, while the EdgeMesa requires you to plug in a USB headset or DAC or pair Bluetooth earbuds for audio output.

Even the Mac Mini gives you three Thunderbolt ports out back and two USB 3 10Gbps ports out front.

In use, the tightly spaced, rear-mounted USB-C ports make for an annoying time when doing frequent unplugging and replugging of devices like card readers and external SSDs that are typical in creative workflows. Even one front-panel USB-C port would have made a huge difference in the ease of use of this design.

The EdgeMesa also drops the handy ConnectX RDMA NIC that lets you cluster DGX Spark systems together to run larger AI models. That omission raises a weighty question: is Windows support and application compatibility important enough to trade away any chance of expanding your local AI foundation in the future?

Having tested DGX Sparks for some time now, I’m not sure that’s a wise trade-off, given that most anybody reading this already has a capable Windows PC or Mac that can link up with a DGX Spark for many local AI compute jobs using the Nvidia Sync app.

DGX Spark prices are rising, but you can get MSI's own EdgeXpert with 128GB of memory and a 4TB SSD on sale for $8,099 right now (or $8,499 at full price). Even a $1000 difference in price for that ConnectX NIC in this rarefied air of PC pricing would give me pause if I'm seriously considering how to add local AI capacity to my home.

If you have no inclination whatsoever to learn Linux or networking concepts and just want a no-questions-asked, AI-capable Windows mini PC that can serve as your primary desktop, an RTX Spark system like this may be the way to go. But you'll want to be really sure of your decision before dropping this much cash.

20-core Arm CPU (10 X925 performance cores, 10 A725 efficiency cores)

6144-core (48 SM) Nvidia Blackwell integrated GPU

128GB LPDDR5X, 273 GB/s shared memory bandwidth

10 Gigabit Ethernet, Wi-Fi 7, Bluetooth 5.4

The performance of the Nvidia GB10 SoC's CPU and GPU resources, whether under DGX OS or Windows, is well-trodden ground by this point. But for a fresh point of comparison, we grabbed AMD’s Ryzen AI Halo, swapped in a spare SSD, and installed Windows 11 Pro, as the Ryzen AI Halo is the only other Windows-ready platform with a large unified memory pool for AI adventurers.

In the Geekbench 7 CPU benchmark under Windows, the Ryzen AI Halo delivers a slightly higher single-threaded score than the MSI EdgeMesa’s RTX Spark Superchip, but its multithreaded score trails the Nvidia part. Using Linux, we would expect both scores to be higher for the Ryzen CPU. But the broader point remains the same: you can expect similar performance from both of these CPUs under Windows.

We’ve left Apple’s Mac Studio with M4 Max in these charts just to emphasize the large performance lead Apple Silicon holds in this benchmark, even with older chips. The current M5 Max would likely open an even larger lead.

In the AgentPerf-Local AI inference benchmark, the MSI EdgeMesa delivered performance practically identical to Microsoft’s Surface Laptop Ultra when running the same Qwen 3.8 27B model with DFlash2 speculative decoding. Its desktop form factor doesn’t seem to give it any extra performance headroom, at least for this LLM serving configuration. Other workloads or models could reveal a difference.

We already tested the RTX Spark against the Ryzen AI Halo in AgentPerf-Local in our Surface Laptop Ultra review, and AMD’s platform ended up way behind GB10 in those tests. And in this new round of testing, AMD's LLM performance is worse under Windows with the Vulkan backend than in Linux with ROCm, taking a whopping four times as long as the EdgeMesa to complete the benchmark.

Strix Halo might have been an appealing local inference option when it was the only open and relatively hackable unified memory platform around, but if you’re serious about local AI performance (and especially if tokens equal dollars for you), a GB10 system is the superior choice in late 2026.

Gaming on RTX Spark continues to be a bit of a weird time, and the MSI EdgeMesa N doesn’t change that experience, even with a more generous power and thermal budget than the Surface Laptop Ultra I've already tested.

This platform just doesn’t respond to tuning as we might expect from our experience with traditional gaming PCs and laptops. That weirdness stems, in part, from the imbalance between memory bandwidth and compute resources on GB10.

The 48 SMs of the RTX Spark N1X GPU have to share just 273 GB/s of LPDDR5X bandwidth with the CPU. Compare that with the 672 GB/s of dedicated bandwidth the similarly-specced RTX 5070 enjoys from its 12 GB of GDDR7.

Architectural decisions aside, the RTX Spark platform's gaming performance isn't helped by the fact that full GPU utilization is seemingly hampered by the overhead of Prism x86 emulation in most games. Few (if any) major titles have Arm ports, so this is just a fact of life for the foreseeable future if you buy one of these systems.

Let's see how this all plays out in practice for the RTX Spark versus the Radeon 8060S on board AMD's Strix Halo.

In the 3DMark Steel Nomad raster benchmark, the RTX Spark holds a wide lead over the Ryzen AI Halo, delivering a roughly 56% higher score. That result would suggest a knockout in games for Nvidia’s platform.

But Steel Nomad isn’t representative of real-world gaming performance on the RTX Spark. If we fire up Control Resonant on both platforms and choose the High raster preset at 1440p with Performance upscaling (a 1280x720 input resolution), the RTX Spark and Strix Halo end up a lot closer.

Notably, though, the RTX Spark desktop delivers considerably worse 1% low FPS performance than the Ryzen AI Halo, and this does manifest as visible stutter and hitching. So it's not a clear win for the Nvidia platform.

AMD fans might take heart in the fact that Strix Halo can get this close to the RTX Spark on this raster gaming baseline, but the Radeon 8060S doesn’t have nearly as many tricks up its sleeve to further juice performance.

For one, the quality of FSR 4 upscaling at lower input resolutions on RDNA 3.5 is still not up to par with what you can get out of DLSS 4.5. For two, the Radeon 8060S’s RDNA 3.5 CUs don't match up well against the Spark's Blackwell-powered RT chops.

And FSR Frame Generation is still limited to a doubling of a base frame rate, so you can’t smooth out gameplay on a high-refresh-rate monitor nearly as well as you can with DLSS MFG.

So even if their raster baseline frame rates are similar, the Radeon 8060S isn't delivering the same image quality in Control Resonant as an RTX Spark system does with all its DLSS features in play.

RT is the next step for squeezing better image quality out of Control Resonant. Surprisingly, enabling RT Medium only results in a 14% performance hit on the Spark versus 38% on the AI Halo. That might be due in part to the efficiency of DLSS Ray Reconstruction versus the Northlight engine's built-in denoiser, but the RT prowess of Blackwell certainly can't hurt.

A 52.3 FPS average with a 75 ms average PC latency is an OK baseline — at least in the context of an RTX Spark system — for adding MFG to the mix for a smoother experience, especially since Control Resonant seems to link Nvidia Reflex latency reduction to that tech anyway. But dialing in MFG 3X with the default MFG Preset B model still results in a PC latency of 96 ms on average, which is too high to be enjoyable, even if we give the Spark some slack.

I found that the new MFG Preset C, available through the Nvidia App, significantly lowered latency versus the more compute-intensive Preset B. While input latency with Preset C is still high at about 84 ms, we have to take every bit of help we can get here, and the resulting 145 FPS average is notably smoother than native gameplay on my 160 Hz monitor.

We only had time to dive into this one title in our testing, but when Nvidia says the RTX Spark platform is great for 1440p gaming, our experience suggests that you need to be ready to tweak DLSS upscaling and dial in MFG for more demanding games. And look: that’s fine. These are proven technologies at this point, and they let us get a better experience out of this platform than native rendering would allow.

But all of these data points should go to show that if you're used to the low input latency and predictable performance scaling characteristics of an x86 desktop gaming PC with a discrete GPU, the RTX Spark platform still has a ways to go before it can truly replace such a system.

During our AgentPerf-Local LLM benchmark, the EdgeMesa N AI+ pulls about 140 W from the wall, and under that load, it produces just 38.2 dBA at 18” (46 cm) according to our calibrated SPL meter, well below the 47.4 dBA of the RTX Spark-powered Surface Laptop Ultra and the 48 dBA of the Ryzen AI Halo.

That makes the EdgeMesa an easy system to live with for local AI work, especially if it’s moonlighting as an AI inference server or always-on agent host in shared spaces.

Under gaming workloads, the EdgeMesa draws about 200 W. With more heat to dissipate, the MSI box’s noise output rises to 40.7 dBA, which is still much quieter than the average gaming laptop and will easily be covered by speakers or a closed-back gaming headset.

The Ryzen AI Halo, on the other hand, emits a piercing 46.9 dBA whine while gaming, much closer to what we expect from a gaming laptop. And that’s not because it has that much more heat to dissipate; the AI Halo is pulling about 209 W with Control Resonant running.

Under that same load, the EdgeMesa’s fans have a slightly high-pitched character to them, but it’s much less pronounced than the typical gaming laptop. Mostly, the sound from the system is that of air moving, and it's easy to ignore (so much so that I missed the aural cues I sometimes listen for when a benchmark finishes).

MSI deserves high praise for this system’s thermal design and cooling system. It’s both quiet and pleasant-sounding, and those factors aren’t always a given with mini PCs.

If you’re a hardcore Windows fan who’s been intrigued by Nvidia’s DGX Sparks for local AI, but you've been repelled by the idea of using Linux, RTX Spark desktops like this MSI EdgeMesa N AI+ finally pair your preferred OS with the same 6144-core Nvidia Blackwell GPU, 20-core Arm CPU, and up to 128GB of unified memory that give those systems their local AI grunt.

But that potential comes with tradeoffs that you’ll need to weigh carefully before buying. And at an eye-popping $7,499 for the 128GB EdgeMesa configuration with a 4TB SSD that we tested, those tradeoffs weigh all the more heavily.

The MSI EdgeMesa N AI+ delivers essentially the same performance from the RTX Spark N1X SoC (aka GB10) we’ve come to know and love for about a year now. It’s reasonably fast for local LLM inference, and it offers leading performance with generative AI workflows like images and videos in ComfyUI. Its Arm CPU offers solid enough single-threaded and multi-threaded performance for general computing tasks, and it can game in a pinch. The EdgeMesa is laudably quiet, too — no small feat for a compact PC like this.

But in exchange for Windows support, RTX Spark desktops lose the valuable expandability offered by the ConnectX 7 RDMA NIC that comes standard on DGX Spark boxes. So if you want to try your hand at running a bigger, more intelligent LLM than you can fit in one of these systems’ unified memory pools through clustering, you are forever out of luck if you go down the RTX path.

Even if you can live without the ConnectX NIC, we’re a bit let down by the peripheral connectivity the EdgeMesa N AI+ offers, both for its price and for its ambitions as a do-it-all creator and gaming system.

With just three 20 Gbps USB Type-C ports on the back panel that have to do double duty as display outputs in multi-monitor setups, folks who have to juggle external storage and card readers frequently may find both the number and placement of those ports an obstacle to a smooth workflow. We really would have liked to see more expansion ports on this system overall, or at least one on the front panel.

Nvidia loves to tout the gaming capability of Windows on Arm with RTX Spark systems, but we're still not that impressed by the experience in practice, especially for a system this expensive.

It's nice that you can enjoy PC gaming on these systems at all, but even in a desktop with more power and thermal budget to spare, the gaming performance and responsiveness we saw can only be described as adequate. We were especially surprised by the gulf in 1% low FPS between the RTX Spark and the much cheaper Ryzen AI Halo in Control Resonant, despite the RTX Spark N1X's much stronger GPU on paper.

In general, we find that it takes a lot of careful tweaking and application of DLSS magic to get around this platform's wrinkles for gaming (or a willingness to just let the Nvidia App optimize your game settings). In other words, don't retire your dedicated gaming PC or laptop just yet.

The large memory pools available from RTX Spark systems have uses beyond local AI, of course. Nvidia GPUs are favorites for creative work on Windows, and if you work with massive 3D or game development projects, or you edit lots of high-resolution video, RTX Sparks are the first unified memory systems to pair a powerful Nvidia GPU with this much VRAM for Windows users.

Adobe just updated several of its Creative Suite apps with Arm-native versions, so if you’re trying to blaze the trail of agentic content creation and generation in this modern age, an RTX Spark desktop (or laptop, for that matter) might be intriguing.

But we feel like $7,499 is still a ton of money to spend on a system like this to run Adobe's apps specifically, unless a large unified memory pool is particularly useful for your work today.

All told, we're left scratching our heads a bit about who RTX Spark desktops are really for. RTX Spark laptops meaningfully expanded the capabilities of mobile Windows on Arm systems thanks to the unmatched AI compute resources of their Blackwell GPUs and support for the full Nvidia software stack, along with solid CPU performance and battery life.

But compared to both the existing DGX Spark platform and other mini PCs, RTX Spark desktops like the MSI EdgeMesa N AI+ ask you to give up a lot in exchange for the Nvidia-powered Windows on Arm experience, especially for the high prices they’re likely to command amid the current silicon supply crunch.

If you’re a hardcore Windows-native developer or creator looking to explore the boundaries of what’s possible from local and agentic AI and want the best possible Microsoft-powered foundation for those activities, an RTX Spark desktop might be the right choice for the job, assuming you have the cash.

But if you're just curious, we'd wait for the value proposition of local or agentic AI under Windows to firm up a bit and for the prices of these systems to come down before spending thousands of dollars on a still-fuzzy vision of the future of computing.

As the Senior Analyst, Graphics at Tom's Hardware, Jeff Kampman covers everything that has to do with graphics cards, gaming performance, and more. From integrated graphics processors to discrete graphics cards to the hyperscale installations powering our AI future, if it's got a GPU in it, Jeff is on it.

#10442. Google’s 3D emoji has wild Android spec requirements: 10GB of free space, 6GB RAM

Google introduced 3D emoji on Pixel this year, and has just detailed the minimum specifications required to get them on your Android phone.

Noto 3D emoji goes from vector-based 2D emoji to a high-resolution bitmap font. This results in “high-resolution designs” for emoji “with more texture and dimension to characters and icons.” Google has an FAQ section about why the new emoji might look low resolution:

Vector vs. bitmap rendering: Older Android emoji used 2D vector graphics, which scale infinitely to any size without losing resolution. The 3D emoji are bitmap graphics, which are pre-rendered pixel-based images. When these images are scaled up by an app or zoomed in, the pixels stretch, which can cause a softer or lower-resolution appearance.

The Google support article published this week lists the minimum specs your Android device must have to get 3D emoji:

The last two bullet points are rather surprising. In fact, Google even lists instructions for checking how much memory and storage your device has:

In terms of availability, Google says a supported device will “automatically download the 3D emoji when it’s on Wi-Fi and charging.”

At this time, there isn’t a way to force download the emoji.

Google announced 3D emoji in May, with the new design shipping on the Pixel 11 in August. It has since arrived on older Pixel devices.

In terms of OEM availability, Google explicitly says “Samsung devices use a proprietary emoji font and do not receive the 3D font update.” Additionally:

This is also true for third-party messaging apps, such as WhatsApp, who use their own emoji fonts rather than relying on your device’s system font.

#10438. How to Build a Unified Lead Tracking Pipeline for Web Forms and Phone Calls

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

A visitor clicks a search ad, submits a quote request, and calls your sales team ten minutes later. The form integration creates a CRM contact. The phone platform records a call. By Friday, the marketing report shows two leads, while sales remembers one conversation that turned into a deal.

Both systems captured something useful. The problem is that neither record explains how the two interactions belong together.

A unified tracking pipeline gives forms and calls a common event format, preserves their acquisition context, and connects them to CRM outcomes. It also needs to survive a less tidy reality: repeated callbacks, missing campaign data, delayed updates, and a CRM that occasionally refuses requests.

This tutorial builds the core of that pipeline with JavaScript and PostgreSQL. We will capture campaign parameters, define a shared event contract, and persist events alongside the work needed to deliver them. Provider authentication and CRM requests belong in adapters around this core, using the documentation for the services you choose.

Before writing an endpoint, decide what you are counting. A phone call can produce several status callbacks. A person can submit two forms. An existing customer can contact sales about a new purchase.

Use separate identifiers for these different things:

This separation prevents two common mistakes. Deduplicating by call ID alone can discard later status updates. Counting every callback as a lead inflates demand.

For this implementation, each event is unique within a tenant and source. A tenant is the business whose data you are processing; the source identifies a website or provider account. The same provider event ID can therefore appear in two accounts without causing a collision.

Keep the original interactions even after you connect them to one contact. The form and the call are both useful evidence. Your reporting layer decides whether they represent one opportunity.

Campaign parameters are easiest to capture when the visitor first arrives. Waiting until the form submission can lose them if the visitor has already moved to another page.

Google's campaign URL documentation explains the roles of utm_source, utm_medium, and utm_campaign. The following helper reads those fields and a few optional ones, without copying the entire query string:

const params = new URL(url).searchParams;

"utm_source", "utm_medium", "utm_campaign",

return Object.fromEntries(keys.flatMap(key => {

The browser's URLSearchParams interface handles query parsing and decoding. It saves you from writing string-splitting code that breaks on encoded characters.

Run this helper through your site's existing consent and attribution flow. Where storage is permitted, associate the result with an opaque attribution reference in your backend and retain that reference through the visit. A later form submission sends the reference; the server resolves it within the correct tenant and visitor session. The form must still work when attribution storage is unavailable.

Keep first-touch and current-touch records separately if you need both. Overwriting the first campaign every time someone returns makes the eventual report answer a different question.

Treat campaign labels as untrusted input. Visitors can edit query strings and hidden fields. Validate them on the server, escape them when displayed, and never use them as authorization or as the sole evidence for commission payments. Avoid putting names, email addresses, or other personal information in UTM values.

Missing tags also need careful handling. An empty UTM object only tells you that no accepted UTM values were captured. Classifying a visit as direct, organic, or referral requires additional evidence and an explicit attribution rule.

Forms carry browser context naturally. Phone calls need a bridge between the number dialed and the marketing interaction that prompted the call.

There are two useful levels of precision. A number assigned to one campaign can identify that campaign. Dynamic number insertion can support a more detailed association by displaying tracking numbers according to a visitor or session mapping. That association depends on the provider's number allocation and expiry rules.

Providers differ in how they implement this, and in what they expose beyond it, such as keyword-level reporting or conversation analysis. If you are still choosing one, this comparison of attribution software with call tracking is a useful starting point. At the integration boundary, check which event identifiers, attribution fields, delivery mechanisms, and authentication options your configured product exposes.

Keep that provider-specific work in an adapter. Its job is to turn an authenticated notification into your event contract and resolve any attribution reference supported by the provider's data. The application schema below is an internal contract; each provider adapter translates its documented payload into that contract.

Store the basis of a match with the attribution record. For example, session_match, campaign_number, and unknown make different levels of evidence visible to reporting. If a number has been reassigned, use the mapping valid when the call occurred, not whichever mapping happens to be current when the callback arrives. If the evidence is ambiguous, leave the association unresolved.

Caller ID is a separate issue. A shared office number, a withheld number, or an outdated contact record can make automatic identity matching unreliable. A confirmed CRM contact ID or an authenticated account provides a stronger link. Treat a matching phone number as a candidate for reconciliation rather than proof that two records belong to the same person.

A shared event envelope lets the rest of your application work with a small, predictable vocabulary. Here is an illustrative call event:

"occurred_at": "2026-11-06T10:14:00.000Z",

The event ID identifies this occurrence. The interaction ID groups it with other events for the same call. contact_ref can remain empty until you have enough evidence to connect the interaction to a contact.

Keep contact details in a controlled contact store and campaign details in an attribution store. The references above point to those records; they are not credentials and must not grant access on their own.

The following factory checks the shared envelope. Each adapter still needs its own validation for provider fields and the contents of data.

"form.submitted", "call.started", "call.completed",

export function makeEvent(input, trusted) {

const ids = [trusted.tenantId, trusted.source,

if (ids.some(id => typeof id !== "string" ||

throw new TypeError("Invalid event identifiers");

throw new TypeError("Unsupported event type");

const time = Date.parse(input.occurredAt);

if (typeof input.occurredAt !== "string" ||

new Date(time).toISOString() !== input.occurredAt) {

throw new TypeError("Expected a canonical UTC timestamp");

attribution_ref: input.attributionRef ?? null,

Resolve trusted from server configuration and the authenticated integration. Never let a submitted tenant_id choose whose records a webhook can write. Resolve contact and attribution references within that same tenant.

For a form, issue a submission ID and reuse it when retrying the same submission. Generate a new one when the visitor deliberately starts another inquiry. Preserve the originally accepted timestamp as well. For a phone event, prefer the provider's stable event ID. If the provider documents a sequence number, a call ID plus that sequence can identify an occurrence within a provider account.

Twilio's call resource documentation provides a concrete example: call progress callbacks include a SequenceNumber, and separate HTTP requests can arrive out of order. Preserve that sequence in the adapter. A late “ringing” event should remain in the history without replacing a newer terminal state.

Authenticate before calling this factory. Use the provider's supported validator and the exact request representation it requires. Twilio's webhook security guide describes signature validation, including the importance of the request URL and payload. Other providers may use different mechanisms. Public form endpoints need their own input limits, abuse controls, and appropriate CSRF protection.

Consider a failure between these two operations: the application saves an event, then crashes before scheduling its CRM update. The event exists, but nothing is waiting to deliver it.

A transactional outbox addresses that gap. Save the event and a delivery task in the same database transaction, then let a worker process the task. AWS explains this dual-write problem in its transactional outbox guidance.

Here is a compact schema. received_at records ingestion time, while the envelope retains the event's original occurred_at value.

received_at timestamptz NOT NULL DEFAULT now(),

PRIMARY KEY (tenant_id, source, event_id)

id bigint GENERATED ALWAYS AS IDENTITY PRIMARY KEY,

available_at timestamptz NOT NULL DEFAULT now(),

FOREIGN KEY (tenant_id, source, event_id)

REFERENCES lead_events (tenant_id, source, event_id)

The ingestion function below accepts a pg pool and a normalized event. Install pg in your Node.js project, save the function as persist.mjs, and initialize the pool using your application's database configuration.

export async function persistEvent(pool, event) {

const key = [event.tenant_id, event.source, event.event_id];

const values = [...key, JSON.stringify(event)];

await client.query("BEGIN ISOLATION LEVEL READ COMMITTED");

ON CONFLICT (tenant_id, source, event_id) DO NOTHING

WHERE tenant_id = $1 AND source = $2 AND event_id = $3

const error = new Error("Event ID reused with new data");

INSERT INTO crm_outbox (tenant_id, source, event_id)

return { inserted: inserted.rowCount === 1 };

PostgreSQL's INSERT documentation defines the conflict handling used here. The unique key makes duplicate detection hold across application processes. A duplicate with the same normalized payload creates no second task; reuse of the key with different data raises a conflict for investigation.

This depends on immutable event bodies. Do not add a fresh receipt timestamp, random identifier, or current CRM status to the normalized payload on each delivery. Store later enrichment separately or emit a new event. Otherwise an ordinary retry will appear to be a conflicting event.

The function deliberately uses one database client throughout the transaction, as required by the node-postgres transaction documentation. Parameter values are passed separately from SQL, following its parameterized query guidance.

After this function commits, acknowledge the notification using the response expected by the sender. An identical duplicate should also receive a successful acknowledgement. If persistence fails, return the appropriate failure response and rely on the configured retry or reconciliation process. A success response sent before durable storage can turn a temporary database failure into a missing event.

For voice integrations, distinguish an informational status callback from a request asking your application how to handle a live call. They can require different response bodies, as explained in the Twilio webhooks FAQ.

The outbox gives you durable work to process. It does not make a remote CRM operation happen exactly once.

Imagine the CRM saves an update, but the worker crashes before setting processed_at. When the task runs again, the same request may reach the CRM twice. Design for that case explicitly.

Use a stable external identifier for each contact, opportunity, and activity, and check which deduplication guarantees the destination supports. HubSpot's contacts API documentation, for example, describes upserts using unique properties. Support for a contact upsert does not automatically make every associated activity or workflow idempotent. Verify the behavior of each operation you send.

If the destination cannot deduplicate a create request and a timeout leaves its result uncertain, reconcile that operation before repeating it. A local “sent” flag alone cannot prove whether the remote write happened.

The worker should claim due tasks, commit a recoverable lease, and perform network calls outside the database transaction. A lease allows another worker to recover work after a crash. When multiple workers poll the outbox, PostgreSQL's SKIP LOCKED behavior can help claim separate rows, but the lease and its recovery logic still need to be implemented.

Retry transient failures with increasing delays and jitter. Respect the destination's rate-limit instructions. Send persistent validation failures to a review queue with a useful error category, and provide a way to replay them after correction. The schema above is the ingestion foundation; a production worker needs lease fields, failure details, and a retry policy appropriate to its destination.

Also preserve ordering where it changes meaning. If two events update the same contact or opportunity, process them serially or reject stale updates using a stored version. A historical event should not move a won opportunity back to an earlier stage.

Once both channels reach the CRM, connect them to the result the business cares about. A form submission is an inquiry. A completed phone call is a technical outcome. Neither alone establishes that a lead qualified or a purchase happened. If agents record call dispositions, such as whether a call was qualified or transferred to a closer, that field is a richer signal than call completion status alone.

Have the CRM or the system that owns the transaction emit explicit outcome events, such as lead.qualified and deal.won. Include the internal opportunity ID and the original outcome time. Model reversals or cancellations explicitly if they affect revenue reporting.

Then choose the unit behind each metric. For a report about opportunities acquired during a particular month, a qualification rate can use qualified opportunities divided by all opportunities in that acquisition cohort. Counting qualification events received during the month would answer a different question and could include older inquiries.

Deduplicate opportunities before calculating channel totals. A person who submits a form and calls about the same purchase should retain both interactions, while the opportunity receives credit according to your chosen attribution rule. Label that rule in the report so first-touch and last-touch numbers are not compared as though they measure the same thing.

Measure data quality alongside performance. Track unresolved attribution, duplicate deliveries, ingestion failures, and the age of the oldest pending outbox task. Once several sources and destinations feed the same reports, a data observability platform can watch these signals across the whole pipeline instead of relying on checks inside a single worker. An apparently quiet campaign may actually have a broken integration.

Operational logs should contain enough context to investigate failures without becoming another customer database. Use internal references and error categories, and avoid dumping webhook bodies into logs. The OWASP Logging Cheat Sheet provides guidance on excluding or protecting sensitive values.

A successful form submission proves little about retries or missing data. Before connecting production traffic, test the cases that could distort reporting or lose an inquiry.

Run database cases against PostgreSQL with separate connections so the tests exercise actual constraints and transaction behavior. Use the provider's test tools or signed fixtures for webhook authentication. Test CRM retries against a sandbox with the destination's real duplicate-handling rules.

Start rollout with one form and one phone source. Trace a known inquiry from its captured campaign through its interactions to its CRM outcome. Reconcile event totals against the source systems over the same time window, allowing for delivery delays. Expand once that path is explainable and failures are visible.

The same adapter pattern extends to inquiries that start offline, such as leads collected at a trade show and exported as a CSV or XLSX file. Import each row as a form.submitted event with the event as its source, a stable row identifier as event_id, and the original capture time as occurred_at. Because the outbox treats an identical event as a duplicate, re-importing the same export won’t create a second CRM task.

What is a unified lead tracking pipeline?

It is a workflow that converts inquiries from different channels into a shared data model, preserves their acquisition context, and connects them to business outcomes. Forms and calls keep their own interaction records, while contacts and opportunities provide the relationships needed for reporting.

Can I connect a phone call to a website visit?

Sometimes. Dynamic number insertion can provide a session or visitor association when the provider's mapping is available and valid. A campaign-specific number usually supports campaign-level attribution. If the caller uses a saved number, switches devices, or calls after a mapping expires, the available evidence may be less precise.

Handle duplicate delivery and identity resolution separately. Stable event IDs and a database constraint prevent repeated notifications from producing repeated work. Connecting several interactions to one contact or opportunity requires a separate matching policy, with ambiguous cases left unresolved until there is enough evidence.

Should a completed call count as a conversion?

Only if that is the conversion your report explicitly measures. A completed call does not establish that a prospect qualified or bought something. Keep technical call status separate from CRM outcomes and use the latter when reporting qualified demand or revenue.

Do I need a message broker for this architecture?

You can begin with PostgreSQL and an outbox worker if that setup meets your throughput and latency needs. A broker becomes useful when several consumers need independent delivery, or when buffering and scale justify the additional operations. In either case, retries still require idempotent consumers.

What happens when tracking consent is unavailable?

Keep the inquiry flow usable and apply your site's existing consent rules to optional attribution collection and storage. Accept permitted form or call data, leave unavailable attribution empty, and avoid reconstructing a visitor identity through fingerprinting. Reports should expose that missing context instead of inventing a campaign match.
