#10437. Cost per task: Using cheap classifiers to route across Gemini models and thinking levels

Per-token API prices keep falling, yet engineering bills for autonomous coding agents keep climbing. Price per million tokens isn't the metric that actually governs production economics anymore. The dominating metric is now cost per successful task: total spend across every loop iteration, tool call, retry and internal thinking pass divided by the number of tasks that pass verification.

When an agent calls a high-thinking reasoning configuration for every micro-decision in its harness, you're paying deep-reasoning prices for questions a lightweight configuration already answers accurately. To prevent this unnecessary overhead, consider adding a lightweight routing layer that can intercept routine decisions before they ever reach an expensive reasoning model.That is the premise behind wiring TypeSafe's Jev classifier in front of Google Antigravity's model calls. Rather than generating text token by token, Jev—a specialized structured-output classifier designed for high-throughput decision pipelines—evaluates structured classification primitives in a single forward pass: a Choice from a list, a numeric Score or a Noul, which returns a 0 to 1 probability for a binary statement.

For full disclosure, Google has no partnership with TypeSafe, and this article is not an endorsement of TypeSafe or its products. Jev is used here as one example of a lightweight classifier, and the same routing pattern can be built with other classifiers, including Google-native ones. The Getting started with discriminative models codelab, for example, runs the same kind of classifier on DiffusionGemma, Google DeepMind's open-weights model, in your own Google Cloud project.

We ran a 50-question, auto-graded, coding-agent benchmark against live Gemini and Jev APIs to test whether a near-free classifier can route each task to the cheapest model that still gets it right. Here's what we learned:

The naive way to ask Jev to route a coding task is a single broad Choice question: simple or complex. When we tested that first on 16 coding-agent tasks, Jev routed nearly everything to simple, even tasks requiring an LRU cache trace or modular exponentiation. TypeSafe's documentation warns against this exact anti-pattern. If a question weighs multiple factors or requires a meta-judgment, decompose it into an atomic check.

When we replaced the broad Choice question with one atomic Noul question, it fixed the separation immediately. The question was: does answering this require performing more than one sequential calculation, simulation step or logical deduction, rather than a single direct lookup, format check or one-step classification?

On those initial 16 tasks, Jev routed 13 of 16 in agreement with our hand-labeled difficulty on the first run and 14 of 16 on the second pass. Many engineering teams ask whether they even need a classifier API when they can write a zero-cost regex and prompt-length heuristic in Python, matching words like sequence, modulo and race condition or escalating any prompt over 35 words.

On the 16 development questions used to write those exact rules, the local heuristic scored 15 of 16, or 93.8%. To test whether that heuristic survived contact with realistic coding-agent traffic, we froze its code without changing a character and added 34 unseen held-out tasks across seven coding-agent buckets: fact extraction, schema validation, short classification, single-hop code checks, state simulation traces, math or logic chains and subtle language-semantics bugs.

On the 34 held-out questions, the frozen heuristic dropped 23.2 percentage points to 70.6%. Realistic simple tasks such as extracting a port from a Kubernetes YAML manifest, a file path from a unified git diff header or a line number from a Python traceback exceeded 35 words and triggered false escalations. Conversely, concise multi-step code traces such as mutable default arguments across calls, Python dictionary key hashing on {1: "a", True: "b", 1.0: "c"} and C short-circuit evaluation lacked the heuristic's hardcoded puzzle words and were falsely routed to the simple path. Jev classified all 34 unseen tasks with 100.0% agreement, 34 of 34, reaching 48 of 50, or 96.0%, across the full suite.

A common mistake when building a fast-path router is assuming that setting a low maxOutputTokens cap on a reasoning model will make it spend less on easy tasks. Our 50-question data shows why that assumption fails.

When we compared gemini-3.8-flash with maxOutputTokens=300 against gemini-3.8-flash with maxOutputTokens=4096 while leaving thinkingConfig at the API default, both configurations spent almost identical thinking tokens on the 27 tasks Jev labeled SIMPLE:

Note: Figures reflect single-run experimental measurements on a sample test suite; actual latencies, token counts, and billed costs will vary based on prompt length, context caching, and production load.

Capping the model's output at 300 tokens didn't save any money on easy tasks. The cap is only a ceiling, and easy answers never came close to it. On the easy tasks, the capped version actually cost slightly more than the uncapped one. All the cap did was break 7 of the 25 hard tasks. On six of them, the model ran out of room while still reasoning and never gave an answer. On the seventh, it squeezed its reasoning to fit, finished on time and got the answer wrong.

So a Jev router that sends easy tasks to the capped model and hard ones to normal Flash got all 50 right. It still cost slightly more than skipping the router and using normal Flash for everything, and it was slower. If your cheap option doesn't actually make the model think less on easy tasks, no router can save you money. Not even a perfect one that costs nothing.

The obvious way to spend less on thinking is to ask the model to think less. So we ran a second round of tests with two lighter options: Flash-Lite, which doesn't think at all by default, and Gemini 3.8 Flash with its thinking level set to low. We added those results to the same results file, matched question by question.

A note on timing. Most of the router's time per task is spent in Flash-Lite and Flash calls, which we measured in the same run and time window as the plain Flash baseline, so the speed comparison between them is fair. Jev's own decision is a small share of the total and comes from the first round. Speed comparisons against default Flash or Pro mix the two rounds, so read those as approximate.

Note: Figures reflect single-run experimental measurements on a sample test suite; actual latencies, token counts, and billed costs will vary based on prompt length, context caching, and production load.

The 7-bucket breakdown shows where gemini-3.5-flash-lite and the heuristic router missed tasks and how Router Jev: Flash-Lite to 3.8 LOW recovered 100% accuracy:

The strongest setup without a router was Gemini 3.8 Flash on low thinking. It solved all 50 tasks. That's the number a router had to beat.

The Jev router beat it. It sends every task to Flash-Lite first and escalates only what Jev flags as complex. It also solved all 50 tasks, and it can help reduce both cost and response time compared with running low thinking on everything. On prompts this short the cost difference per task is small, so for an interactive agent the shorter wait is the bigger benefit.

Why it works. On its own, Flash-Lite answered every simple task in half a second but missed 6 of the 25 hard ones. Jev flagged all 6 as complex with high confidence and escalated them. That brought its cost close to what a perfect router would achieve.

The keyword heuristic was cheaper, but only because it got things wrong. It sent 5 hard tasks to Flash-Lite, and 2 of them failed, for 48 of 50.

Escalating to a heavier setting didn't pay here. Routing hard tasks to Flash on default thinking was cheaper than running default thinking everywhere. It still cost more than running low thinking on everything, which already solved all 50. A heavier escalation target only makes sense when the low setting starts failing.

Limits: one run per setup, 50 prompts with hand-assigned difficulty and simple automatic grading.

Two things in our results only show up once you look past it.

Routing and caching pull in opposite directions. Our test prompts were short, standalone tasks, and on those the Jev router helped reduce both cost and response time. Real coding agents often look different. They send the same big block of context on every turn, such as a map of the repository and the tool definitions. Agent Platform Context Caching stores that block so you pay 75% to 90% less to resend it. The catch is that a cache belongs to one model. If your agent switches between Flash-Lite and Flash from turn to turn, it either keeps two caches or loses the discount. The practical split: route short, self-contained tasks like log parsing or schema checks, and keep long, context-heavy turns on one model.

Match the model to the workload. Gemini 3.1 Pro is built for deep, multi-step reasoning, and it brings that same depth to every request, including short lookups that don't need it. For a workload made mostly of quick checks and extractions, a Flash configuration is the better fit, and Pro is best saved for the tasks that need its reasoning depth. Model configurations can also apply different safety settings to security-related prompts. In our run, the Pro configuration declined two short code-security review prompts that Flash answered. If your agent performs security reviews, test those prompts on the configuration you plan to use.

Other ways to build the router. Jev is one way to get a fast classifier, but not the only one. The Getting started with discriminative models codelab runs the same Choice, Score and Noul questions on DiffusionGemma, Google DeepMind's open-weights model, hosted on a Compute Engine GPU VM or on Cloud Run in your own Google Cloud project. An open-source server exposes the same API in front of it, so the routing code in this article only needs a different endpoint. Our results come from Jev, so measure accuracy and latency on your own tasks before switching, and keep in mind that a self-hosted model is billed by the GPU-hour rather than by the token.

Taken together, the results give three practical rules.

Start with Flash on low thinking before you build a router. It solved all 50 tasks and cost considerably less than the default setting. Lowering the output token limit is not a substitute, because it doesn't reduce how much the model thinks on easy tasks. Set the thinking level directly, with thinkingLevel: "LOW".

Use Jev to route short, standalone tasks. Send simple tasks to Flash-Lite and hard ones to Flash on low thinking. Ask Jev one simple yes-or-no question per factor. In our test, this helped reduce cost and response time compared with running Flash on low thinking for everything, and came close to what a perfect router would achieve. Keep long, context-heavy turns on a single model so caching keeps working. For high-volume warehouse workloads, Jeff Nelson's BigQuery and Cloud Run cascade pattern applies the same idea.

Measure what your agent actually spends. Send your agent's traces and thinking-token counts to Cloud Logging and BigQuery, so you can see which tasks drive your spend and catch truncation or retries that inflate it. The benchmark code will be published after open-source review.

In summary, the most cost-effective way to run an agent is to match each task to the model that fits it. Start with a fast model on a light setting. You'll often find it already gets the job done. Then add a small, almost free classifier like Jev to send only the hard tasks to a stronger model, and ask simple yes-or-no questions rather than one broad one. Judge every option by what it costs per task done right, not per token.

#10436. Lawyer’s $9,450 private AI server with 12 used Nvidia V100s now runs every DeepSeek V4.1 Flash expert

He says it decodes one request faster than published DGX Spark stacks, at half the price of three.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

A lawyer has upped the ante by upgrading his private AI server from eight to 12 used Nvidia V100s, which can run every DeepSeek V4.1 Flash expert at 4-bit NVFP4. Performance can reach up to 184 tokens per second for code, and long documents start answering in about half the time as before. He says it can outrun published DGX Spark stacks at decoding a single request, for half the price of three units, based on the $9,450 he paid.

Some of the best movie franchises come in threes, and Nick Mermiges seems determined to match that, taking his original setup — which he previously upgraded with more memory and other fixes — and expanding it for fuller precision, after a previous upgrade got him most of the way there. The newest upgrade, this time, involves a third board of V100s, for a total of 12 with four per board. There may be a method to his madness.

The original eight 32GB cards could manage 254 GiB, but the experts for the chosen model, with Nvidia’s NVFP4 release, need 278.9 GiB. His previous workaround was to have every expert on the cards at 2-bit precision, with the most-used, 5,949 or about 38.7%, swapped to NVFP4 until the cards were full. Now, all 15,360 experts can live on the cards in their full glory, as he intended, with “none compressed further,” according to his GitHub repo. In a longer write-up on his site, he says the expert values “equal the ones in DeepSeek’s own release,” although other parts of the model use compact forms.

A keen observer will notice a limitation of the V100s, which he spells out: the V100 has no FP4 or FP8 tensor cores. Every NVFP4 number has to be unpacked in software, first, and into half precision. The compact experts still pay off “because the cards read fewer bytes, not because they compute faster.” This helps explain why a card from 2018 can run a format from the Blackwell era.

The model’s layers are, again, divided among the boards, this time with 0–13, 14–27, and 28–39, and each board’s section is split across its own four cards. The cards on a board share Nvidia’s fast link, or NVLink, and the hand-offs between boards cross over the host’s PCIe through two separate PCIe switches. The first two boards share a PEX88096 switch, so their hand-off stays inside it, but the third board is behind a separate PLX 8749, so the second hand-off also has to cross the host’s PCIe bridges, which adds latency.

All this trouble is worth it, though, as with different settings — each card’s power cap raised from 150 W to 175 W and its clock cap lowered from 1,522 MHz to 1,452 MHz — there are noticeable improvements to performance as well as precision.

His measurements show the first code token, with 256K context, at 58 seconds, almost twice as fast as the 108.4 seconds after his previous upgrade. Prompt reading is at “about 4,500 tokens a second” past 32K, which is more than twice the roughly 2,200 tokens a second of his first build, though the higher power cap likely accounts for some of that. Code decode, meanwhile, is 23% faster at 1K, but shows no real gains at 32K and up, so the real gain is a reduced wait for the first token.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

There was some eagerness to get more out of the system by pushing each card to 200 W for a day, but “the host tripped once under four long chats at once,” implying the power draw was perhaps too much. He also worked to optimize the setup as much as possible, but four optimizations — a conversation store that saves to disk, a leaner hand-off between sections, tighter expert packing, and exact four-card-sum arithmetic — had to be turned off due to missing their targets.

Another common technique is to run multiple requests at once, usually netting a speed improvement. In this case, three requests at 128K got about 360 tokens per second in total on code and 213 on legal. That said, “this is not a batched-decode engine; more requests than sections gain little.”

However, that might be next on the docket, as he plans to study batched decoding, “the one thing the Sparks have that I want,” he wrote on Reddit. Beyond that, there are plans to turn on the conversation store so a matter’s documents can survive a restart. While he acknowledges that the numbers do not establish “answer quality in use,” the setup has been his default DeepSeek machine since Monday, and he says the model is the first one “that works like Opus 4.6” for him.

A Bloomberg reporter was ejected from a historic trilateral meeting between the leaders of the United States, China, and Russia after reportedly asking a sensitive question to the U.S. President while in the presence of the leader of the Chinese Communist Party. According to The Washington Post, Bloomberg News journalist Annmarie Hordern, the chief political correspondent and co-host of the media outlet’s leading program, Bloomberg Surveillance, asked, “President Trump, has Jensen Huang been disloyal to the United States?”

A second reporter had then started asking a second question before Chinese officials shushed the group. It’s unclear if Trump was able to answer both these questions. Senior White House Correspondent for Fox News Jacqui Heinrich said on X that China, the host country of the Asia-Pacific Economic Cooperation (APEC) summit where the trilateral meeting was happening along the side, told journalists that “yelling questions is considered extremely rude” and that anyone doing so “will be pulled from the room,” possibly implying the nature of the questions' delivery, rather than its content, was behind the decision.

Jensen Huang’s sudden resignation as the CEO of Nvidia has been a major scandal in the past few weeks. Even though he admitted to having an affair, he did not name the third party, reported to be Alice Huang, a Chinese executive for Megaspeed who’s also accused of diverting $7.5 billion worth of Nvidia chips into China and passing along information to Chinese intelligence.

The U.S. and China have been locked in a rivalry in recent years, with artificial intelligence being one of the industries that the Chinese Communist Party is focusing on. This race to achieve “supremacy” in AI has led the White House to impose export controls on its most powerful chips and to bar hardware containing U.S. tech from making its way into China. But even though Trump made a 180-degree turn and finally allowed Nvidia H100 chips into China in December 2025, provided the company can secure export licenses, the most advanced Blackwell and upcoming Vera Rubin chips from Nvidia are still out of reach for Chinese companies. Aside from that, Washington is also closing a loophole that allowed Chinese AI firms to take advantage of American compute by exporting their data to data centers located in other countries. Despite all those controls, rumors of AI chip smuggling still persist, with several individuals, including Supermicro co-founder Yih-Shyan “Wally” Liaw, getting arrested for chip diversion.

China also puts tighter control on its journalists and censors sensitive topics. As the host of the APEC summit and the trilateral meeting, it could exclude reporters that it thinks violate its standards. Heinrich said that the White House advocated for Hordern’s inclusion in the trilateral meeting, but it seems that the Chinese refused to budge on their decision.

Among the new features of Android 17 are improvements to the system interface, with more customization options, new icons, and changes to the layout of buttons and widgets. The Settings menu has been redesigned to make navigation more intuitive and facilitate access to the smartphone’s main functions.

With the update, Circle to Search now offers real-time translations, allowing you to understand content in other languages ​​more easily, without having to interrupt your current activity on your smartphone.

Android 17 enhances connectivity with Instant Hotspot , which simplifies sharing your internet connection between compatible devices. Quick Share has also received improvements to facilitate file transfer, including compatibility with iOS smartphones via QR Code.

Security remains a top priority for the system. The Authentication Failure Lock , a security feature in case of theft, locks the screen after unsuccessful authentication attempts. Meanwhile, Message-Based Fraud Detection enhances the identification of potential threats and fraud. Furthermore, access to emergency calls has become easier and faster.

The update also improves HDR brightness control, which adjusts the screen intensity to highlight bright details in compatible photos and videos. Users can adjust this brightness for more comfortable viewing, or even disable the feature.

Despite the smaller footprint, though, this model can delivers up to 600 gallons per day, which should be more than enough for the everyday drinking, cooking, and beverage needs of a smaller household. #10423. Securing FastAPI Applications: JWT Authentication and Rate Limiting with Redis

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Building a modern web API is only half the battle. Ensuring it is secure, resilient, and capable of handling malicious traffic is what separates a hobby project from a production-ready service. When building highly scalable applications, we typically lean on stateless architectures. However, features like token revocation and rate limiting require state.

In this guide, we will explore the architectural concepts behind building a secure API using FastAPI, Redis, JSON Web Tokens (JWT), and SlowAPI. We will break down the theory, the tech stack, and the flow of data—without getting bogged down in the code.

Before diving into the architecture, it is crucial to understand the role each tool plays in our security model:

Security starts with how you manage your secrets. The first step in our architectural journey is setting up a configuration file and a local environment file.

Hardcoding secret keys, database URLs, or algorithm types directly into your application is a severe security risk. Instead, we load these from environment variables. The application uses a settings manager to read variables like the Redis host, port, the JWT secret key, the algorithm (typically HS256), and the token expiration time. This ensures that sensitive data stays out of your version control system.

To handle rate limiting and token tracking, we need a connection to Redis. Because FastAPI is inherently asynchronous, we initialize an asynchronous Redis client.

Redis acts as the central state-holder for our API. By using Redis, we ensure that even if our API is running on five different servers behind a load balancer, the rate limits and the token blacklists remain globally synchronized.

Next, we establish the utility functions that handle the cryptographic heavy lifting. This step is divided into two main parts:

Here, we initialize the FastAPI application and attach slowapi.

slowapi needs a way to identify the client making the request. By default, it uses the client's IP address. We attach the limiter to the application state and register an exception handler. This ensures that if a user breaches the rate limit, the API gracefully returns a "429 Too Many Requests" error instead of crashing.

We also set up a dependency that acts as a security guard for our protected routes. This dependency intercepts incoming requests, extracts the token, and decodes it. It then checks Redis to see if the token has been blacklisted (for example, if the user logged out before the token expired). If the token is valid and not blacklisted, the request proceeds; otherwise, a "401 Unauthorized" error is thrown.

The login endpoint is where the magic happens. When a user submits their credentials, the API verifies the username and password. In a production scenario, this involves querying a database; in our example, we verify it against a hardcoded credential.

Once the credentials are validated, the API generates a JWT. To ensure we can invalidate this token later if needed, we store the token in Redis with a time-to-live (TTL) that matches the token's expiration time. Finally, the token is returned to the client, which will store it and send it in the Authorization header of future requests.

Finally, we create a route that requires both authentication and rate limiting.

Before the route's logic even executes, two things happen:

If the client passes both security checks, they are granted access to the highly secure data provided by the route.

To validate this system, you would conceptually walk through several test scenarios. First, you test the login flow with correct and incorrect credentials to ensure tokens are only issued to valid users.

Next, you test the rate limit by rapidly firing requests at the protected route to ensure the "429 Too Many Requests" response triggers. Finally, you test the security guard by trying to access the route with no token, an expired token, and a manually blacklisted token to ensure the API correctly denies access in all scenarios.

Building a secure API requires layering defenses. By combining FastAPI's speed, Redis's fast state management, JWT's stateless authentication, and slowapi's rate-limiting capabilities, you create a system that is both robust and scalable.

While this foundational text explains the core concepts, moving to production requires adding token refresh flows, implementing per-user rate limiting (rather than just IP-based), deploying Redis with high availability, and ensuring your secrets are managed by a secure vault. These practices will ensure your API remains secure as your user base grows.

Drama has already taken off on X (formerly Twitter), where Noah Cat reposted the video, and it seems Samsung fans aren’t happy. Samsung has needed to change up the vertical camera bar for years, as it has regularly gotten in the way of Qi2 and MagSafe magnetic accessories. This new design might help that, but it seems fans wanted Samsung to return to the Galaxy S10 horizontal camera bar days, not this new iPhone-esque raised island.

#10420. Agent Distraction

What is easy for humans to skim past in code, agents see as a call to action. They do not know better, latching on to that which is littered across the files read during the planning and research phase of the coding journey.

As agents' agency through tools and skills grows the ability for side-quests scales proportionally. Obsolete specs, long resolved bugs, and lagging documentation no longer rest in long forgotten corners of the codebase, but rather are unintentionally ingested as context indirecting and misdirecting the next wave of coders - the agents. This indirection is not free: in the best case it only costs tokens, steps, and time - in the worst case it distracts and grounds the agent on falsehoods leading to incorrect logic and decisions during implementation.

Measuring how often agents fall into these traps across millions of monthly sessions is impossible with manual human review - we need an objective signal derivable from the code itself. Inline TODO(bug/...) comments provide the perfect proxy. TODOs tend to be left behind long after the underlying bug is resolved or abandoned, while the linked bug ticket acts as the canonical record. By cross-referencing every TODO an agent encounters against the TODO's bug status and the user's prompt, we can automatically separate valid work from wasted detours at scale.

By leveraging the distributed traces of agent interactions across thousands of engineers and over hundreds of millions of individual agent steps, we quantify the impact of TODO distractions in the codebase and how agents act on them:

Controlling for developer, codebase complexity, task type, model tier, number of files read, initial prompt length, and the step where the bug reference appeared, using a multi-way fixed-effects regression with developer-clustered standard errors, we see that unprompted bug detours add substantial overhead (all stat sig p<0.0001). Per session:

The Obsolete Detour: In the following trajectory we can observe how this drift plays out. During an investigation into an unrelated bug The agent views SurfaceControlTest.java and sees // TODO(bug/XXX)

This bug, while fixed in 2023, remains lingering in the codebase leading to subsequent investigation distraction across Steps with the agent digging into the bug to learn more.

The Eager Agent: In the trajectory the agent encountered a TODO in the bug it was investigating and decided (unprompted) to "fix" the code

The agent executed multiple Code Search and internal knowledge search queries for the bug ultimately resulting in the agent changing the ModelDownloaderV1 code. Later in the trajectory the human challenged the scope creep.

With the model when admitting it was an incorrect increase in scope:

While in both of these cases they were only trivial to minor detours - they were real distractions costing time, tokens, and human intervention.

As part of addressing this finding at scale we created agents that scrubbed the totality of our codebases, finding bug references in the code that were marked as fixed or obsolete for cleanup. That step alone however was not enough to determine if the TODOs in the codebase were load-bearing or not. The agents had to go further looking at the history of the bug, the file, and changes to the codebase, at times spanning years of history. Only in cases where the agent was able to confirm that the TODO and the intent behind the TODO was obsolete did our agent send a change for review to delete it from the codebase. As a result of this multi-layer approach, our todo cleaner has been able to remove thousands of actually obsolete references over the last 6 months exceeding 95% acceptance from human reviewers.

In recap, as we task agents on more and more ambitious problems, "agent friendly codebases" require more than just the code in the hotpath being healthy. The forgotten corners of the codebase now more than ever carry a significant tax levied against agents.

Tooling like presubmit analyzers, entire corpus scanners, and automated coding agents to remediate dead-todos offer out of the box ready to scale solutions to address the tax enabling a healthier and distraction free codebase for agents to thrive.

Projects that once were on the backburner to cleanup lingering debt but deprioritized due to perceived low value need to be reevaluated with the new agents acting at scale on your codebase.

Hacks across Europe caused supermarket fridges to reach temperatures of 50 degrees Celsius, but a repeat of the issue isn't expected in the U.K.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Following devastating hacks that caused European fridges to reach 50 degrees Celsius, destroying food stocks, the UK has implemented nationwide patching to ensure similar attacks won't affect its fridges. While it was thought that the UK had avoided similar hacks because it used different devices from those used in mainland Europe, an even worse issue was just waiting to be discovered. The BBC reports that a different flaw was discovered by security researchers in late October, one that could have caused even worse damage if it had been identified by hackers. Since then, work has been carried out to harden security across the U.K., with fridges in supermarkets, warehouses, and elsewhere now protected.

Refrigeration consultants Omega Solutions were the first to identify the issue before the National Cyber Security Centre (NCSC), the Department for Environment, Food & Rural Affairs (Defra) and Food Standards Agency (FSA) all became involved in working on the problem. As a precaution, thousands of devices that control tens of thousands of fridges were blocked from accessing the internet, with supermarket IT workers scrambling to sometimes physically isolate devices by removing network cables.

"From a business perspective, it could have been potentially devastating with business interruption and supply chain problems. Our warehouses have millions of pounds worth of food that could have been spoiled if any hackers found out about this method of attack," one supermarket worker warned.

The issue that they were trying to address rested within the most popular refrigeration controllers used across the U.K., made by British firm Resource Data Management (RDM). It was found that these controllers had a similar authentication security flaw to those hacked across Europe, although RDM points out that hacking its devices would have required a specific piece of hardware. However, that hardware is freely available.

RDM has since confirmed that there was no sign of any malicious activity on any of its controllers throughout the patching process.

The situation in France, Belgium, and other European countries was much more dire. After initially hitting France in October, a further wave of attacks swept through supermarkets and even blood banks. Even the fridges at a U.S. military base were not safe. While no responsibility for the attacks has been taken, France has so far laid the blame at the door of Iran — something the country's government denies.

#10415. Microsoft is shutting down the ‘To Do’ app that replaced Wunderlist, with no real replacement

Microsoft announced this week that its To Do app is shutting down soon, taking the legacy of Wunderlist with it.

Microsoft is shutting down its To Do app at the end of June 2027, with focus moving to the newer Planner app. The company explains:

As we bring personal task management into Planner, we will phase out To Do. The To Do apps will retire on June 30, 2027. Connections that other apps use to access To Do tasks will continue beyond that date, and we’ll provide separate notice before those connections are retired. We’re also updating existing task integrations across Microsoft 365 to connect with Planner, so you’ll be able to create or manage tasks in the Microsoft experiences you already use. Although To Do tasks are already visible in Planner, they will not be migrated to the new private plan. Customers using personal Microsoft accounts will need to choose another app. Our To Do transition guidance  will help you understand what’s changing and plan your next steps.

To Do was built on the foundation of Wunderlist, a wildly popular to do list app that Microsoft purchased in 2015. Wunderlist later shut down in 2020.

Microsoft’s shuffle, notably, leaves general consumers without a replacement app. To Do was available to anyone with a Microsoft account, where Planner is available only to enterprise/business users. So, for long-time Wunderlist users who followed Microsoft migration, this is the end of the road and it’s officially time to move to another app.

#10413. Phison details SSD with a built-in NPU and 64GB of memory that supports AI frameworks on the drive

Keep exploring and earning more as a member.

Reviews, benchmarks, and updates on current GPUs.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

Neither design has a product name, a named controller or a sample date yet.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Phison detailed two new enterprise SSD designs at Supercomputer 2026 (SC26) that aim to answer two separate problems: one with a built-in NPU and 64GB of 130 GB/s memory that supports running PyTorch, vLLM, OpenVINO, and llama.cpp directly on the drive, and another that merges fast pSLC cache with large QLC capacity behind just one controller. The company notes that “many of the products and features mentioned are still in development” and will arrive once finalized.

The computational-storage design’s premise is that a server can offload more to the SSD, which runs AI code on the data where it already resides and sends back results, instead of whole datasets. The pSLC-QLC design, meanwhile, puts the cache inside each drive, which may reduce system complexity, as every drive bay contributes usable capacity and none goes to dedicated cache drives.

Phison says that the integrated NPU is expected to deliver “36,800 DMIPS of compute performance,” or Dhrystone Million Instructions Per Second, a typical synthetic benchmark. This is admittedly an old CPU integer benchmark from the 1980s, while NPUs tend to be rated in TOPS, but that figure was not given. For reference, the Topaz NPU add-in card Phison showed at Computex carried eight NPUs rated at up to 40 TOPS each. The DMIPS figure therefore likely refers to, instead, the CPU cores that work alongside it, which we would expect to be based on an Arm Cortex design, adequate for running an SSD of this class but no measure of its AI capability.

The memory and its bandwidth are more important for the expected workload, anyway, and while the company doesn’t state the type, LPDDR5X is a popular option and may be used with the Topaz NPU, according to one secondary report. If the drive is a PCIe 6.0 part comparable to Phison’s X3 controller, it could hit up to 28 GB/s to the host, which would put the memory above 4x that speed. For reference, 130 GB/s is a bit less than half of that of the DGX Spark’s hardware, designed for AI development.

Phison says developers can build on the drive “without specialized programming tools,” which, in this case, is a good thing. The drive does, of course, support multiple general-purpose inference servers and runtimes, but nothing was disclosed about how a host reaches the drive’s compute. The drives “scan and process data in parallel,” which indicates scalability, and return “relevant results” which Phison claims could mean 10 to 100 times less data transferred for applicable workloads. This may free up the host CPU for other tasks.

Phison’s idea to put compute on storage isn’t new. Samsung tried this with its SmartSSD — where an FPGA sat on the drive to process data without moving it — which was shown in 2018. Later, in 2022, the design was refined with AMD’s Xilinx Versal FPGA in its second generation, with Samsung claiming more than 50% lower processing time, up to 70% lower energy consumption, and up to 97% lower server CPU usage for scan-heavy database queries, compared to conventional SSDs. The drive has reportedly since disappeared from Samsung’s lineup, though the first-generation model was still sold under AMD’s Xilinx name as of last year.

ScaleFlux took a narrower route, with compression handled inside the SSD controller. The server doesn’t see this, so the drive looks like any other NVMe SSD, and no application has to change its behavior. The CEO claims it is the only company with computational storage in production at hyperscalers. This suggests that not making customers write new code for the drive is the secret to success, as SmartSSD needed that for its FPGA and for each workload. Phison’s selling point here is flexibility for the client to use code written for common AI frameworks.

Company CTO Sebastien Jean, writing in All About Circuits in 2025, argued that generic computational storage had stalled because “each use case is highly unique and it’s simply not scalable.” He called the idea of running the Linux operating system on a drive with bigger processors a “pie-in-the-sky approach.”

Accelerators have long been widely used in data centers, and Jean’s proposal was to put targeted ones inside the SSD, each running a set operation over a range of logical block addresses (LBAs). By his reckoning, a chassis with 90 PCIe 6.0 SSDs could process data at 2.5 TB/s without affecting the CPU. The design revealed at SC26, though, appears closer to the general-purpose model, possibly due to the increasing relevance of AI and the demand for both memory and compute.

Jean also spoke at the Emerging Storage Technology panel at the conference to discuss future developments, including PCIe 7.0 drives that would operate at “50, 56 gigabytes per second,” which he called “faster than a DDR channel.” With 32 drives at this speed, a single chassis could reach “1.8 terabytes per second.” He described this specifically as computational storage for HPC.

Turning to the Mixed Media solution, that’s the “high-endurance pSLC and high-capacity QLC flash” SSD; it’s a 16-channel part, so not comparable to anything you see in retail. The pSLC portion of the drive has the 4-bit QLC flash act in a 1-bit SLC mode, which trades space for performance and endurance. Phison says the design builds on standard NVMe interfaces and fits into existing infrastructure “without requiring specialized host software.”

Jean elaborated at the same panel that the “endurance group on the drive, which is SLC,” is managed separately from the XLC — 3-bit TLC or 4-bit QLC — and each region gets a namespace, so it appears as multiple drives to the OS. Then, high-write data can be sent specifically to the SLC group, while data in the QLC is likely to be more read-heavy. This follows a general trend of enterprise shifting toward QLC for capacity, but having some of it in SLC mode can help reduce the need for specialized caching.

Right now, high-capacity SSDs usually have separate SSDs to fulfill this role, but that takes up more drive bays. The tradeoff with Phison’s approach is that a single drive failure takes out both capacity and a share of the cache. That said, Phison’s approach is more robust from a cache perspective, as “losing one drive out of 24 would result in a 4% cache loss instead of a 25% loss,” the company says, with the 25% presumably assuming four dedicated cache drives.

Dedicated cache drives need tiering handled by the host or array software, while with Mixed Media each drive holds both tiers and the host simply picks the namespace it wants to write to.

The company is not the first to have this idea, either, as Kioxia presented its Mixed Mode idea during FMS 2025, where each drive has a ratio of pSLC to QLC, set once per drive when it’s first used. Kioxia called 1:4 a “good beginning ratio,” though it said customers typically want pSLC at just 0.5% to 2% of maximum QLC capacity. That implementation also had parity protection on the pSLC space across multiple drives. Kioxia similarly marketed the benefit of needing fewer slots and field-replaceable units (FRUs), and its slides even used the term “Mixed Media” in two chart headings.

DapuStor went further with its dual-mode J5060, a QLC SSD with a pSLC region that was in customer validation as of August; it compared the design against “four dedicated SLC drives” in a 24-drive system, the kind of setup that would produce Phison’s 25% figure. Solidigm’s Cloud Storage Acceleration Layer works differently, with a host-side flash translation layer, where it can manage small writes on SLC or TLC drives before writing to QLC in favorable large sequential chunks.

There’s also the Enmotus FuzeDrive P200, previewed at CES 2020 as the MiDrive, which used a Phison controller and seems to be the closest to an ancestor of the current Mixed Media. That consumer drive, which we reviewed in 2021, had SLC and QLC portions that acted distinctly, but they were managed by Enmotus software.

In every drive, behind one 16-channel controller

Separate NVMe endurance groups assigned to namespaces; no specialized host software, Phison says

In every drive, with parity protection on the pSLC space across drives

1:4 as a “good beginning ratio”; customers typically want pSLC at 0.5% to 2% of maximum QLC capacity

Ratio set once, creating a pSLC namespace and endurance group

In every drive, as a pSLC region beside the QLC

400GB, 800GB or 1.2TB of pSLC on a 30.72TB drive

Separate SLC and QLC block devices on one drive

In the drive, as a fixed SLC portion of a consumer QLC SSD

Reference system: three 800GB SLC drives and seven 61.44TB QLC drives

Flash translation layer running on the host

It’s also inevitable that these two approaches will be compared to Phison’s other upcoming part, the X3 enterprise SSD controller, a PCIe 6.0 design promising up to 28 GB/s of sequential throughput and 6.8 million IOPS. Those figures are unchanged since Computex in June.

The X3’s IOPS sounds like a lot, but we’ve heard considerable banter about AI needing way more than that, as Nvidia’s roadmap target for PCIe 7.0 is at 100 million IOPS, though that target counts 512-byte reads, a smaller unit than the 4KB that SSD ratings usually use, so the two figures aren’t directly comparable. Jean said at the panel that Phison is “very optimistic” it can achieve that. He added that the eightfold jump in IOPS that comes with moving from 4KB to 512-byte accesses would, on existing NAND, mean “eight times as much power,” or “roughly in the 140 to 180 watt range,” which is beyond reasonable, so perhaps a different solution will be required.

For the X3, Phison told us in June to expect customer samples by the end of the year, with volume coming in the middle of next year, though the SC26 release gives no dates. Notably, Micron and Samsung are already offering PCIe 6.0 SSDs, and the Bravera SC6 controller is expected from Marvell. Phison also says its PS7161, which its site lists as a customer sample, is “the first PCIe 6.0 redriver on the PCI-SIG Integrators List.”

Of the two designs, Mixed Media looks like the nearer product. It builds on standard NVMe features, and DapuStor’s similar dual-mode drive reached customer testing this summer, while the one processor figure given for the computational-storage drive says little about what it can do for AI. What remains to be seen for Mixed Media is its pSLC-to-QLC ratio and how configurable it is. CES and Computex are the notable dates on the 2027 calendar to find out more about both, as well as the X3.

Shane Downing is a Contributing Writer for Tom’s Hardware, covering consumer storage, PC hardware, and AI.

#10412. How AI Image-to-Image and Image-to-Video Tools Work, and How to Build a Reliable Workflow

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Creating polished visual content used to require professional equipment, editing skills and hours of manual work. Generative AI has changed that for photographers, designers, marketers and developers who build creative tools, but the results depend heavily on understanding what these systems actually do. Two techniques sit at the center of most workflows: image-to-image generation, which transforms an existing picture, and image-to-video generation, which adds motion to a still.

This article explains how both techniques work, which settings control the outcome, how several kinds of tools differ, and how to assemble a workflow with review steps, including a short code example you can run yourself. It also covers rights, authenticity and the limits of the technology. Feature details come from each vendor’s own pages at the time of writing and change often, so confirm current terms before you plan a production.

Image-to-image resembles text-to-image, but it starts from your picture instead of pure noise. According to the Hugging Face Diffusers image-to-image guide, the initial image is encoded into latent space and noise is added to it. The model then uses the prompt and the noisy latent to predict and remove that noise, and a decoder converts the result back into an image.

A single setting, usually called strength or creativity, decides how much of the original remains. In Diffusers, a higher strength gives the model more freedom, and a value of 1.0 means the starting image is largely ignored. Strength is linked to the number of denoising steps: with 50 inference steps and a strength of 0.8, the pipeline adds noise for 40 steps and then denoises for 40.

The ranges above are rules of thumb, not guarantees. Models and tools respond differently, so test on your own images. The script below, adapted from the Diffusers documentation, sweeps four strength values on one source image so you can compare them side by side.

Running this requires a compatible GPU setup and model downloads, and the checkpoint name and defaults can change between library versions, so check the current documentation.

Plain image-to-image can drift from the layout you wanted. Research such as ControlNet adds spatial control to pretrained text-to-image diffusion models, with conditions such as edges, depth, segmentation and human pose. In practice, this is why many tools offer reference images, sketches, masks and pose guides on top of a prompt: they supply structure that a text prompt cannot describe precisely.

The key advantage is speed of iteration. The main limits are consistency between variations, small details such as hands and text, and the gap between a convincing concept and a usable final asset.

The right tool depends on what you need to control and where the output will be used. The table summarizes what each vendor’s own page states, and “not stated” means the page did not say, not that the feature is absent.

OpenArt’s own page describes an AI image to image generator that accepts JPEG, PNG, WEBP or HEIC files, offers a creativity slider and an optional reference image, and lets you switch between several models. Its FAQ advises uploading only images you own or have rights to use. Adobe’s Firefly page describes generative editing and video tools and states that outputs include Content Credentials, while saying its own models were trained on licensed and public-domain content. These are vendor statements, so read the current terms for the plan you use.

Image-to-video uses a still as the starting point for an animated scene. Most current systems extend diffusion models across time, so that a whole sequence of frames is denoised together. The research paper Video Diffusion Models describes this extension from images to video. The practical consequence is that the first frame strongly shapes what follows, so a clean composition and a clear subject make better clips.

For example, a still of a person walking through a rainy city can be animated with falling rain, reflections and a slow camera move. The motion instruction should describe one clear action and one camera movement rather than a long list.

Kling’s main site announces Kling 4.0 and describes stable dynamic motion with an audio-visual experience, with image-to-video features and an API platform listed. OpenArt’s Kling 4.0 page describes how the model is offered on that platform. According to that page, videos run from 3 to 30 seconds at 720p to 1080p, you can use up to 10 keyframes and up to 15 references, and you can save a character for reuse. The page does not state pricing or generation quotas for the model. Capabilities depend on the platform that provides access and on the settings available there, so check supported inputs, output limits and cost before planning a production.

Generated motion needs checking. Warped limbs, drifting faces, flickering textures and unwanted text are common. A quick review script samples frames and reports basic file properties:

Consider a small business preparing a campaign for a new skincare product. The same steps apply to any visual project.

This process reduces the effort of exploring concepts and makes it easier to test several versions of an advertisement before choosing a direction. It does not remove the need for human review.

They can be, with different priorities. Photographers can explore creative treatments, mood boards and alternative backgrounds without replacing traditional photography. Marketers can develop campaign concepts, create variations of social graphics and test promotional videos. In both cases the value comes from supporting a clear creative objective rather than adopting a tool because it is new.

A technique that transforms an existing image using a prompt, by adding noise to the image and then regenerating it under guidance. A strength or creativity setting controls how much of the original is kept.

Use a lower strength, a reference image or a structure condition such as edges or depth where the tool supports it.

Yes. Image-to-video tools animate a still from a motion description, though complex motion can introduce inconsistencies, so review every clip.

No. Several vendors and platforms offer video generation. Compare supported inputs, length, resolution, cost and licensing against your needs.

It depends on the tool, the plan and the rights you hold in the inputs. Read the current terms and keep records of your sources.

Image-to-image and image-to-video tools give creators a faster way to explore ideas, but they work best when you understand their controls. Strength settings decide how much of the original survives, conditioning methods preserve structure, and the starting frame shapes video. Compare hosted platforms, vendor apps and open-source libraries against your needs, build review and provenance into the workflow, and treat the output as a draft that a person checks before it ships.

#10408. Expect Samsung’s good-value Galaxy devices to get more expensive, report says

Rising memory costs are hitting mid-range devices the hardest. The latest report suggests Samsung will increase the price of its Galaxy A device series by 19% next year.

The report comes from Korean news outlet SEDaily. An industry source has reportedly stated that Samsung plans to increase the price of its mid-range phones starting next year. More specifically, the Galaxy A series.

The range between price jumps varies across a reported 8 Galaxy A series devices, but the highest value difference nearly hits a notable 19% increase. The largest jump reaches 18.6% for the Galaxy A17, which could increase from $269 to $319. It would no longer sit in a sub-$300 spot and wouldn’t be much of a value buy anymore.

Again, these are phones praised for sitting at a convenient price point. Samsung’s Galaxy A devices are generally good value and offer a lot of the best One UI features for a price that doesn’t come close to the current flagship selling points.

This comes after a report came out last month indicating Samsung cut production of its Galaxy A devices by “approximately 20%.” Memory makes up more of the overall component cost in mid-range devices. So the cost of the overall device is more influenced by memory prices than in a flagship, like the Galaxy S26 Ultra. Even with a specced-out build, there’s more dispersion of costly components, rather than one expensive component like memory taking up the bulk of the production price.

That cut is estimated to be around 10 million units, and this latest report only furthers the claim that mid-range devices are too costly to make for Samsung.

The opposite is true for the Galaxy S27 Pro and Ultra, which reportedly got the green light for increased production prior to launch. Price suggestions average an increase of $200 for the Galaxy S27 and Galaxy S27 Plus. As for the high-spec Galaxy S27 Pro and Ultra, they start at $1,399 and $1,499, respectively.

There was already a massive disparity between those devices and the increasingly validated Galaxy A devices, and the gap is shrinking.

Before the X3D2 was released in April of this year, this was the flagship X3D SKU and offered not only a lot of cores and threads (16c/32t) but also X3D cache (128MB) on one of the CCDs, which is great for gaming. Clock speeds start at 4.3 GHz, with turbo boosting a couple of cores up to 5.7 GHz. With its clock speeds, core count, and extra cache, this processor is not only great for gaming but also awesome for any PC work that needs more cores and threads, like video editing, 3D rendering, and even running VMs.

The third part of the combo is the Samsung 990 Pro M.2. This PCIe 4.0 x4 SSD delivers up to 7,250/6,450 MB/s sequential read/write speeds and up to 850K/1,200K IOPS for random reads/writes. This is a very speedy PCIe 4 drive, only eclipsed by more costly PCIe 5-based drives. We reviewed this drive when it was released and loved its strong all-around performance, excellent software (Samsung Magician), and consistent, efficient, cool-running performance.

Last but certainly not least, we reviewed the Asus ROG Strix X870E-E Gaming Wifi motherboard and gave it four stars as one of our best options around the ~$500 price point. This premium offering gives users everything they want out of a motherboard. It has strong power delivery to support the 9950X3D processor, five M.2 sockets (three PCIe 5.0) and four SATA ports for storage, fast networking with 5GbE and 320MHz Wi-Fi, ample USB ports on the rear I/O including dual USB4 (40 Gbps) Type-C ports, and a premium audio solution using the Real ALC4080 codec along with an integrated amplifier. On top of that, it’s packed with “AI” features (Overclocking, Cooling, Cache Boost) and sports a DIY-friendly design.

If you were waiting for Black Friday week to take the pain out of buying PC components, that time is now. Even if you weren't, that time is still probably now. This 4-item combo with its huge $369 discount is only $1549 and one of the best bundles Newegg has going for those looking for a high-end entry into the AM5 platform and who need good storage too. The combo only lasts until supplies run out, so jump on this stellar deal while you can.

#10402. What is Pathways? Single-controller training for large models

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

Today, we announce the General Availability of Pathways on Google Cloud Platform. In this post, you will learn about Pathways, the distributed system Google built to train its own large models, including Gemini. We discuss how Pathways addresses the coordination challenge by introducing one client that describes the whole computation as a graph, and how the distributed runtime places it, moves the tensors, and keeps the accelerators busy.

Most large-model training runs as Single Program, Multiple Data (SPMD) programs. Every accelerator runs the same program in lockstep and coordinates through collectives like AllReduce.

It works well when your job is the same program running on every device. It struggles when the job is no longer uniform, like a pipeline whose stages wait on each other or an RL loop where a sampler, a reward model, and a trainer are different programs that have to overlap.

Large runs also have to survive hardware failures, which interrupted Gemini 2.5 training multiple times per hour. In SPMD, one lost machine stops every process and sends the run back to its last checkpoint. In each case, the challenge is coordination. You have to decide what runs where and in what order, how tensors move between devices, and how the job keeps going when a machine drops out.

In this post of the series “Pathways on Google Cloud”, you will learn about Pathways, the distributed system Google built to train its own large models, including Gemini. The post discusses how Pathways addresses the coordination challenge by introducing one client that describes the whole computation as a graph, and how the distributed runtime places it, moves the tensors, and keeps the accelerators busy.

By the end, you'll know where SPMD is suboptimal, what single-controller execution changes, and how Pathways coordinates a whole training job across a cluster.

If you train PyTorch models, you almost certainly use Distributed Data Parallel (DDP) or Fully Sharded Data Parallel (FSDP). DDP runs a copy of your script on every accelerator and averages gradients with a collective operation such as AllReduce. FSDP shards parameters, gradients, and optimizer states across workers so that the model fits at all.

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

DDP and FSDP both follow the Single Program, Multiple Data (SPMD) execution model. Every process runs the same copy of your script, owns its accelerators for the whole run, and moves in lockstep with the others, because the only way they coordinate is through collectives like AllReduce. Inside one tightly connected group of accelerators those collectives run over a fast interconnect and are cheap. Scale the job across groups and the same collectives cross the datacenter network, which is far slower. On a TPU v5p, for example, each chip gets 1,200 GB/s of bidirectional chip-to-chip bandwidth inside its group, but only 50 Gb/s (gigabits, about 6 GB/s) to the datacenter network which is roughly two orders of magnitude less.

And because every step blocks on its collectives, that communication lands directly in step time. The more groups you span, the larger the share of each step spent moving data, so throughput grows slower than the number of accelerators you add.

As long as your training job is one program that fills the hardware you give it, this execution model fits well. You write your model as if there were one device, and the collectives do the rest. But fitting a model into memory is only part of the problem. Keeping every accelerator busy is the harder part, and this is where the SPMD model starts to show its limits.

Take pipeline parallelism as an example. You split a model into blocks across devices, and each device now runs different code over smaller micro-batches. Activations flow forward, activation gradients flow back, and while device 0 works on the first micro-batch, devices 1 and 2 wait. Apart from the idle accelerators you keep paying for, this drops utilization and throughput, even though every device has work in the steady state.

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

Another example is RL post-training for an LLM. You have a sampler that generates rollouts, a reward model that scores them, and a trainer that updates the policy and sends the new weights back to the sampler. These are three different programs that should overlap; otherwise the sampler is idle while the trainer runs, and the trainer is idle while the sampler generates.

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

In both scenarios, the job is now several different programs running on different devices, and their dependencies can no longer be managed through a single collective. You now have to coordinate which devices share which tensors, which processes run while others are still finishing, and how early to enqueue the next buffer so an accelerator never waits for input. Because each worker only has visibility into its local devices and data, no individual process can manage all of these dependencies itself. That has several consequences.

First, because each process owns its accelerators for the whole run and every collective blocks until all devices finish, one failed machine prevents the collective from completing and stops the entire job, forcing a restart from the last checkpoint. You cannot add devices mid-run, and you cannot give devices back.

Second, because each process holds its accelerators from start to finish, capacity can't move to where it's needed. Within one job, a stage that needs fewer chips than another still keeps the extra ones. They sit idle, you keep paying for them, and no other part of the job can use them until the run ends. Across a team, the same happens at cluster scale. Every job owns its accelerators for as long as it runs, so sharing a cluster means splitting it into per-job allocations, and each new job brings up its own processes on its own hosts.

Finally, the development process itself gets harder. On one host you iterate fast because you run the script, drop into a Python shell, set a breakpoint, and debug. This is not true once the job spans many hosts. You need a launcher to push your script to every host, you hit coordination bugs that never showed up on one machine, and there's no single process to break into. When something hangs, each worker is stuck at a collective waiting for the others, with no one place that shows the whole job.

So the common challenge here is the coordination itself. As soon as the job stops being one program in lockstep, you have to decide what runs where, in what order, and how tensors get from one device to the next.

One way to describe this is as a graph, where each block is a node, each tensor that moves between blocks is an edge, and the devices are where the nodes are placed. You can wire that graph up by hand in SPMD, one process at a time. But what if a single client declared that graph, including what each block does, what it needs, and where it runs, and a distributed runtime handled the transfers, dependencies, and ordering along its edges?

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

That is the idea behind Pathways, the distributed system Google built to train its own largest models, including Gemini. Pathways runs JAX programs on TPUs and it is available on Google Cloud.

With Pathways, you define the end-to-end computation from a single Python client, without writing custom coordination code, and a distributed runtime executes it. That client is decoupled from the hardware. It runs on an ordinary CPU machine and you write your program against virtual devices; a resource manager maps them onto real chips, and a worker on each accelerator host runs the compiled programs it receives.

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

Because your code refers to virtual devices rather than physical ones, the runtime is flexible to allow users to change the devices used for a given computation. This allows you to stay in control while the runtime handles the mapping to hardware. In an RL loop, for example, the trainer and the samplers rarely want the same number of chips, so you can give the sampler stage more chips by asking for more virtual devices.

Also, the same capability helps when a host fails or the hardware isn't ready yet. If a host dies, the resource manager remaps the work onto healthy chips and the run keeps going instead of relaunching the whole job, which is the basis for elastic training. And because the program targets virtual devices, compilation can start before the physical chips are provisioned, so setup overlaps with provisioning instead of waiting on it.

The resource manager owning the chips also means one Pathways cluster can serve several clients, so researchers on a team can share its capacity. With the Shared Pathways Service, the cluster stays up as a long running service, which lets you develop interactively on TPUs hosted on GKE. Each client connects on demand from a laptop or VM, gets its own slices (groups of tightly connected TPU chips) from the shared pool, and hands them back when it disconnects. When every chip is busy, the next workload waits until capacity frees up.

Take the three-block model from Figure 2. Block A runs on one group of devices, block B on a second, block C on a third. You declare what each block computes, its target devices, and where its outputs go. Pathways expresses those dependencies as a distributed dataflow graph, in which each block is a compiled SPMD program and each edge is a tensor the runtime has to deliver.

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

In a single SPMD program, the framework handles the collectives that keep the devices in sync within a group, with or without Pathways, and your code doesn't change. The difference shows up once the job runs several programs on different groups (Multiple Programs, Multiple Data, MPMD), like these three blocks or the sampler, reward model, and trainer of an RL loop. Then no single process sees both sides (the same limit from before, where each worker only knows its own devices), so getting a tensor from block A to block B is a hand-off you have to coordinate yourself.

Pathways closes that gap. You declare the dependency once, and the runtime moves and reshards the tensor for you, over the fast interconnect within a group and over the datacenter network between groups.

To keep the accelerators busy, Pathways prepares launches ahead of time with futures and parallel asynchronous dispatch. While block A is still computing, block B can't run yet, but its input shapes and dtypes are already known, so the runtime allocates B's buffers and schedules the transfer before A's output even exists. Host-side setup for every block happens up front, and each block launches when its inputs arrive.

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

This is also what keeps the single client from becoming a bottleneck. Because dispatch is asynchronous, the coordination work overlaps with the compute already running on the chips, so the client stays off the critical path instead of stalling the accelerators between steps.

In Pathways, the runtime decides execution order when a group has more than one operation ready. In pipelined training, B's devices often hold both the forward pass for an upcoming micro-batch and the backward pass for an earlier one. If each device picked on its own, their collectives would conflict and the group would stall.

Pathways has a central scheduler that puts every program's operations into one consistent order, so operations that must communicate start together instead of deadlocking.

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

And because it's still one client, the development loop we mentioned before comes back. It's the same single Python process whether you drive one host or thousands of hosts, so you can drop into a shell, set a breakpoint, and inspect the run as it goes. When something stalls, there's one place that knows what's queued on every device and which dependency hasn't been met, instead of a hang spread across separate processes.

Put together, Pathways separates model building from execution. You choose how the model partitions across hardware using the client. And the runtime handles the transfers, ordering, and scheduling that keep accelerators busy, across the whole cluster rather than just your slice of it.

Described this way, Pathways can start to sound like an orchestrator you already know. If scheduling work, moving tensors, and keeping a whole cluster busy brings Ray to mind, it should. But the two work at different layers.

Ray is an open-source framework for orchestrating Python-native AI workloads across large clusters. It provisions the cluster, schedules your Python code as tasks and actors, and launches your workload across hosts, holding data loading, training, serving, and tuning under one control plane. To Ray, an accelerator is a resource to schedule.

What Ray doesn't change is how your training runs on the chips. The job inside each worker still runs as SPMD. The framework inserts the collectives, and each process sees only its own devices. Ray places and starts that job but it doesn't turn it into a single program that drives the whole cluster.

That is the layer Pathways works at. It changes the execution model underneath, so one client coordinates every chip. Because they sit at different layers, the two are complementary. In principle, Ray can orchestrate the cluster and the pipeline while Pathways handles how the training step executes.

So concretely, Ray's job is about orchestration while Pathways' job is about execution. With Ray, you tie your data, training, serving, and tuning together and spread heterogeneous work across accelerators and clouds. With Pathways, you run one model across many groups of chips, express patterns beyond SPMD, and recover from a failed host instead of relaunching the whole job.

When a training job stops being one uniform program, the challenge becomes coordinating the dependencies between stages, the tensors that cross the network, and the ordering that keeps every accelerator fed.

SPMD requires you to address these dependencies. With Pathways, you describe the computation once, from a single client, as a graph of blocks and the tensors between them. And you have a runtime that places the blocks on virtual devices, moves and reshards the tensors, orders the work across the cluster, and remaps around a failed host.

With Pathways, model building and execution are separated and you reason about the whole cluster instead of one slice of it.

If you are curious to learn more about Pathways, you can take a look at the following resources:

Multi-Turn Agentic Reinforcement Learning with Tunix and Gemma 4 on Google TPUs

Distributed LLM Training Across Metros: Streaming DiLoCo in MaxText
